## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Shabani, 513 U.S. 10

Argued October 3, 1994 | Decided November 1, 1994

From: The Ninth Circuit reversed Shabani’s conviction under 21 U.S.C. § 846 because the district court had refused to require proof of an overt act in furtherance of the narcotics conspiracy. The question is whether § 846 contains that element. No controlling simulated holding previously construed the statute. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Ninth Circuit shall restore the district court’s statutory-elements ruling and address any remaining preserved issues.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Overt acts under 21 U.S.C. § 846

Holding: Section 846 does not require the Government to allege or prove an overt act in furtherance of the conspiracy.

Operative rule: A narcotics conspiracy under § 846 is complete upon formation of the unlawful agreement. The Government must prove the agreement and the defendant’s knowing participation beyond a reasonable doubt, but no additional overt act is an element.

Reasoning: Section 846 omits the overt-act language Congress used in neighboring conspiracy provisions. Congress legislated against the settled common-law understanding that the agreement itself constitutes the actus reus of conspiracy. Adding an overt-act requirement would alter rather than construe the enacted offense. The rule of lenity does not apply because the statutory omission, structure, and common-law background leave no grievous ambiguity.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not lessen the Government’s burden to prove an unlawful agreement and knowing participation. It does not determine the elements of differently worded conspiracy statutes.

Legal basis: 21 U.S.C. § 846.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s statutory construction and announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This is the first controlling simulated construction of § 846. Existing rules concerning willfulness under Ratzlaf and knowledge of regulated characteristics under Staples remain unchanged.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the caption, dates, posture, historical judgment, and statutory analysis. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., 513 U.S. 30

Argued October 3, 1994 | Decided November 14, 1994

From: The Third Circuit treated PATH as an arm of New York and New Jersey and dismissed two employees’ FELA suits on Eleventh Amendment grounds. The question is whether the bistate compact enterprise shares the States’ immunity. Lake Country Estates and Mt. Healthy supply the entering framework, but no controlling simulated holding addresses PATH or a comparable compact entity. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.

The FELA actions may proceed against PATH in federal court, subject to ordinary defenses and merits adjudication.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Emphasizes the federal character of a congressionally approved compact enterprise |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Would give greater weight to state control, statutory characterization, and compact structure |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Eleventh Amendment status of a bistate compact entity

Holding: PATH is not an arm of either compacting State and is not entitled to Eleventh Amendment immunity.

Operative rule: An entity created under the Compact Clause is not presumed to share the States’ immunity unless the compact and implementing law supply good reason to conclude that the States structured it as their own alter ego and that Congress concurred. State-treasury responsibility is highly probative, but not categorically dispositive.

Reasoning: PATH has a separate corporate identity, raises and controls its own revenues, pays its own debts, and may sue and be sued. Neither State is legally or practically responsible for PATH judgments. The compact’s capped provisions for possible administrative contributions do not make every PATH liability a state debt. Requiring this distinct enterprise to answer in federal court therefore threatens neither a State’s treasury nor its sovereign dignity. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: A materially different compact that makes an entity a direct state alter ego, subjects state funds to its judgments, or otherwise identifies its obligations as state obligations may produce a different result.

Legal basis: U.S. Const. amend. XI and the Compact Clause framework.

Authority of the proposition: Justice Ginsburg’s opinion is joined by six Justices and is controlling at the stated scope.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s compact-structure and sovereign-immunity analysis without additional writing.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor dissent: State control over the Authority, the compacting States’ statutory treatment of it, and the governmental character of its functions should establish arm-of-state status notwithstanding the absence of direct treasury liability. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Lake Country Estates is clarified. Treasury exposure receives great weight within the arm-of-state inquiry, but immunity turns on the entity’s complete legal relationship to the States and the purposes of the Eleventh Amendment.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court divided 5-4, with Justice Ginsburg writing for Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer, and with the historical Chief joining O’Connor’s dissent. In the simulation, Stone takes the Chief Justice line and joins Ginsburg’s position, producing a 6-3 judgment while leaving the other alignments intact. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the historical posture, financial structure, judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### United States v. X-Citement Video, Inc., 513 U.S. 64

Argued October 5, 1994 | Decided November 29, 1994

From: The Ninth Circuit held 18 U.S.C. § 2252 facially unconstitutional after concluding that the word knowingly did not reach the performer’s minority. The question is whether the statutory knowledge requirement applies to minority and to the sexually explicit nature of the depiction. Ratzlaf and Staples preserve strong mens rea requirements but do not directly construe § 2252. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The facial First Amendment invalidation is set aside. Further proceedings shall use the Court’s scienter construction.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Adds observations concerning statutory context |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm the statutory construction below while contesting the resulting constitutional treatment | Rejects extension of knowingly across the intervening clauses |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Scienter for the facts making a depiction unlawful

Holding: Sections 2252(a)(1) and (2) require proof that the defendant knew the depiction was sexually explicit and knew that the performer was a minor.

Operative rule: The Government must prove knowledge of the operative facts that distinguish the prohibited depiction from lawful material. It need not prove knowledge of the statute, the precise legal age boundary, or the formal legal classification of the material.

Reasoning: A strictly local grammatical reading would confine knowingly to the transportation, receipt, distribution, or reproduction verbs and would permit serious punishment of actors unaware that the material was sexually explicit or involved minors. The traditional mens rea presumption applies to the facts separating innocent from criminal conduct. The statute can fairly bear that construction, and it avoids an unnecessary facial constitutional ruling. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern unmistakable contraband under differently worded statutes or require proof that a defendant knew the precise statutory age threshold.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. §§ 2252 and 2256.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone construes knowingly to reach the factual characteristics that make the depictions unlawful and rejects the facial constitutional ruling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Staples is applied and clarified. Its mens rea presumption is not confined to a physical characteristic of a regulated object; it can extend across statutory clauses to operative facts that distinguish familiar or potentially lawful conduct from a serious crime. Ratzlaf remains limited to the separate statutory requirement of knowledge of unlawfulness.

#### Historical Comparison

The judgment and 7-2 division match the historical comparator. The historical Chief authored for the same seven-member merits coalition, while Stone occupies that institutional line and authors the simulated opinion. Stevens’s concurrence and the Scalia-Thomas dissent remain. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the record, statutory issue, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Church of Scientology Flag Service Organization, Inc. v. City of Clearwater, 2 F.3d 1514

Argued October 11, 1994 | Decided December 5, 1994

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The Eleventh Circuit invalidated most of Clearwater’s financial, operational, organizational, and refund requirements as applied to churches, sustained limited identifying requirements and written disclosure of voluntarily offered refund terms, and remanded the sectarian-purpose and severability questions. RFRA became law after that judgment but before this prospective challenge reached decision. The case asks how RFRA and the First Amendment apply to the ordinance and what remains enforceable. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/2/1514/616094/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: Landgraf governs temporal application of intervening law, and Kiryas Joel requires religion-neutral allocation of civil authority. No completed simulated decision has yet applied RFRA to a municipality.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The district court shall complete the remand ordered by the Eleventh Circuit.

The broad prospective and retrospective disclosure and recordkeeping requirements and the mandatory sixty-day refund requirement may not be enforced against churches on this record. Limited identifying registration requirements and the requirement to provide a complete written statement of voluntarily offered refund terms may remain enforceable, subject to the unresolved purpose challenge and Florida severability law.

The district court shall determine whether forbidden sectarian purpose materially shaped the ordinance and then decide severability. A materially different future investigative or enforcement application must be assessed on its own record under RFRA.

#### Decision of the Court

##### RFRA and prospective implementation of the ordinance

Holding: RFRA governs Clearwater’s prospective implementation of its preexisting ordinance. The broad compulsory disclosure and recordkeeping regime and the mandatory sixty-day refund rule violate RFRA as applied here. Limited identifying registration does not establish a substantial burden on this record, and written disclosure of the terms of a voluntarily offered refund policy survives.

Operative rule: A claimant must identify a substantial burden on religious exercise. Once that burden is established, government bears both production and persuasion on whether application of the particular burden furthers a compelling governmental interest through the least restrictive means. Review is provision-specific and application-specific. RFRA applies to prospective implementation of state and local laws adopted before its enactment. ([U.S. Government Publishing Office](https://www.gpo.gov/fdsys/pkg/STATUTE-107/pdf/STATUTE-107-Pg1488.pdf))

Reasoning: Preventing fraud in charitable solicitation is compelling, but it does not justify continuous governmental access to broad categories of internal church finances, organization, member-directed solicitation information, and supporting records under threat of criminal enforcement. Targeted anti-fraud law, complaint-based investigation, limited identification rules, and transaction-specific process are materially narrower. The sixty-day rule changes the substance of the Church’s refund policy rather than requiring truthful description of that policy. By contrast, the written-policy provision requires disclosure of terms the Church itself has chosen and directly addresses evidence of misleading refund representations. The identifying provisions perform ordinary civil identification without authorizing theological review. The lower court likewise identified narrower fraud and disclosure alternatives. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/2/1514/616094/))

Limits and questions left open: RFRA does not exempt churches from ordinary fraud, contract, tax, restitution, or criminal law. The decision does not invalidate a properly supported, transaction-specific subpoena or audit, and it does not authorize civil officials to determine the truth of religious beliefs.

Legal basis: Religious Freedom Restoration Act of 1993.

##### Sectarian purpose and religious targeting

Holding: Clearwater is not entitled to summary judgment on the claim that sectarian hostility or denominational targeting materially shaped the ordinance’s design or administration.

Reasoning: Government may regulate charitable solicitation through neutral law, but it may not use an ostensibly general ordinance to supervise or disadvantage a named religion. Evidence of sustained official focus on Scientology is legally material. The district court, rather than this Court, must resolve the disputed purpose evidence.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not find that every provision was enacted for a forbidden purpose and does not disable a neutral replacement ordinance aimed at fraud rather than religious identity.

Legal basis: Establishment, Free Exercise, and Free Speech Clauses.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins RFRA’s application to prospective enforcement, the provision-specific judgment, and the remand for purpose and severability findings. He announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated application of RFRA to state or local implementation of a preexisting law. Landgraf is applied without alteration because the judgment governs prospective enforcement rather than completed liability. Kiryas Joel is applied to the unresolved targeting claim without changing its religion-neutrality rule.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation affirms the Eleventh Circuit’s mixed judgment, but RFRA supplies the primary ground for the provisions conclusively invalidated here, while the constitutional purpose and severability questions remain for remand. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/2/1514/616094/))

Research sources: The published Eleventh Circuit opinion supplies the ordinance, record, judgment, and remand scope. The enacted RFRA supplies the statutory burdens, governmental coverage, temporal reach, and relief provision. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/2/1514/616094/))

### Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund, 513 U.S. 88

Argued October 11, 1994 | Decided December 6, 1994

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The FEC filed its certiorari petition through its general counsel within the ninety-day period but without prior authorization from the Solicitor General. The Solicitor General supplied authorization after the filing deadline. The antecedent question is whether 2 U.S.C. § 437d(a)(6) authorized the Commission to petition independently. Only if jurisdiction exists does the Court reach the constitutional status of the Secretary of the Senate and Clerk of the House as nonvoting ex officio Commission members. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Participation: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No simulated nonparticipation is recorded.

#### Judgment

Certiorari dismissed for want of jurisdiction, 7-2. Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone and Stevens.

The D.C. Circuit’s judgment remains in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports dismissal | Complete threshold opinion |
| Dissent | Stone | Stevens | Would find jurisdiction, reach the merits, reverse, and remand | Independent litigating authority and the nonvoting ex officio arrangement |

#### Threshold Decision

Threshold holding: Section 437d(a)(6) does not independently authorize the FEC to petition this Court for certiorari, and the Solicitor General’s authorization after expiration of § 2101(c)’s filing period cannot relate back to validate the petition.

Reasoning and application: Section 437d(a)(6) authorizes the Commission to initiate, defend, or appeal civil enforcement actions, but related provisions enacted in the same statutory setting separately authorize the Commission to petition for certiorari in presidential-fund cases. That textual contrast preserves the Solicitor General’s ordinary responsibility for Supreme Court litigation. Ratification cannot be effective when the principal could no longer have taken the act on the ratification date, and it may not be used to extend the statutory filing period. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Effect on judgment below: The D.C. Circuit judgment ending the enforcement action remains undisturbed.

Questions not reached: The Court does not decide whether the nonvoting congressional officers violated separation of powers, whether the ex officio provision was severable, or whether later Commission ratification could cure a structural defect.

Legal basis: 2 U.S.C. § 437d(a)(6), 26 U.S.C. §§ 9010(d) and 9040(d), 28 U.S.C. §§ 518(a) and 2101(c).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Would reverse the D.C. Circuit and remand for adjudication of the unresolved FECA issues.

Public role: Author of a dissent joined by Justice Stevens.

Announced ground: Stone reads the authority to appeal any civil enforcement action through the Commission’s general counsel to include discretionary appellate review in this Court. On the merits, the dissent concludes that congressional officers possessing no vote, veto, quorum power, chairmanship authority, binding directive power, or other legally operative control do not exercise executive power merely by attending and advising. The formal capacities of the ex officio members were limited in those respects. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/6/821/576739/))

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision establishes that § 437d(a)(6) does not independently authorize FEC certiorari petitions and that untimely Solicitor General authorization does not relate back. It creates no controlling law concerning nonvoting congressional participation in an executive commission.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court dismissed 7-1, with Justice Stevens dissenting and Justice Ginsburg not participating. The simulation records all nine Justices as participants. Stone joins Stevens’s jurisdictional position, while Ginsburg joins the seven-Justice threshold majority, producing a 7-2 dismissal. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the petition history, statutory issue, historical judgment, and nonparticipation. The D.C. Circuit opinion supplies the structure and formal powers of the ex officio members. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Reich v. Collins, 513 U.S. 106

Argued October 11, 1994 | Decided December 6, 1994

From: Georgia collected taxes under a scheme later held unconstitutional. Its refund statute appeared to provide a postpayment remedy, but the Georgia Supreme Court later construed that remedy as unavailable for taxes collected under an invalid law and relied instead on prepayment procedures. McKesson and Harper supply the entering due-process rules. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Georgia must provide Reich a clear and certain opportunity to obtain the refund or equivalent backward-looking relief required by federal law, subject to ordinary proof of payment and amount.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Withdrawal of an apparent postdeprivation tax remedy

Holding: When a State holds out a clear postdeprivation refund remedy for unlawfully collected taxes, due process bars it from withdrawing that remedy after taxpayers have paid under the apparent remedial scheme.

Operative rule: A State may choose an adequate predeprivation system, an adequate postdeprivation system, or a combination. It may change that system prospectively with fair notice. It may not require payment under an apparently available refund route and then reinterpret that route away after payment.

Reasoning: Georgia’s refund statute and prior administration represented that illegally assessed taxes could be recovered after payment. The later construction converted compliance with the tax law into forfeiture of the remedy and left affected taxpayers without the clear and certain relief McKesson requires. The possible adequacy of Georgia’s prepayment procedures does not cure the unfair midcourse withdrawal. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not dictate the design of Georgia’s future tax procedures. The State may adopt prospective protest, prepayment, limitations, and proof requirements that provide constitutionally adequate notice and opportunity.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the due-process remedy holding and announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

McKesson is clarified. A State’s freedom to choose between predeprivation and postdeprivation relief does not include authority to withdraw an apparently available postpayment remedy after taxpayers have complied with the payment requirement.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the facts, posture, due-process issue, and remand requirement. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Nebraska Department of Revenue v. Loewenstein, 513 U.S. 123

Argued October 11, 1994 | Decided December 12, 1994

From: Nebraska taxed income distributed by mutual funds from repurchase agreements collateralized by federal securities. The Nebraska Supreme Court held the income exempt under 31 U.S.C. § 3124(a). No controlling simulated holding previously classified repo income under that statute. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Nebraska courts shall uphold the tax as applied to the repo income and conduct any remaining proceedings consistent with the Court’s statutory characterization.

Justice Thomas delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Income from repurchase agreements involving federal securities

Holding: Section 3124(a) does not exempt the income because the mutual funds receive interest on private loans to seller-borrowers, not interest on obligations of the United States.

Operative rule: Federal securities used as collateral do not transform the return on an independent private lending obligation into federally exempt interest. The transaction is classified according to its legal and economic operation rather than the parties’ sale-and-repurchase labels.

Reasoning: The funds advance a fixed amount of cash and receive repayment with interest at a rate unrelated to the yield on the federal securities. The securities secure repayment, their collateral value must be maintained, the funds may recover a deficiency following default, and excess liquidation proceeds return to the seller-borrower. Those are the incidents of a secured loan. Taxing the resulting interest therefore does not tax the federal obligation itself. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: A transaction conveying genuine beneficial ownership of federal securities and the accompanying federal coupon or discount income may present a different question.

Legal basis: 31 U.S.C. § 3124(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the transaction-specific statutory characterization and announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This is the first controlling simulated rule governing repo income under § 3124(a). Rockford Life is applied to distinguish a federal obligation from a separate private right secured by that obligation.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the transaction structure, lower-court judgment, and statutory issue. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Brown v. Gardner, 513 U.S. 115

Argued October 31, 1994 | Decided December 12, 1994

From: The Federal Circuit invalidated the VA regulation that required negligence, accident, or other fault before compensating additional disability under 38 U.S.C. § 1151. The question is whether the statute authorizes that requirement. MCI supplies the current simulated boundary against using delegated authority to replace enacted coverage with a materially different rule. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Gardner’s claim shall proceed under the statute’s causal standard without the invalid fault requirement.

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Fault and causation under 38 U.S.C. § 1151

Holding: Section 1151 requires a causal connection between VA hospitalization or treatment and additional disability. It does not require negligence, accident, malpractice, or other fault.

Operative rule: A claimant must establish additional disability resulting from VA treatment. The VA may exclude disability attributable to the natural progression of the underlying disease and consequences that were certain or intended incidents of treatment to which the veteran consented, but it may not impose a fault prerequisite Congress omitted.

Reasoning: The statute speaks in causal terms and contains no language requiring fault by the VA. Its express treatment of a claimant’s own willful misconduct confirms that Congress knew how to address fault when it wished to do so. Reenactment, administrative longevity, and deference cannot preserve a regulation contrary to clear statutory coverage. The regulation changed the class of compensable injuries rather than filling a statutory gap. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not make the VA an insurer against the underlying disease or against disabilities that treatment did not cause.

Legal basis: 38 U.S.C. § 1151.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the causal construction of § 1151 and the invalidation of the regulatory fault requirement.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The delegated-power rule from MCI is applied without alteration. Section 1151 now supplies a distinct controlling causation rule: administrative authority does not permit the VA to add fault as a condition of statutory compensation.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the treatment history, regulation, statutory text, historical judgment, and causation limits. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### In re Baby K, 16 F.3d 590

Argued October 31, 1994 | Decided December 12, 1994

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The Fourth Circuit affirmed a declaratory judgment requiring the hospital to provide respiratory stabilization whenever Baby K presented with an emergency respiratory condition. The hospital had the necessary ventilator and intensive-care capabilities, and no other facility had accepted transfer. The question is whether EMTALA contains a medical-futility exception and whether contrary state-law authority can permit withholding treatment the federal statute requires. No controlling simulated holding previously construed EMTALA. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/16/f3d/590/in-re-baby-k))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy, and Breyer.

The declaratory judgment remains in force. When Baby K presents with emergency respiratory distress, the hospital must provide the stabilizing respiratory treatment within its capabilities unless it effects an appropriate statutory transfer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Kennedy, Breyer | Would reverse | Would construe EMTALA not to compel treatment rejected as medically futile for the patient’s underlying condition |

#### Decision of the Court

##### EMTALA’s stabilization duty

Holding: A participating hospital that determines an individual has an emergency respiratory condition must provide treatment within its capabilities necessary to prevent material deterioration of that condition, or effect an appropriate transfer, even when an underlying condition makes the treatment incapable of producing a long-term cure.

Operative rule: The stabilization duty is activated by the emergency medical condition actually presented. It is not displaced by a physician’s broader judgment that treatment is futile for an incurable underlying condition. The statute contains no independent medical-futility exception.

Reasoning: Baby K’s emergency condition during the relevant admissions is respiratory distress, not anencephaly. Respiratory support prevents material deterioration and has repeatedly stabilized those episodes. The hospital possesses the required capability and has identified no accepting transfer facility. A court may not add an exception based on medical ethics or long-term prognosis when Congress enacted an episode-specific emergency duty. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/16/f3d/590/in-re-baby-k))

Limits and questions left open: EMTALA does not require indefinite intensive treatment after stabilization, prescribe nonemergency care, cure the underlying condition, or establish a general federal right to every medically requested treatment.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1395dd.

##### State law and conflict preemption

Holding: State law cannot authorize a participating hospital to withhold stabilization that EMTALA requires. State medical law remains operative outside the direct conflict.

Reasoning: The federal duty is conditional on hospital participation and presentation of an emergency condition. To the extent state law permits conduct the federal statute forbids, the federal requirement controls.

Legal basis: Supremacy Clause and EMTALA’s preemption provision.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the episode-specific stabilization rule, the rejection of an implied futility exception, and the limited conflict-preemption holding.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor dissent: EMTALA was enacted to prevent emergency-room exclusion and transfer of patients for improper reasons, not to place federal courts in charge of bedside determinations concerning treatment that offers no therapeutic benefit for the patient’s underlying condition. The dissent would require clearer congressional language before displacing professional judgment in this setting. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of EMTALA’s stabilization command. It creates no general federal medical-treatment entitlement and leaves nonemergency care and post-stabilization duties outside the holding.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation affirms the published Fourth Circuit judgment and adopts its distinction between the emergency respiratory episode and the underlying anencephaly. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/16/f3d/590/in-re-baby-k))

Research sources: The published Fourth Circuit opinion supplies the facts, statutory definitions, treatment history, transfer record, judgment, and competing interpretation. ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/16/f3d/590/in-re-baby-k))

### Plakas v. Drinski, 19 F.3d 1143

Argued November 28, 1994 | Decided January 9, 1995

From: The Seventh Circuit affirmed summary judgment for Officer Drinski and Newton County. The record, taken in the estate’s favor where genuinely disputed, showed that Plakas had attacked an officer with a fireplace poker, threatened that one of the men would die, advanced at close range with the poker raised, and continued while Drinski retreated. The estate relied on pepper spray, a police dog, further retreat, and different positioning as asserted alternatives. Garner, Graham, Monell, and Heller supply the entering law. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/19/1143/628368/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Summary judgment for Officer Drinski and Newton County remains in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Agrees that no least-deadly-alternative requirement exists and that the force was reasonable; does not join the encounter-wide tactical proposition |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Deadly force and asserted less-deadly alternatives

Holding: The Fourth Amendment does not require an officer to exhaust every feasible less-deadly tactic before using otherwise reasonable deadly force. Earlier police conduct may bear on the totality when officers unreasonably create or materially intensify the need for force, but the record here permits no reasonable finding that Drinski did so.

Operative rule: Reasonableness is assessed without hindsight from the circumstances confronting the officer. Availability of another tactic is relevant evidence only insofar as it bears on whether the force actually used was objectively unreasonable. It does not create a least-deadly-means requirement or authorize courts to direct tactical sequencing after the event.

Reasoning: Drinski did not initiate an avoidable escalation. Plakas repeatedly resisted, armed himself, struck an officer, issued an express lethal threat, and advanced from close range while Drinski warned him and retreated until further retreat was impeded. Speculation that spray, a dog, a barrier, or another step might have produced a better outcome does not create a triable constitutional violation. The Seventh Circuit likewise found no constitutional duty to use every feasible alternative. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/19/1143/628368/))

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not protect an officer who needlessly manufactures a confrontation, uses force after the threat has ended, or relies on earlier unlawful conduct that proximately creates the asserted necessity.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: Justice Kennedy’s opinion is joined by seven Justices and supplies the controlling rule. Scalia and Thomas join only the judgment and the rejection of a least-deadly-alternative requirement.

##### Municipal liability

Holding: A municipal policy claim resting solely on the assertedly excessive shooting fails because the shooting was constitutionally reasonable and no distinct municipal constitutional injury is established.

Reasoning: Monell does not provide liability without an underlying constitutional injury caused by municipal policy. The estate identifies no separate injury attributable to county training or policy.

Legal basis: 42 U.S.C. § 1983.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the encounter-wide but nonhindsight reasonableness inquiry, the rejection of a least-deadly-alternative rule, and the disposition of the municipal claim.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia concurrence in the judgment: Fourth Amendment analysis should focus on the objective threat existing when the seizure occurs. Earlier police misconduct may be actionable under an independently applicable constitutional rule and ordinary causation principles, but it should not become a free-standing tactical component of the deadly-force inquiry. Thomas joins. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

Graham and Garner are clarified. The Fourth Amendment imposes no least-deadly-alternative requirement. Earlier police conduct may remain relevant when it unreasonably creates or intensifies the need for force, but tactical alternatives do not become mandatory elements or a judicial checklist.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation affirms the Seventh Circuit’s judgment but clarifies that earlier officer-created circumstances can remain relevant without converting Graham reasonableness into a least-deadly-means requirement. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/19/1143/628368/))

Research sources: The published Seventh Circuit opinion supplies the summary-judgment record, asserted alternatives, judgment, and lower-court reasoning. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/19/1143/628368/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Tome v. United States, 513 U.S. 150

Argued October 5, 1994 | Decided January 10, 1995

From: The Tenth Circuit affirmed Tome’s conviction after the district court admitted six witnesses’ accounts of statements by Tome’s daughter under Federal Rule of Evidence 801(d)(1)(B). The statements followed the alleged custody-related motive to fabricate. The question is whether the Rule nevertheless admits them as substantive evidence to rebut recent fabrication or improper influence. No prior simulated holding construes the Rule. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Thomas, and Breyer.

The Court of Appeals shall determine whether any statement is admissible on another preserved ground and, if not, apply ordinary harmless-error principles.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg in full; Scalia except for Part II-B | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion commands five votes; the premotive holding commands six |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Relies primarily on the enacted Rule’s text and traditional legal usage |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor and Thomas | Would affirm | Would permit postmotive statements when sufficiently probative for rehabilitation |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Prior consistent statements and the premotive requirement

Holding: Rule 801(d)(1)(B) admits a prior consistent statement to rebut recent fabrication or improper influence or motive only when the statement was made before the alleged fabrication, influence, or motive arose.

Operative rule: A statement satisfying the premotive requirement may be admitted substantively under Rule 801(d)(1)(B). A postmotive statement does not qualify under that provision merely because it has rehabilitative relevance.

Reasoning: The Rule employs language drawn from the settled common-law doctrine and operates within a hearsay system that does not make relevance alone sufficient for substantive admission. Eliminating the temporal requirement would allow broad introduction of repetitive out-of-court statements and replace an enacted evidentiary boundary with case-specific balancing. The statements at issue followed the custody motive alleged by the defense and therefore do not qualify under Rule 801(d)(1)(B).

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether any statement is admissible under another hearsay exception, the residual exception, or for a legitimate nonhearsay purpose accompanied by an adequate limiting instruction.

Legal basis: Federal Rule of Evidence 801(d)(1)(B).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Rule’s common-law premotive construction, its rejection of free-form relevance balancing, and the remand preserving other properly presented evidentiary grounds.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of Rule 801(d)(1)(B). It creates a temporal condition for substantive admission under that provision without changing Rules 403, 803, or the treatment of statements offered for a genuinely nonhearsay purpose.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court divided 5-4. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s vote in Breyer’s dissent and joins Kennedy’s position, producing a 6-3 judgment. Stone’s complete join also gives Part II-B of Kennedy’s opinion a fifth vote, while Scalia retains his partial join and separate concurrence. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the record, dates, lower-court disposition, historical holding, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, 21 F.3d 184

Argued November 29, 1994 | Decided January 17, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The Seventh Circuit affirmed dismissal for lack of subject-matter jurisdiction. Young, a probationary minister, alleged race, sex, and retaliation discrimination in the denial of advancement and termination of her ministerial relationship. Her requested relief included reinstatement, reconsideration of her ministerial status, backpay, and damages. The questions are whether the Religion Clauses bar adjudication of those claims and whether that bar concerns jurisdiction or the merits. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/21/184/623632/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kiryas Joel* requires religion-neutral civil government. *Church of Scientology v. Clearwater* permits neutral regulation of secular conduct but forbids governmental supervision of internal religious governance.

#### Judgment

The jurisdictional characterization is vacated, dismissal of the ministerial-employment claims is affirmed on the merits, and the case is remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall enter a merits judgment dismissing with prejudice the Title VII claims challenging ordination, ministerial promotion, assignment, discipline, retention, or removal. A separately pleaded secular claim may proceed only if it can be resolved without deciding religious doctrine, ministerial fitness, or the church’s selection of religious leaders.

Chief Justice Stone delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Church autonomy and ministerial employment

Holding: The Religion Clauses bar application of Title VII to a claim whose adjudication or requested relief would require a civil court to determine whether a religious body properly selected, ordained, promoted, assigned, supervised, disciplined, retained, or removed a minister.

Operative rule: Ministerial status turns on the employee’s religious functions rather than title or ordination alone. Civil courts may not review the religious body’s reasons, test them for pretext, order reinstatement to ministry, or substitute civil criteria for the organization’s choice of religious leaders.

Reasoning: Selection and control of persons who preach, lead worship, teach doctrine, or represent a faith are internal acts of religious governance. Adjudicating Young’s discrimination and retaliation theories would require examination of the church’s ministerial criteria, procedures, motives, and remedy. The seriousness of Title VII’s equality norms does not authorize the State to decide who should exercise religious authority.

Limits and questions left open: Religious organizations remain subject to neutral civil law governing severable secular conduct. Salary, property, contract, tort, or comparable claims may proceed when independently pleaded and resolvable through neutral principles without deciding doctrine or ministerial fitness. Not every employee of a religious institution is a minister.

Legal basis: First Amendment Religion Clauses.

##### Jurisdictional characterization

Holding: The ministerial protection is a constitutional limitation on applying Title VII to the merits, not a withdrawal of federal-question jurisdiction.

Operative rule: A federal court with statutory jurisdiction over a substantial federal employment claim resolves the church-autonomy defense on the merits. It does not dismiss under Rule 12(b)(1) merely because the Constitution forecloses application of the statute.

Reasoning: Jurisdiction, statutory coverage, constitutional defense, and remedy are distinct. Sections 1331 and 1343 authorize the court to determine whether the pleaded federal claim may constitutionally be adjudicated. The answer defeats Young’s claims but does not retroactively eliminate the court’s adjudicatory power.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not address a federal claim that is immaterial, wholly insubstantial, or pleaded solely to manufacture jurisdiction.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. §§ 1331 and 1343; First Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate the jurisdictional characterization, affirm dismissal on the merits, and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes statutory jurisdiction from the constitutional merits defense and holds that civil government may not assume authority over the selection or retention of persons performing genuine ministerial functions.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Kiryas Joel* and *Church of Scientology* are applied without alteration to a distinct sphere of church autonomy. This decision adds the first controlling simulated rule governing ministerial-employment claims and establishes that the protection operates on the merits rather than as a jurisdictional withdrawal.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The Seventh Circuit affirmed a jurisdictional dismissal and treated review of ministerial promotion and termination as constitutionally forbidden. The simulation preserves the dismissal but changes its legal character to a merits judgment and states a function-based rule for ministerial status. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/21/184/623632/))

Research sources: The published Seventh Circuit opinion supplies the allegations, requested relief, lower-court judgment, and historical church-autonomy analysis. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/21/184/623632/))

### United States v. Mezzanatto, 513 U.S. 196

Argued November 2, 1994 | Decided January 18, 1995

From: The Ninth Circuit reversed Mezzanatto’s conviction after holding unenforceable his agreement permitting the Government to use plea-discussion statements to impeach him if he testified inconsistently. The question is whether Rules 410 and 11(e)(6) categorically prohibit such a waiver. No prior simulated holding resolves waiver of the plea-statement exclusions. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Souter.

The Ninth Circuit shall reinstate the district court’s impeachment ruling and address any remaining preserved issues.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer in full; Stone as to the rejection of a categorical antiwaiver rule and the impeachment-use disposition | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion commands six votes |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | O’Connor and Breyer | Supports reversal | Emphasizes that only impeachment use is before the Court |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Requires the Government to establish a knowing and voluntary represented waiver and reserves broader uses |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens | Would affirm | Concludes that routine waiver conditions would undermine the Rules’ protection of plea negotiations |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Waiver of the plea-statement exclusions

Holding: Rules 410 and 11(e)(6) do not categorically prohibit enforcement of a pre-proffer agreement permitting plea-discussion statements to be used to impeach the defendant’s inconsistent testimony.

Operative rule: Evidentiary protections are ordinarily waivable. An impeachment waiver is enforceable unless the defendant establishes an affirmative basis for concluding that the agreement resulted from fraud, coercion, lack of understanding, or involuntariness.

Reasoning: Neither Rule contains an express antiwaiver command. The Rules were adopted against the general background that litigants may waive procedural and evidentiary protections. Limited impeachment use prevents a defendant from using the exclusion as a license for materially inconsistent testimony, while case-specific invalidity doctrines remain available to police prosecutorial overreaching. Mezzanatto consulted counsel and did not claim that the agreement was unknowing or involuntary.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide waivers authorizing use in the Government’s case in chief, at sentencing, or in response to defense evidence or argument when the defendant has not personally testified.

Legal basis: Federal Rule of Evidence 410 and Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 11(e)(6).

Authority of the proposition: Thomas’s complete opinion is joined by six Justices. Stone supplies a seventh vote for the narrower proposition that a knowing and voluntary impeachment-only waiver is not categorically forbidden.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Partial join and concurrence in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that a represented defendant may make a clear pre-proffer impeachment waiver, but would place on the Government the burden of proving knowing and voluntary agreement. He does not approve case-in-chief use, sentencing use, or triggers based solely on defense examination, evidence, or argument.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Ricketts* and *Brady* are applied to a rule-based evidentiary protection. The decision supplies the first controlling simulated law governing waiver of plea-discussion exclusions, while leaving broader proffer waivers unresolved.

#### Historical Comparison

The 7-2 judgment matches the historical comparator. The historical Chief joined Thomas’s opinion in full. Stone instead joins only the narrower impeachment-waiver proposition and writes separately on burden, representation, and scope, leaving Thomas’s complete opinion with six votes. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the proffer agreement, procedural posture, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### American Airlines, Inc. v. Wolens, 513 U.S. 219

Argued November 1, 1994 | Decided January 18, 1995

From: Frequent-flyer members challenged American’s retroactive capacity controls and blackout dates under the Illinois Consumer Fraud Act and contract law. The Illinois Supreme Court permitted both monetary theories to proceed. The questions are whether the Airline Deregulation Act preempts the state statutory claim and whether it also preempts enforcement of the airline’s own contractual undertaking. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Participation: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Nonparticipation: Justice Scalia, basis not publicly stated.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Illinois Consumer Fraud Act claims | Reversed, 7-1 | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens | The statutory consumer-fraud claims are preempted and shall be dismissed. |
| Contract claims | Affirmed and remanded, 6-2 | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Thomas | The claims may proceed only to enforce American’s own undertaking without enlargement through external state policy. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer in full; Stevens as to Part I except its last paragraph and Part II-B | Supports the mixed judgment | Complete opinion commands five votes; the contract proposition commands six |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Would permit both monetary claims | Joins the contract disposition but rejects preemption of the Consumer Fraud Act claim |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Thomas except for Part I-B | Would preempt both sets of claims | Supports statutory-claim dismissal but opposes the contract exception |

#### Decision of the Court

##### State consumer-fraud regulation

Holding: The ADA preempts application of the Illinois Consumer Fraud Act to frequent-flyer restrictions directly related to airline rates and services.

Operative rule: A State may not impose prescriptive standards governing airline rates, routes, or services through consumer-protection law. A generally applicable statute is preempted when its particular application directs or polices the carrier’s primary economic conduct in those fields.

Reasoning: Mileage credits function as part of the price of air transportation benefits, while capacity controls and blackout dates govern access to flights and upgrades. The statutory claim would apply state standards external to the parties’ bargain and would regulate the marketing and administration of those benefits. Characterizing the program as peripheral or nonessential does not remove the direct relationship to rates and services.

Legal basis: Airline Deregulation Act preemption clause.

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion and O’Connor’s separate writing support the preemption holding at the same operative level, producing seven votes.

##### Enforcement of self-imposed contractual obligations

Holding: The ADA does not preempt routine enforcement of an airline’s own contractual commitments.

Operative rule: A court may determine and enforce the parties’ agreement through ordinary contract law and ordinary contract remedies. It may not enlarge, reform, or supplement the undertaking through state policies external to the bargain.

Reasoning: Enforcement of a voluntarily assumed obligation does not prescribe an airline rate or service. It holds the carrier to a business judgment it chose to offer and passengers accepted. Whether American reserved authority to alter previously accumulated credits remains a question of contract interpretation for remand.

Limits and questions left open: Good faith, implied terms, and related doctrines may be used to identify and enforce the actual agreement, but not to recreate consumer regulation under a contract label.

Legal basis: Airline Deregulation Act and the retained federal saving clause.

Authority of the proposition: Ginsburg’s opinion is joined on the contract proposition by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Breyer, producing six votes.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse the Consumer Fraud Act component, affirm the contract component, and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between state-imposed regulation and enforcement of an airline’s self-imposed undertaking. He announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of the ADA’s express preemption clause. *O’Melveny & Myers* remains applicable only where federal law has not displaced state law; here the express clause displaces prescriptive consumer regulation but leaves narrowly confined contract enforcement intact.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated disposition, issue coalitions, partial joins, and Scalia nonparticipation track the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Ginsburg’s complete opinion. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the frequent-flyer terms, lower-court judgment, historical participation, component holdings, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### NationsBank of North Carolina, N.A. v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co. / Ludwig v. Variable Annuity Life Insurance Co., 513 U.S. 251

Argued December 7, 1994 | Decided January 19, 1995, on the supplied simulated calendar

From: The Fifth Circuit rejected the Comptroller’s approval of a national bank subsidiary’s proposed sale of fixed and variable annuities. The consolidated cases ask whether annuity brokerage is an incidental part of the business of banking under 12 U.S.C. § 24 Seventh and whether § 92 classifies annuities as insurance. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *MCI* bars deference beyond the statutory boundary. *Brown v. Gardner* confirms that administrative longevity cannot overcome clear statutory coverage. Neither decision eliminates ordinary deference within a delegated and textually permissible domain.

#### Judgment

Reversed in both dockets, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Comptroller’s approval is reinstated, subject to otherwise applicable banking, securities, and insurance law.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer | Supports reversal in both dockets | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supports reversal in both dockets | Independently construes the National Bank Act and treats agency expertise as persuasive rather than controlling |

#### Decision of the Court

##### National-bank authority to broker annuities

Holding: The Comptroller reasonably construed the National Bank Act to permit national banks to act as agents in the sale of fixed and variable annuities.

Operative rule: When the National Bank Act leaves the precise boundary of an incidental banking power unresolved, the Comptroller’s permissible construction receives controlling weight so long as it remains within the statutory design.

Reasoning: Section 24 Seventh’s listed activities are illustrative rather than exhaustive, and its reference to incidental powers permits customer services forming part of the business of banking. Annuity brokerage offers customers investment and asset-management products closely related to activities banks traditionally perform. The bank acts as broker and assumes no underwriting risk.

##### Treatment of annuities under 12 U.S.C. § 92

Holding: The Comptroller reasonably classified the annuities at issue as investment products rather than insurance for purposes of § 92.

Reasoning: Annuities serve investment and retirement functions and differ from conventional indemnity against loss. Fixed annuities have features resembling debt instruments, and the Comptroller permissibly classified fixed and variable products together. The Court therefore does not decide whether § 92 would otherwise prohibit banks in larger communities from selling products properly classified as insurance.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize national banks to underwrite insurance generally or displace state regulation preserved by federal law.

Legal basis: 12 U.S.C. §§ 24 Seventh and 92.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse in both dockets.

Public role: Concurs in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone independently reads § 24 Seventh to encompass brokerage of financial investment products closely connected to banking and concludes that § 92 does not classify these annuities as insurance. He gives the Comptroller’s expertise persuasive weight but does not join an account under which deference itself fixes the statutory boundary.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*MCI* is distinguished. The Comptroller has not eliminated or replaced a statutory requirement, but has classified an activity within a delegated banking domain. The open question left by *Thomas Jefferson University* concerning deference to an agency’s own regulation remains untouched because this case concerns interpretation of a statute.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed unanimously in an opinion by Ginsburg. The simulation preserves the unanimous judgment and the eight-associate Court opinion, but Stone does not join its deference rationale and instead concurs on independent statutory grounds. The simulated release occurs January 19 rather than the historical January 18 date. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the consolidated dockets, transaction structure, lower-court judgment, historical date, unanimous disposition, and statutory analysis. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### Schlup v. Delo, 513 U.S. 298

Argued October 3, 1994 | Decided January 23, 1995

From: The Eighth Circuit applied *Sawyer v. Whitley* and required Schlup to prove by clear and convincing evidence that no reasonable juror would find him guilty before his successive habeas petition could receive merits review. Schlup asserted that constitutional trial errors prevented the jury from hearing evidence supporting innocence of the offense. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *McFarland* protects effective access to capital habeas proceedings, while *Heck* channels conviction-invalidating claims into habeas when that route remains available. Neither establishes the actual-innocence gateway.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The lower courts shall apply the probability-based gateway stated by the Court. They may adjudicate the defaulted constitutional claims only if Schlup satisfies that gateway.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Emphasizes the demanding character of the gateway and the habeas court’s responsibility to assess reliability |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Thomas | Would affirm | Would apply Sawyer’s clear-and-convincing standard |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Would give controlling effect to statutory and traditional limits on successive petitions |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Actual innocence as a gateway to defaulted trial claims

Holding: A petitioner asserting innocence of the offense may obtain review of otherwise barred constitutional trial claims by showing, in light of all reliable evidence, that it is more likely than not that no reasonable juror would have found guilt beyond a reasonable doubt.

Operative rule: The habeas court considers the complete evidentiary record, including reliable evidence not presented at trial. It assesses how the new material changes the case a reasonable juror would evaluate. Timing, corroboration, credibility, the reason the evidence was unavailable, and any impairment caused by delay are relevant but nonexclusive considerations bearing on reliability and probability.

Reasoning: *Carrier* governs claims that constitutional error may have produced conviction of a person innocent of the offense. *Sawyer* addresses the different assertion that a concededly guilty prisoner was legally ineligible for the death penalty. The offense-innocence gateway must remain demanding in light of finality and comity, but it need not require the heightened proof appropriate to death eligibility.

Application: The videotape, timing evidence, eyewitness accounts, and trial record must be evaluated together on remand. The Court makes no finding that Schlup is innocent or that his constitutional claims are meritorious.

Limits and questions left open: Satisfaction of the gateway authorizes merits review only. It does not establish a freestanding constitutional innocence claim, shift the ultimate burden on the constitutional claims, or require release.

Legal basis: Federal habeas corpus and the miscarriage-of-justice exception.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the *Carrier* probability standard, the requirement to consider all reliable evidence, and the distinction between a procedural gateway and an adjudication of innocence.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Carrier* is applied and *Sawyer* is limited to innocence of death eligibility. *McFarland* and *Heck* remain unchanged; they govern access to and channeling into habeas, while *Schlup* now governs the innocence showing necessary to pass a procedural bar in the stated setting.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court divided 5-4, with the historical Chief joining the Sawyer dissent. Stone joins Stevens’s *Carrier* position, producing a 6-3 judgment. Kennedy carries the former Chief’s principal dissenting ground, while Scalia and Thomas retain the separate successive-petition objection. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the successive-petition posture, competing gateway standards, evidentiary setting, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))
