### McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co., 513 U.S. 352

Argued November 2, 1994 | Decided January 23, 1995

From: The Sixth Circuit held that McKennon’s copying of confidential documents, discovered only during litigation over her discharge, barred all ADEA relief. The question is whether later-acquired evidence defeats liability for an earlier discriminatory discharge and, if not, how it affects remedies. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall adjudicate liability without treating the later-discovered misconduct as the employer’s original motive and shall then tailor relief under the rule stated by the Court.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### After-acquired evidence and ADEA remedies

Holding: After-acquired evidence of employee wrongdoing does not bar ADEA liability for an earlier discriminatory discharge, but misconduct that would independently have caused lawful termination materially limits the available remedy.

Operative rule: The employer bears the burden of proving that the wrongdoing was sufficiently serious and that the employee in fact would have been discharged upon its discovery. When that showing is made, reinstatement and front pay are ordinarily unavailable. Backpay generally runs from the unlawful discharge until the employer discovered the misconduct, subject to extraordinary equitable circumstances.

Reasoning: Information unknown to the employer could not have caused the challenged discharge and therefore cannot erase a completed discrimination violation. A complete defense would weaken statutory deterrence and reward unlawful motives. At the same time, make-whole relief cannot disregard the lawful consequence the misconduct would have produced once discovered.

Limits and questions left open: Minor wrongdoing, newly invented standards, or pretextual assertions do not shorten relief. The district court retains equitable authority to address extraordinary circumstances and other remedies authorized by the statute.

Legal basis: Age Discrimination in Employment Act.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the separation of liability from remedy, the employer’s proof burden, and the limitation of prospective and backpay relief when lawful termination would actually have occurred.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for after-acquired evidence in federal employment-discrimination litigation. *Mt. Healthy* and *Price Waterhouse* are distinguished because later-discovered facts were not part of the employer’s actual decision and therefore cannot establish the motive for that decision.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the discharge, discovery, lower-court judgment, unanimous historical disposition, remedial structure, and employer burden. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer, 18 F.3d 526

Argued December 12, 1994 | Decided February 13, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The Eighth Circuit affirmed summary judgment sustaining North Dakota’s informed-consent requirements, twenty-four-hour period, medical-emergency definition, abortion definition, and associated penalty provisions. The litigation was pre-enforcement, and the district court made no trial findings concerning operation of the provisions. The questions concern the governing facial standard, the role of actual access conditions and interacting requirements, and the legal adequacy of the emergency route and penal notice. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/18/526/531294/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: No completed simulated case has altered *Casey*. Before this merits adjudication, O’Connor and Souter had publicly stated in the same litigation that *Casey* did not require invalidity in every conceivable application and called for an undue-burden inquiry tied to the persons for whom the restriction is relevant. ([O'Connor Institute Library](https://library.oconnorinstitute.org/supreme-court/fargo-womens-health-org-v-schafer-1993/))

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The district court shall apply *Casey* provision by provision on a properly supported record. Because the provisions carry penal consequences, it shall promptly determine whether interim relief is necessary while the remand proceeds.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Would reject displacement of Salerno and sustain the provisions as materially consistent with Casey |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Facial review under Casey

Holding: *Casey*, not *Salerno*’s no-set-of-circumstances formulation, governs a facial challenge to a regulation of previability abortion.

Operative rule: A provision is invalid when, in a large fraction of the cases in which it operates as a restriction, its purpose or predictable effect is to place a substantial obstacle in the path of a person seeking a previability abortion. Persons for whom the provision is irrelevant do not enlarge the denominator.

Reasoning: *Casey* invalidated the spousal-notice provision without requiring proof that it would be unconstitutional in every application. The Eighth Circuit therefore erred by treating unaffected or constitutionally regulated persons as an independent barrier to facial relief.

##### Operational burden and the remand record

Holding: Courts must measure the additional burden attributable to the challenged provision as it operates against actual access conditions, and must consider linked requirements together when their combined operation determines whether a substantial obstacle exists.

Operative rule: Geographic distance, provider availability, scheduling limitations, cost, and comparable conditions are not automatically attributable to the State. They remain relevant to identifying what the enactment adds to the claimant’s circumstances. Requirements may not be insulated from review through artificial isolation when they operate as an integrated restriction.

Reasoning: Similarity to Pennsylvania’s text is not a substitute for examining operation in North Dakota. The existing affidavits may inform the inquiry, but disputed predictions may not be converted into appellate findings on summary judgment. The district court must identify the relevant class for each provision and determine the practical burden added by state law.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not hold that a twenty-four-hour period is always valid or always invalid. Nor does cumulative analysis justify relief against a provision that contributes nothing to the combined constitutional burden.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment and *Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey*.

##### Medical emergency, statutory definitions, and penal notice

Holding: A penal abortion regulation must preserve a practically usable emergency route permitting prompt action in the physician’s good-faith clinical judgment when delay creates a material risk of death, serious physical injury, substantial irreversible impairment, or comparable grave medically established harm.

Operative rule: A court may adopt a saving construction only when the enacted text and controlling state law fairly bear it. Applicable state scienter rules must be included in the notice analysis.

Reasoning: The lower court relied on textual similarity and a nonjudicial state interpretation without resolving whether authoritative North Dakota law supplied the necessary clinical latitude. A construction forcing physicians to wait for deterioration would be unconstitutional. The state court’s culpability provisions may mitigate vagueness, but their operation and the permissible construction must be established rather than assumed.

Limits and questions left open: The emergency route does not immunize negligent practice or an unsupported invocation of medical necessity.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins *Casey*’s relevant-class formulation, record-based measurement of the burden attributable to state law, review of linked requirements in combination, and the requirement of a usable good-faith medical-emergency route.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Casey* is clarified. Its large-fraction formulation governs abortion-specific facial challenges instead of *Salerno*, and operational review must focus on the persons for whom the provision is relevant. The decision also establishes that interacting restrictions may be considered together and that a saving construction of a penal emergency provision must be one state law fairly supports.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The Eighth Circuit affirmed the North Dakota provisions, although its dissent would have remanded for application of *Casey*’s large-fraction standard. The simulation vacates the judgment and requires the factual and provision-specific inquiry the lower courts did not perform. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/18/526/531294/))

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion supplies the challenged provisions, summary-judgment record, Salerno dispute, state-law constructions, judgment, and dissent. O’Connor’s 1993 concurrence supplies the date-eligible Supreme Court treatment of the facial-standard question in this litigation. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/18/526/531294/))

### Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., 513 U.S. 374

Argued November 7, 1994 | Decided February 21, 1995

From: The Second Circuit reversed a judgment treating Amtrak as a governmental actor and holding its rejection of Lebron’s political display unconstitutional. The questions are whether the governmental-entity argument is properly before the Court and whether Congress’s statutory disclaimer controls Amtrak’s status under the First Amendment. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor.

The lower courts shall adjudicate Lebron’s First Amendment claim treating Amtrak as governmental for constitutional purposes. The Court does not decide the ultimate constitutionality of the advertising rejection.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | No other Justice | Would affirm | Would enforce ordinary preservation limits and give greater effect to Amtrak’s statutory and corporate separation |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Preservation of the governmental-entity argument

Holding: The Court may consider Lebron’s contention that Amtrak is itself governmental because it is a new argument supporting the preserved First Amendment claim, was passed upon below, and falls within the question presented.

Reasoning: Lebron has not introduced a different claim or requested a different form of relief. The legal characterization of Amtrak was central to the lower-court judgment and is properly resolved.

##### Government-created and controlled corporations

Holding: A corporation created by special federal law to advance governmental objectives and permanently controlled through federal appointment of a majority of its directors is part of the Government for purposes of constitutional rights, notwithstanding a statutory declaration that it is not an agency.

Operative rule: Governmental creation, governmental objectives, and permanent governmental control establish constitutional responsibility. Funding, regulation, chartering, or temporary participation alone does not.

Reasoning: Congress created Amtrak to preserve national passenger rail service, prescribed its structure and objectives, and retained permanent authority to appoint a majority of its board. Congress may determine Amtrak’s status for statutes, immunities, and powers within legislative control. It may not free an instrument it created and controls from constitutional limitations merely by assigning it a private corporate label.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not convert every federally chartered, subsidized, or heavily regulated corporation into a governmental actor. Nor does it decide whether the billboard was a public forum or whether Amtrak’s particular restriction survives First Amendment review.

Legal basis: First Amendment and constitutional state-action principles applicable to the Federal Government.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the creation, governmental-purpose, and permanent-control rule and the conclusion that Congress cannot disclaim constitutional responsibility for the instrument it established.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule for constitutional attribution to a federally created corporation. Statutory corporate form and an agency disclaimer remain relevant for matters Congress may control, but they do not determine whether constitutional restraints apply.

#### Historical Comparison

The 8-1 judgment and substantive lineup match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Scalia’s opinion, while O’Connor remains the sole dissenter. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies Amtrak’s creation and control, the preservation issue, lower-court judgment, historical holding, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### Milwaukee Brewery Workers’ Pension Plan v. Jos. Schlitz Brewing Co., 513 U.S. 414

Argued December 5, 1994 | Decided February 21, 1995

From: The Seventh Circuit held that interest used in calculating Schlitz’s ERISA withdrawal-liability installment schedule begins on the first day of the plan year following the year of withdrawal, rather than on the earlier valuation date. The question is the meaning of 29 U.S.C. § 1399(c)(1)(A)(i). ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The plan shall calculate the installment schedule on the assumption that interest begins on the first day of the plan year following withdrawal.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Interest under the withdrawal-liability amortization formula

Holding: For purposes of the MPPAA installment calculation, interest begins accruing on the first day of the plan year following the plan year in which withdrawal occurs.

Operative rule: Section 1399(c)(1)(A)(i) requires the schedule to be calculated as though the first payment were made on that date. Interest therefore runs over the subsequent amortization period, not during the withdrawal year or from the preceding valuation date.

Reasoning: The word “amortize” assumes an interest component but does not identify when the withdrawal debt becomes outstanding. The statute supplies that answer through its assumed first-payment date. The valuation date measures unfunded liability; it does not create a pre-withdrawal debt or begin the installment interest period.

Limits and questions left open: The holding concerns calculation of the statutory installment schedule. It does not alter separate rules governing assessment, delinquency, default, or interest on payments actually made late.

Legal basis: 29 U.S.C. § 1399(c)(1)(A)(i).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the statutory calculation and announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of the interest start date under § 1399(c)(1)(A)(i). It creates no broader ERISA interest presumption.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the withdrawal and valuation dates, statutory formula, lower-court judgment, unanimous historical disposition, and calculation rule. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### O’Neal v. McAninch, 513 U.S. 432

Argued October 31, 1994 | Decided February 21, 1995

From: The Sixth Circuit assumed that a jury instruction produced constitutional trial error but held the error harmless under *Brecht*. Its statement that the habeas petitioner bore the burden of establishing prejudice could require the petitioner to lose when the reviewing court remained in virtual equipoise about the error’s effect. The question is who prevails in that circumstance. *Schlup* now enters as same-term habeas law, but its actual-innocence gateway does not answer the harmless-error question. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The Sixth Circuit shall determine whether the assumed constitutional error is established and shall treat genuine grave doubt concerning its effect as nonharmless error. If the error is established and no other lawful ground defeats relief, the writ shall issue subject to the State’s opportunity to retry O’Neal within a reasonable time.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm | Would place the risk of unresolved equipoise on the habeas petitioner |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Grave doubt under the federal habeas harmless-error standard

Holding: When a federal habeas court finds constitutional trial error and remains in grave doubt about whether the error had a substantial and injurious effect or influence on the verdict, the error is not harmless.

Operative rule: Grave doubt exists when, after a conscientious examination of the complete record, the reviewing judge is in virtual equipoise about the error’s effect on the verdict. The rule does not permit a court to avoid making a difficult judgment when the evidence supports a reasoned conclusion one way or the other.

Reasoning: *Brecht* and *Kotteakos* direct the reviewing court to determine whether the error substantially influenced the judgment. A court that remains genuinely uncertain cannot responsibly conclude that the judgment was not substantially swayed. The State’s interests in finality and federalism are already reflected in *Brecht*’s more forgiving substantive standard and do not justify a second tie-breaking rule against the prisoner.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not shift the burden on every habeas issue, alter the substantive *Brecht* standard, decide structural error, or establish that O’Neal’s instruction was unconstitutional.

Legal basis: Federal habeas corpus and 28 U.S.C. §§ 2241 and 2254.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the grave-doubt rule, the distinction between the court’s ultimate judgment and formal burden terminology, and the conditional-remedy instruction.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Brecht* is clarified for true equipoise. *Schlup* remains confined to the gateway for otherwise barred constitutional claims based on asserted innocence; it does not govern harmlessness after a trial error is reached.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court vacated and remanded, 6-3, with the historical Chief joining Thomas’s dissent. Stone instead joins Breyer’s position, producing a 7-2 judgment while preserving the remaining alignments. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the posture, dates, historical judgment, standard, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### United States v. National Treasury Employees Union, 513 U.S. 454

Argued November 8, 1994 | Decided February 22, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The respondent union represented Executive Branch employees below grade GS-16 who wished to receive honoraria for speeches and writings. Most of the expression and payors had no connection to official duties. The district court enjoined enforcement against all Executive Branch employees, and the D.C. Circuit affirmed. The questions are whether the prospective ban is constitutional as applied to the represented lower-level employees and how far relief may extend. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Application to no-nexus expression by the represented class | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The Government may not enforce the ban against represented lower-level employees receiving honoraria for expression unrelated to their official duties or status. |
| Injunction against all enforcement as to the represented class | Affirmed, 5-4 | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | The represented class remains protected from enforcement of § 501(b); the Court declines to reconstruct a narrower statutory nexus rule. |
| Relief extending to unrepresented senior Executive Branch officials | Reversed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The injunction shall be confined to the parties and represented class. |

The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded for modification of the injunction.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports the mixed judgment | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Stone | Supports relief for no-nexus expression and reversal of relief for senior nonparties; opposes the broader represented-class injunction | Pickering analysis and narrower remedy |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse the injunction in full | Would uphold the honoraria prohibition as a reasonable prophylactic employment rule |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Prospective honoraria prohibition for lower-level employees

Holding: Section 501(b) violates the First Amendment as applied to the represented lower-level employees because it suppresses compensated citizen speech unrelated to official duties without an adequate connection to actual or apparent misuse of office.

Operative rule: When government imposes a prospective restriction that deters a broad category of citizen speech by a massive number of employees before any particular workplace disruption occurs, it must show that the interests of employees and their audiences are outweighed by the expression’s necessary impact on governmental operations. The required justification increases with the breadth of the deterrent.

Reasoning: The ban imposes a substantial financial disincentive on expression concerning religion, history, dance, the environment, and other public subjects. The Government’s evidence concerned principally legislators and high-level officials, not the rank-and-file employees before the Court. Existing conflict rules, disclosure requirements, restrictions on work-related compensation, and penalties for actual misuse demonstrate the availability of narrower responses. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not invalidate restrictions on senior policymakers, honoraria tied to official duties or status, use of nonpublic information, actual conflicts of interest, or materially different statutory applications supported by an adequate record.

Legal basis: First Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: Stevens’s opinion and O’Connor’s separate opinion, joined by Stone, support the same no-nexus proposition at the stated level, producing seven votes.

##### Scope of the injunction

Holding: Relief may protect the represented class, but it may not extend to senior Executive Branch officials who are not parties or represented members.

Reasoning: The Court declines to write a new nexus limitation into the statutory text or regulations. It therefore leaves the represented class protected from enforcement while removing relief for senior nonparties. O’Connor and Stone would instead limit the injunction to no-nexus speech, leaving possible work-related applications open. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Legal basis: Article III remedial limits and First Amendment relief.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm relief against the ban as applied to no-nexus expression by the represented lower-level employees; reverse the broader represented-class remedy and all relief for unrepresented senior officials; remand.

Public role: Joins Justice O’Connor’s opinion concurring in the judgment in part and dissenting in part.

Announced ground: Stone applies *Pickering* with heightened attention to the breadth of the prospective deterrent, but would preserve applications involving compensation connected to official duties, governmental status, nonpublic information, or a genuine conflict of interest.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Waters v. Churchill* is clarified. Its allowance for discipline based on a genuinely held and objectively reasonable understanding of particular employee speech does not authorize a nationwide prospective restriction on unrelated citizen expression. The Government must justify the actual breadth of such an ex ante burden.

#### Historical Comparison

The formal historical disposition is preserved. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s dissenting vote by joining O’Connor’s narrower constitutional and remedial position. The five-Justice Court opinion therefore remains controlling, while O’Connor’s separate writing gains a second vote. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the class definition, factual record, historical judgment, remedy, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### Harris v. Alabama, 513 U.S. 504

Argued December 5, 1994 | Decided February 22, 1995

From: An Alabama jury recommended life imprisonment without parole, but the trial judge imposed death after independently finding that the statutory aggravating circumstance outweighed the mitigating circumstances. Alabama required the judge to consider the advisory verdict but prescribed no fixed weight. The question is whether the Eighth Amendment requires a more specific rule. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2. Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone and Stevens.

The conviction and death sentence remain in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stone | Stevens | Would affirm the conviction, reverse the death sentence, and remand | As-applied reviewability of the life-to-death override |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Weight assigned to an advisory life recommendation

Holding: The Eighth Amendment does not require a State to prescribe a numerical weight, fixed verbal formula, or Florida-style limitation governing a trial judge’s consideration of an advisory jury recommendation of life.

Operative rule: A State may place final capital-sentencing responsibility in the trial judge, require consideration of the jury’s recommendation, and rely on written findings and appellate review to guard against arbitrary sentencing. The Constitution does not make the recommendation binding or dictate the weight it must receive.

Reasoning: *Spaziano* permits judicial capital sentencing. Alabama requires the judge to consider the advisory verdict, identify statutory aggravating and mitigating circumstances, and enter written findings subject to appellate review. The Eighth Amendment demands a principled capital-sentencing process, but it does not constitutionalize a particular division of responsibility between judge and jury.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not permit refusal to consider relevant mitigation, reliance on an invalid aggravating circumstance, or a sentence unsupported by the findings and record. It does not decide a scheme in which the advisory verdict is disregarded altogether.

Legal basis: Eighth and Fourteenth Amendments.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the conviction, reverse the death sentence, and remand for a lawful penalty determination.

Public role: Author of a dissent joined by Justice Stevens.

Announced ground: Stone accepts that the advisory verdict need not be binding and that no numerical weight or universal formula is constitutionally required. He concludes, however, that this record supplies no meaningful legal explanation for displacing a jury’s life recommendation with death and therefore does not permit reliable appellate review of the ultimate selection.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Tuilaepa* is applied without changing its distinction between eligibility and individualized selection. *Romano*’s protection of independent capital judgment does not make an advisory jury recommendation controlling or require a prescribed weight. The decision establishes that written judicial findings and appellate review may constitutionally channel an override without a fixed formula.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 8-1, with the historical Chief joining O’Connor and Stevens dissenting alone. Stone joins Stevens’s judgment position on a narrower as-applied ground, producing a 7-2 judgment. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the Alabama procedure, record, dates, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

---

### Gustafson v. Alloyd Co., 513 U.S. 561

Argued November 2, 1994 | Decided February 28, 1995

From: Alloyd’s sole shareholders sold substantially all of the company’s stock through a privately negotiated agreement containing warranties, an estimated net-worth adjustment, and an audit-based correction mechanism. The buyers sought rescission under § 12(2) by treating that agreement as a prospectus. The Seventh Circuit permitted the theory to proceed. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Souter. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The § 12(2) claim based on the private stock-sale agreement shall be dismissed. Independently preserved contract, warranty, fraud, indemnification, price-adjustment, or other securities claims remain available.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality opinion | Kennedy | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter | Supports reversal and remand | Would confine § 12(2) prospectus liability to public offering communications |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supplies the controlling narrower ground | Excludes the bespoke sale of substantially an entire business while reserving standardized exempt or institutional distributions |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Reads the statutory definition to include private written communications offering securities |
| Additional dissent | Ginsburg | Breyer | Would affirm | Emphasizes the enacted definition and relationship between prospectuses and oral communications |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Section 12(2) and a bespoke private sale of an operating company

Holding: Section 12(2) does not apply to this individually negotiated sale of substantially an entire business through a contract allocating disclosure and valuation risks between parties with direct access to the enterprise.

Controlling rule under *Marks*: A bespoke, privately negotiated transfer of substantially an entire business is outside § 12(2) when the parties do not use standardized offering materials as the common disclosure instrument and instead allocate information risk through individualized contractual terms. The Court does not decide whether a standardized exempt or institutional distribution using common offering materials qualifies.

Reasoning: Section 12(2)’s prospectus remedy operates within the Securities Act’s distribution and disclosure structure. The transaction involved direct negotiation by the sole shareholders, an estimate necessitated by unavailable final data, warranties, and an audit-based price adjustment. Treating that agreement as a prospectus would convert § 12(2) into a general federal warranty for privately negotiated stock-sale contracts.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not restrict § 17(a), § 10(b), ordinary fraud, contract remedies, or another provision whose text reaches private transactions. It leaves standardized offerings to multiple institutional investors unresolved.

Legal basis: Securities Act of 1933, §§ 2(10), 10, and 12(2).

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s concurrence is a true logical subset of Kennedy’s broader plurality rationale and yields the same result whenever its narrower conditions are met. It therefore supplies the controlling rule under *Marks*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Concurs in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone excludes this bespoke whole-business sale from § 12(2) but does not join a categorical rule limiting prospectuses to formally public offerings.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas dissent: The definition’s reference to notices, circulars, advertisements, letters, and communications reaches private written offers and does not permit the judicial public-offering limitation. Ginsburg and Breyer separately emphasize that the plurality’s construction gives insufficient effect to the enacted definition. These propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Central Bank of Denver* is distinguished. Its limited simulated theory of culpable assistance under § 10(b) neither expands nor narrows the separate express remedy in § 12(2). The present decision governs only the prospectus requirement.

#### Historical Comparison

The 5-4 judgment matches the historical comparator, but the source of controlling law changes. The historical Chief joined Kennedy’s five-Justice opinion. Stone instead concurs on a narrower ground, leaving Kennedy with a four-Justice plurality and making Stone’s whole-business-sale rationale controlling under *Marks*. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the transaction, statutory setting, lower-court judgment, historical result, and separate writings. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/513bv.pdf))

### Arizona v. Evans, 514 U.S. 1

Argued December 7, 1994 | Decided March 1, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

Phoenix police arrested Evans after a computer record incorrectly showed an outstanding misdemeanor warrant that had already been quashed. A search incident to arrest revealed marijuana. The Arizona Supreme Court suppressed the evidence after discussing state and federal exclusionary principles together. The Court must first determine jurisdiction and then decide whether the Fourth Amendment exclusionary rule reaches the court-maintained record error. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1257 | Jurisdiction sustained, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Ginsburg | The Court reaches the federal exclusionary-rule question. |
| Suppression judgment | Reversed and remanded, 6-3 | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg | The marijuana need not be suppressed on the record before the Court. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Jurisdiction and narrow court-record exclusionary holding |
| Concurrence | Souter | Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Reserves recurring, systemic, or police-maintained database failures |
| Dissent | Stone | Stevens | Would affirm suppression | Accepts jurisdiction but rejects extension of *Leon* on this record |
| Jurisdictional dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens | Would dismiss for lack of jurisdiction | Does not reach the federal exclusionary-rule merits |

#### Threshold Decision

Threshold holding: The Court has jurisdiction because the Arizona judgment fairly appears to rest on federal law or on state law interwoven with the federal exclusionary rule, and no adequate and independent state ground is clearly stated.

Reasoning and application: The Arizona Supreme Court treated the state and federal rules together and did not announce that state law alone required suppression. Under *Michigan v. Long*, the federal question is reviewable.

Effect on judgment below: The Court proceeds to the merits.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1257.

Authority of the proposition: O’Connor’s opinion is joined on jurisdiction by six Justices, and Stone and Stevens independently agree that jurisdiction exists, producing eight votes.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained warrant record

Holding: The exclusionary rule does not require suppression when an officer makes an arrest in objectively reasonable reliance on a court-maintained record that incorrectly reports an outstanding warrant, where the record identifies an isolated court-clerical failure and shows no police participation or recurring systemic unreliability.

Operative rule: The court considers whether exclusion could materially deter the governmental actors responsible for the error and whether the officer executing the apparent warrant acted objectively reasonably. The holding does not create a general exception for inaccurate databases.

Reasoning: The exclusionary rule is directed principally to deterring unlawful police conduct. The arresting officer did not create, maintain, or have reason to doubt the record. On the record before the Court, suppressing evidence would have little effect on court personnel who failed to transmit the quashing of the warrant. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide errors generated or maintained by police, recurring inaccuracies known to law enforcement, reckless recordkeeping, or a system whose unreliability makes officer reliance objectively unreasonable.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment exclusionary rule.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Sustain jurisdiction, affirm the Arizona suppression judgment.

Public role: Author of a dissent joined by Justice Stevens.

Announced ground: Stone declines to extend *Leon* because the invalid warrant remained actionable in the integrated governmental system and the State did not establish that the failure was genuinely isolated and nonrecurring within an otherwise reliable process. The employee’s institutional title does not determine deterrent value.

#### Material Separate Writings

Souter concurrence: Repeated court-record mistakes, police knowledge of unreliable systems, or errors in databases controlled by law enforcement could justify suppression even when the executing officer acted without personal fault. The proposition is noncontrolling beyond the Court’s express reservation.

Ginsburg dissent: The Arizona opinion did not clearly rest on federal law and should not be reviewed under § 1257. Stevens joins this threshold position while separately addressing the merits. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Leon* is extended only to the stated isolated court-record error. No general database exception is created, and the treatment of systemic, reckless, or police-maintained inaccuracies remains unresolved.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 7-2. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s majority vote with a merits dissent, producing a 6-3 judgment. O’Connor authors the simulated Court opinion and incorporates the limiting concerns expressed in the historical concurrences. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the record, jurisdictional issue, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

### Swint v. Chambers County Commission, 514 U.S. 35

Argued January 10, 1995 | Decided March 1, 1995

From: The Eleventh Circuit possessed interlocutory jurisdiction over the individual officers’ qualified-immunity appeal, but it also reviewed the denial of summary judgment to Chambers County, which had no immunity from trial. The municipal issue was separable and remained reviewable after final judgment. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Vacated in part and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Eleventh Circuit shall dismiss the Commission’s interlocutory appeal and proceed only within an independently authorized appellate route.

Justice Ginsburg delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Interlocutory review of municipal liability

Holding: A court of appeals reviewing an interlocutory denial of qualified immunity to individual officers may not also review a nonappealable municipal-liability ruling merely because the matters arise from the same litigation.

Reasoning: Qualified immunity protects officers against the burdens of trial and therefore supports immediate review under the collateral-order doctrine. A municipality possesses no corresponding immunity, and the denial of its summary-judgment motion remains fully reviewable after final judgment. The officers’ immunity and the Commission’s potential liability were analytically separable. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: Section 1292(b), mandamus, and any genuinely inseparable issue necessary to meaningful review remain governed by their own requirements. The Court does not create general pendent party appellate jurisdiction.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. §§ 1291 and 1292(b).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate in part and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the final-judgment analysis, the distinction between officers’ immunity and municipal liability, and the dismissal instruction.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Digital Equipment* is applied and clarified without changing its narrow collateral-order rule. Immediate review remains available for a genuine immunity whose value would be destroyed by trial, but that appeal does not draw separable nonfinal parties or issues into appellate jurisdiction.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the posture, jurisdictional ruling, historical unanimous judgment, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

### Shalala v. Guernsey Memorial Hospital, 514 U.S. 87

Argued October 31, 1994 | Decided March 6, 1995

From: Refinancing produced an accounting defeasance loss. The hospital sought immediate Medicare reimbursement based on generally accepted accounting principles, while the Secretary required amortization over the remaining life of the old bonds under Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233. The Sixth Circuit rejected the Secretary’s position. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, and Thomas.

The Secretary’s reimbursement determination is reinstated.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality opinion | Kennedy | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion, including deference to the Secretary |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supplies the fifth vote for the statutory, regulatory, and rulemaking conclusions | Independent construction; agency expertise treated as persuasive |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Souter, Thomas | Would affirm | Would treat the regulations as requiring recognition of the loss under GAAP and the manual as an invalid substantive departure |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Medicare reimbursement and generally accepted accounting principles

Holding: The Medicare statute and regulations do not require immediate recognition of the hospital’s defeasance loss merely because generally accepted accounting principles would recognize the loss in the refinancing year.

Operative rule: Generally accepted accounting principles may inform Medicare cost accounting, but they do not conclusively determine reimbursement. Program rules may spread a financing loss across the periods to which the refinanced debt and reimbursable costs relate.

Reasoning: The regulations require standardized accounting records while separately reserving determination of costs payable by Medicare. Immediate GAAP recognition would not necessarily match the program periods benefiting from the refinancing. Amortization is consistent with the statutory reasonable-cost structure and does not deny recognition of the loss altogether.

Legal basis: Medicare Act and 42 C.F.R. §§ 413.20 and 413.24.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s plurality and Stone’s concurrence support the same reimbursement conclusion at the stated level, producing five votes. Only four Justices make deference independently controlling.

##### Provider Reimbursement Manual § 233

Holding: Section 233 is an interpretive rule that explains the application of existing reasonable-cost principles to advance refunding and did not require notice-and-comment rulemaking.

Operative rule: An agency guideline is interpretive when it explains how existing statutory and regulatory standards apply to a recurring transaction without creating a new binding obligation inconsistent with those standards. A manual may not be used to amend the governing law or impose an obligation outside the statute and regulations.

Reasoning: Section 233 does not create the Secretary’s authority to match costs to program periods. It states how existing reimbursement principles operate when debt is defeased and replaced. The hospital remains governed by the statute and regulations, not by a freestanding manual obligation.

Legal basis: Administrative Procedure Act, 5 U.S.C. § 553.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s plurality and Stone’s concurrence supply five votes for the interpretive-rule characterization.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Concurs in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone independently construes the statute and regulations to permit amortization and treats the Secretary’s technical expertise as persuasive rather than controlling. He agrees that § 233 is interpretive because it explains, rather than changes, the governing reimbursement rules.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Thomas Jefferson University v. Shalala* remains controlling on its anti-redistribution regulation, and its open question concerning controlling deference to an agency’s interpretation of its own genuinely ambiguous regulation remains unresolved. *NationsBank* is distinguished because its controlling deference proposition concerned a statute administered by the Comptroller within the National Bank Act’s delegated domain. No majority here adopts a general rule of controlling deference to the Secretary’s interpretation of Medicare regulations.

*MCI* and *Brown v. Gardner* remain unchanged. An agency manual may explain enacted law but cannot enlarge the agency’s statutory domain or add a substantive prerequisite the governing text does not support.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4 in a majority opinion by Kennedy. The same judgment division remains, but Stone does not join Kennedy’s deference rationale. Kennedy’s writing becomes a four-Justice plurality, while the reimbursement and interpretive-rule conclusions remain controlling through Stone’s narrower concurrence. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the refinancing transaction, governing regulations, historical disposition, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

### Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc., 514 U.S. 52

Argued January 10, 1995 | Decided March 6, 1995

From: An arbitration panel awarded punitive damages under a standard-form securities agreement containing both a broad arbitration clause and a general clause selecting New York law. The lower courts vacated the punitive component because New York allocated punitive awards to courts rather than arbitrators. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

The punitive-damages award is reinstated unless another preserved ground independently requires vacatur.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would affirm | Would read the New York choice-of-law clause to incorporate the state allocation rule |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Choice of law and arbitral punitive damages

Holding: A general clause selecting New York law does not clearly withdraw punitive damages from a broad arbitration agreement that otherwise authorizes the arbitrators to resolve the controversy and award available relief.

Operative rule: A general choice-of-law clause ordinarily selects the substantive rules governing the parties’ rights. A state rule specially allocating decisionmaking authority between courts and arbitrators is incorporated only when the agreement expresses that limitation clearly. The contract must be read as a whole, and unresolved ambiguity in a standard form is construed against the drafter.

Reasoning: Reading the choice-of-law clause to eliminate arbitral punitive authority would conflict with the contract’s broad arbitration promise and incorporated arbitral rules. New York substantive law may determine whether the conduct warrants punitive damages, but the agreement does not clearly reserve that remedy to a court.

Limits and questions left open: An express exclusion of punitive damages or a clear reservation of that remedy to courts would control. The decision does not authorize a remedy forbidden by the governing substantive law.

Legal basis: Federal Arbitration Act and the parties’ agreement.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the whole-contract interpretation, the distinction between substantive law and a special allocation rule, and reinstatement of the arbitral award.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule concerning a general choice-of-law clause and arbitral punitive damages. *Volt* is applied according to its contractual premise: federal arbitration law enforces the consent the agreement fairly expresses, but does not create consent absent from the text.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the contract, lower-court disposition, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Supreme Court of the United States](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/514bv.pdf))

### Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission, 874 P.2d 274

Argued January 17, 1995 | Decided March 27, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

Swanner operated commercial rental properties and refused to rent or show available units to unmarried couples because he believed that facilitating their cohabitation would violate his Christian faith. Alaska and Anchorage prohibited marital-status discrimination in rental housing, and the Commission entered a final enforcement order. The Court separately considers RFRA and the Free Exercise Clause. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/alaska/supreme-court/1994/s-5362-1.html))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

The state enforcement judgment remains in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer in full; Thomas as to the Free Exercise holding | Supports affirmance | Complete RFRA and constitutional grounds for eight Justices; Free Exercise ground for all nine |
| Dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Would reverse under RFRA but agrees that the Free Exercise Clause supplies no exemption | RFRA compelling-interest and least-restrictive-means issues |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Religious Freedom Restoration Act

Holding: RFRA does not require an exemption from a neutral commercial housing-nondiscrimination law when the requested exemption would authorize denial of an otherwise available tenancy on the prohibited ground, remove the identified applicant’s statutory protection in that transaction, and no less restrictive accommodation preserving that protection has been shown.

Operative rule: After a substantial burden is established or assumed, government bears production and persuasion on compelling interest and least restrictive means. In a commercial civil-rights transaction, the interest may include protecting each covered applicant from the prohibited denial itself, not merely ensuring that some alternative housing can eventually be found. An asserted accommodation must preserve the applicant’s statutory protection without transferring a material loss of access, cost, delay, or unequal treatment.

Reasoning: The Commission regulates neither Swanner’s belief nor his teaching, worship, or public expression. It regulates the legal terms on which residential property is offered in the commercial market. Referral or notice would still leave the rejected applicants denied equal treatment by the landlord who made the units available. No accommodation preserving their complete protection in the transaction has been identified.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide owner-occupied shared housing, intimate living arrangements, housing operated as part of a religious institution’s religious mission, or an accommodation that preserves the applicant’s full statutory protection without material transferred cost or loss of access.

Legal basis: Religious Freedom Restoration Act of 1993.

##### Free Exercise Clause

Holding: The Free Exercise Clause does not require an exemption because the state and municipal provisions are neutral and generally applicable, prohibit the same commercial refusal without regard to religious motivation, and contain no system of comparable secular exemptions.

Reasoning: The provisions do not target religion or make religious belief an element of liability. Swanner remains free to hold and express his religious judgment, but *Smith* does not convert that judgment into constitutional authority to deny another person an offered commercial tenancy on a ground the neutral law forbids.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern religious teaching or advocacy, internal religious governance, or a scheme that permits comparable secular refusals while withholding religious accommodation.

Legal basis: First and Fourteenth Amendments.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the transaction-specific RFRA analysis, the requirement that a less restrictive accommodation preserve the applicant’s statutory protection, and the independent conclusion that *Smith* governs the constitutional claim.

#### Material Separate Writings

Thomas dissent in part: Marital-status classifications do not carry the national history or settled constitutional significance associated with race or sex, and Alaska’s interest therefore does not qualify as sufficiently compelling under RFRA to override Swanner’s religious exercise. The proposition is noncontrolling. Thomas joins the Court’s separate Free Exercise holding.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Church of Scientology v. Clearwater* is applied and extended to a claimed exemption whose legal operation would withdraw a statutory protection from an identified third party. Its assignment of production and persuasion to government remains unchanged.

*Young v. Northern Illinois Conference* is also applied without alteration. Internal ministerial governance remains protected, while severable commercial property transactions remain subject to neutral civil law.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The historical Court denied certiorari on October 31, 1994, with Justice Thomas dissenting and questioning whether opposition to marital-status discrimination was a sufficiently compelling RFRA interest. The simulation’s prior grant produces an 8-1 affirmance on the merits, with Thomas preserving that objection in dissent. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/94-169.ZA.html))

Research sources: The Alaska Supreme Court opinion supplies the facts, statutory setting, procedural posture, and judgment. The historical denial and Thomas writing supply the federal comparator. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/alaska/supreme-court/1994/s-5362-1.html))
