## Decisions and Dispositions

### Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co., 514 U.S. 159

Argued January 9, 1995 | Decided March 28, 1995

From: The Ninth Circuit set aside Qualitex’s trademark-infringement judgment because it understood the Lanham Act to impose a categorical prohibition on registration of color alone. The district court had found that Qualitex’s green-gold press-pad color had acquired secondary meaning and served no functional or competitively necessary purpose. The question is whether the Act permits color alone to serve as a trademark. No controlling simulated holding directly resolves the question. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-514/pdf/USREPORTS-514-159.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The lower courts shall remove the categorical bar against color marks and apply ordinary Lanham Act rules governing source identification, secondary meaning, functionality, and likelihood of confusion, giving established and undisputed findings their lawful effect.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Color as a protectable trademark

Holding: The Lanham Act permits a color, standing alone, to serve as a trademark when the color has acquired source-identifying significance and is not functional.

Operative rule: A claimant seeking protection for color alone must establish that consumers understand the color to identify the source of the goods. Protection is unavailable when the color is essential to the product’s use or purpose, affects cost or quality, or is competitively necessary. Ordinary likelihood-of-confusion requirements remain applicable.

Reasoning: The Act defines marks in broad language encompassing symbols and devices capable of identifying source. Nothing in the text categorically excludes color. Once a color performs the same informational function as a word, shape, or other symbol, the ordinary purposes of trademark law support protection. Concerns about shade comparison, color depletion, and competitive need are addressed through secondary meaning, functionality, and infringement analysis rather than an extra-statutory prohibition.

Application: The established findings show that purchasers associate the green-gold color with Qualitex and that competitors have no functional need to use that particular shade. Other colors perform the same stain-masking function.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not protect color in the abstract, functional or competitively necessary colors, or colors lacking secondary meaning. It does not eliminate ordinary proof of likely confusion.

Legal basis: Lanham Act, 15 U.S.C. §§ 1052 and 1127.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the Act’s broad treatment of source-identifying symbols, the secondary-meaning requirement, and the functionality limitation. He announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Inwood Laboratories* and *Two Pesos* are applied without alteration. This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule specifically governing a mark consisting solely of color.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the record, dates, established findings, historical judgment, and unanimous opinion structure. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-514/pdf/USREPORTS-514-159.pdf))

---

### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Jefferson Lines, Inc., 514 U.S. 175

Argued November 28, 1994 | Decided April 3, 1995

From: The Eighth Circuit affirmed disallowance of Oklahoma’s bankruptcy tax claim, concluding that a tax on the full price of an interstate bus ticket sold in Oklahoma was not fairly apportioned. The tax applies alike to intrastate and interstate tickets sold in the State. The question is whether the in-state sale is a sufficiently discrete taxable event to support taxation of the full purchase price under *Complete Auto*. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/175/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor and Breyer.

The bankruptcy court shall allow Oklahoma’s tax claim to the extent otherwise valid and conduct any remaining proceedings under ordinary tax and bankruptcy law.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Would sustain the nondiscriminatory tax without adopting the Court’s complete dormant Commerce Clause analysis |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor | Would affirm | Would require apportionment because the purchased transportation is performed across several States |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Sales tax on an interstate transportation ticket

Holding: Oklahoma may impose its nondiscriminatory sales tax on the full price of an interstate bus ticket sold in Oklahoma for travel originating there.

Operative rule: A conventional sales tax may be measured by the full price of an interstate service when the taxable event is a discrete local sale, the tax is internally consistent, the State’s claim to the sale is externally consistent, interstate and intrastate transactions receive equal treatment, and the tax is fairly related to state-provided services. Apportionment is not required merely because performance of the purchased service later crosses state lines.

Reasoning: Agreement, payment, issuance of the ticket, and commencement of the service are connected to Oklahoma. If every State imposed the same tax only on tickets sold within the State for travel originating there, each sale would be taxed once. Oklahoma taxes the transaction rather than mileage, multistate gross receipts, or income attributable to operations in several jurisdictions. The State’s legal protections and commercial infrastructure facilitate the sale, and no discrimination against interstate carriers or travelers is shown.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not authorize multiple taxation of the same sale, discriminatory origin-based taxes, or unapportioned taxes on receipts or income earned through multistate operations. A tax failing internal or external consistency remains invalid.

Legal basis: Commerce Clause and *Complete Auto Transit, Inc. v. Brady*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the treatment of the in-state ticket sale as the taxable event, the internal and external consistency analysis, and the conclusion that the neutral sales tax is fairly related to Oklahoma’s services.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The OT1993 rules governing origin-based charges, operational discrimination, and linked discriminatory subsidies remain unchanged. Those rules concern state measures that discriminate against interstate commerce. *Jefferson Lines* establishes that an evenhanded tax on a discrete local sale does not become discriminatory or unapportioned solely because the purchased service has an interstate component.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the correct Eighth Circuit posture, tax structure, dates, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/175/case.pdf))

---

### Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc., 514 U.S. 211

Argued November 30, 1994 | Decided April 18, 1995

From: After a federal securities action was dismissed with prejudice under *Lampf* and the judgment became final, Congress enacted § 27A(b), directing reinstatement of specified pre-*Lampf* actions that had already been dismissed as untimely. The Sixth Circuit held that the statutory reopening command could not constitutionally displace the final judgment. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/211/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

The final dismissal remains binding. Section 27A(b) may not compel the district court to reopen the action.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Agrees that § 27A(b) impermissibly intrudes on final judicial decisions but does not join the Court’s complete historical account |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would reverse | Would permit Congress to alter the legal consequences of the class of judgments through generally applicable legislation |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Legislative reopening of final Article III judgments

Holding: Congress violates Article III when it commands federal courts to reopen final judgments in private civil actions and replace them with dispositions required by subsequently enacted law.

Operative rule: Congress may change substantive law and apply it to pending or nonfinal cases, regulate future conduct, alter the prospective operation of continuing equitable relief, and enact lawful rules governing judicial relief. Once a private judgment has become final within the Article III hierarchy, Congress may not direct a federal court to reopen that judgment and decide the completed case differently.

Reasoning: Article III assigns courts authority not merely to announce views of law but to issue conclusive judgments in cases and controversies. Section 27A(b) identifies actions already terminated by final judgment and requires their reinstatement under a new limitations rule. The defect does not depend on whether Congress acted unfairly, legislated for a class rather than a named party, or addressed a limitations dismissal rather than a merits judgment. It lies in legislative revision of the judiciary’s completed disposition.

Limits and questions left open: The holding does not prevent new law from governing pending cases, lawful prospective legislation, modification of continuing injunctions when governing law changes, waiver of federal claims or defenses, or generally applicable reopening rules that operate consistently with Article III.

Legal basis: Article III and separation of powers.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between changing governing law and legislatively replacing a conclusive Article III judgment. He announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Landgraf* and *Rivers* are distinguished. Their presumption against retroactive legal consequences governs the temporal reach of statutes but does not authorize Congress to revise final federal judgments. *Plaut* adds an independent constitutional finality limit.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the final-judgment posture, statutory command, dates, historical holding, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/211/case.pdf))

---

### Heintz v. Jenkins, 514 U.S. 291

Argued February 21, 1995 | Decided April 18, 1995

From: Jenkins brought an FDCPA action against a lawyer and his firm based on a letter sent while pursuing collection of a defaulted consumer car loan. The district court dismissed on the theory that the Act did not apply to lawyers engaged in litigation, but the Seventh Circuit reversed. The corrected controlled brief returns the case for decision on whether litigation lawyers who regularly collect consumer debt are debt collectors under the amended statute. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/291/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The FDCPA action may proceed under the statute’s ordinary elements and defenses. The Court makes no finding that the particular letter violated the Act.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Attorneys engaged in consumer-debt litigation

Holding: The FDCPA applies to lawyers who regularly attempt to collect consumer debts through litigation.

Operative rule: A lawyer qualifies as a debt collector when the lawyer regularly collects or attempts to collect consumer debts owed to another, whether the collection method is correspondence, negotiation, filing suit, or litigation activity. The plaintiff must still establish a specific practice prohibited by the Act, and all express statutory exceptions remain operative.

Reasoning: Litigation is a method of attempting to obtain payment. The enacted definition does not distinguish courtroom activity from other collection activity. Congress previously exempted attorneys and then repealed that exemption without enacting a narrower litigation exception. Restoring an attorney exemption judicially would disregard both the amended definition and Congress’s deliberate deletion.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not cover a lawyer who does not regularly collect consumer debts. It does not make ordinary advocacy automatically false, misleading, abusive, or unfair, and it does not decide whether Heintz’s particular demand was unlawful.

Legal basis: Fair Debt Collection Practices Act, 15 U.S.C. §§ 1692a(6) and 1692e.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the amended statutory definition, the effect of Congress’s repeal of the attorney exemption, and the preservation of ordinary statutory elements and defenses.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of the FDCPA’s application to litigation attorneys. It adds no general rule concerning attorney regulation outside consumer-debt collection.

Research sources: The official opinion supplies the corrected posture, statutory amendment, dates, historical unanimous judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/291/))

---

### Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick, 514 U.S. 280

Argued February 22, 1995 | Decided April 18, 1995

From: The plaintiffs alleged that tractor-trailers manufactured without antilock brakes were negligently designed. The relevant portion of federal Standard 121 had been suspended, leaving no federal stopping-distance or vehicle-stability standard in force. The Eleventh Circuit held that the common-law claims were neither expressly nor impliedly preempted. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/280/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The state-law claims may proceed subject to ordinary proof, causation requirements, and defenses.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Agrees that neither express nor conflict preemption bars the claims, while declining portions of the Court’s broader preemption methodology |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Express preemption without a federal standard in force

Holding: The Safety Act’s express preemption clause does not displace state requirements concerning a vehicle-safety feature when no federal standard governing the same aspect of performance is in effect.

Reasoning: The statutory clause is activated when a federal motor-vehicle safety standard is in force. Suspension of the relevant portion of Standard 121 left no operative minimum federal rule governing the stopping-distance or stability questions presented. Regulatory absence is not itself a standard.

Legal basis: National Traffic and Motor Vehicle Safety Act.

##### Implied conflict preemption

Holding: The state defect claims are not impliedly preempted because compliance with both state and federal law is possible and the claims obstruct no identified federal objective.

Operative rule: Agency inaction does not establish a federal policy requiring freedom from state law unless the statute, an operative regulation, or an adequately demonstrated federal decision shows that nonregulation itself was the chosen means of accomplishing federal objectives.

Reasoning: Federal law neither required nor prohibited antilock brakes during the relevant period. The federal standard disappeared because a court found the agency’s evidentiary support inadequate, not because federal officials adopted a substantive policy forbidding ABS requirements or liability. State-law liability therefore creates no impossibility and defeats no federal design.

Limits and questions left open: A valid federal standard governing the same performance feature, or an affirmative and lawfully adopted federal policy requiring a uniform no-regulation result, could present a different preemption question.

Legal basis: Supremacy Clause and ordinary conflict-preemption principles.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the conclusion that the express clause is inactive without a federal standard and that neither regulatory silence nor the suspended rule establishes an obstacle to the state claims.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*American Airlines v. Wolens* is distinguished. Its express preemption holding depended on statutory language that reached the challenged state prescription. Here the governing clause is not activated, and no independent federal conflict exists.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the suspended-standard posture, statutory provisions, dates, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/280/case.pdf))

---

### Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado, 21 F.3d 1508

Argued February 21, 1995 | Decided April 18, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

Colorado generally made specified criminal-justice records available through its statutory access system but required requesters to certify that they would not use the records for direct commercial solicitation. A law firm and a treatment provider refused the certification because they intended to contact potential clients or customers. The Tenth Circuit recognized that the use-based line implicated commercial speech but upheld it under *Central Hudson*. The questions are whether the First Amendment independently compels access to the records and whether Colorado’s selective denial of otherwise available records survives commercial-speech review. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/021/21.F3d.1508.92-1363.html))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer.

Colorado may enforce neutral access limits, lawful fees, privacy redactions, delayed release rules, and properly supported restrictions on abusive uses. It may not deny the covered records solely because an otherwise eligible requester intends truthful and lawful commercial solicitation under the categorical rule before the Court.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg in full; O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer as to the rejection of a general constitutional access right | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for five Justices; constitutional-access proposition for all nine |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Would affirm | Agrees that no general First Amendment access right exists but would sustain the commercial-use restriction |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Constitutional access to executive criminal-justice records

Holding: The First Amendment does not create a general right to inspect executive-branch criminal-justice records merely because the information could facilitate protected speech.

Operative rule: Ordinary access to executive records begins with enacted disclosure law. A constitutional access right attaches only within a separately established category, such as a judicial proceeding or record satisfying the governing historical and structural requirements. A desire to speak using government-held information does not itself compel disclosure.

Reasoning: Protection for the use of lawfully acquired information is distinct from a governmental duty to create or release the information. The criminal-proceeding access cases rest on the historical openness and institutional function of particular proceedings. The record does not establish that every executive criminal-justice file falls within that doctrine.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide access to a court filing, hearing, or other judicial record satisfying the experience-and-logic inquiry.

Legal basis: First Amendment.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined on this proposition by every participating Justice.

##### Selective denial based on intended commercial speech

Holding: Once a State establishes an access regime, it may not categorically deny otherwise available records solely because the requester intends truthful, lawful commercial solicitation unless the use-based restriction satisfies the commercial-speech standard. Colorado’s categorical rule fails on this record.

Operative rule: Government must establish a substantial interest, show that the speech-use distinction materially advances that interest, and demonstrate a reasonable fit between the restriction and the proven harm. The absence of a freestanding access entitlement does not remove a discriminatory access condition from First Amendment review.

Reasoning: Protecting privacy, preventing harassment, and reducing deceptive or coercive solicitation are substantial interests. Colorado, however, withholds identical records based solely on the requester’s planned protected communication and does so without a record showing why neutral redaction, delayed release, direct regulation of abusive solicitations, or comparable measures would be inadequate. The alternatives are not a mandatory checklist; their evident availability demonstrates that the wholesale use-based exclusion burdens more protected communication than the State has justified.

Limits and questions left open: Colorado need not disclose confidential records, eliminate neutral access qualifications, or tolerate false, deceptive, coercive, or harassing solicitation. A materially narrower use restriction supported by an adequate record remains open.

Legal basis: First Amendment and *Central Hudson Gas & Electric Corp. v. Public Service Commission*.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined on this proposition by five Justices and is controlling.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone rejects a general constitutional entitlement to executive records but holds that Colorado’s own access system may not discriminate according to an intended protected commercial use without satisfying the governing commercial-speech standard.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor’s partial dissent: The State’s control over records it is not constitutionally required to disclose, together with the privacy of persons recently drawn into the criminal process and the risk of exploitative solicitation, supplies a reasonable fit under *Central Hudson*. Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer join. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Ibanez* is applied without altering its evidentiary and proportionality requirements. *Lanphere* adds that those requirements govern a speech-use classification within a state-created public-records system even though the Constitution does not independently require the records to be disclosed.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The historical Court denied certiorari on December 5, 1994, leaving the Tenth Circuit’s affirmance intact. The simulation’s controlled grant produces a 5-4 reversal while preserving the lower court’s conclusion that no general First Amendment access right exists. ([Internet Archive](https://archive.org/details/micro_IA40386012_0634))

Research sources: The published Tenth Circuit opinion supplies the statute, summary-judgment posture, access analysis, state interests, commercial-speech reasoning, and historical judgment. The historical Supreme Court record supplies the denial of certiorari. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/021/21.F3d.1508.92-1363.html))

---

### McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, 514 U.S. 334

Argued October 12, 1994 | Decided April 19, 1995

From: Margaret McIntyre was fined for distributing independently prepared leaflets opposing a school tax levy without placing her name and address on every copy. The Ohio Supreme Court sustained the identification requirement as a reasonable and nondiscriminatory election rule. The question is whether the broad prohibition on anonymous campaign literature violates the First Amendment. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/334/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia.

The enforcement order and fine shall be set aside.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Emphasizes the narrow application to independent citizen leafletting |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Grounds the result in the historical protection of anonymous political advocacy |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would affirm | Would sustain the identification rule as a traditional election regulation |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Anonymous political leaflets

Holding: Ohio may not require every person distributing campaign literature to identify herself on the face of the communication when the rule broadly prohibits anonymous core political advocacy and is not narrowly fitted to fraud or corruption.

Operative rule: A content-defined identification requirement imposed directly on independent political advocacy receives exacting review. The State must identify an overriding interest and fit the disclosure obligation narrowly to the activity producing the asserted harm.

Reasoning: Anonymous pamphleteering has long enabled citizens to participate in political debate without exposing themselves to retaliation or unwanted attention. Ohio’s rule reaches every leaflet intended to influence an election, including truthful, independently produced advocacy by a private citizen. General interests in helping voters evaluate speech and deterring falsehood do not justify suppressing anonymity across the entire category when laws against fraud, false attribution, libel, and deceptive campaign practices address the asserted harms more directly.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not invalidate disclosure rules governing candidates, political committees, coordinated expenditures, substantial campaign financing, or fraudulent impersonation. Those settings implicate interests and institutional relationships not present in McIntyre’s independent leafletting.

Legal basis: First and Fourteenth Amendments.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the protection of anonymous core political advocacy, the rejection of Ohio’s broad identification mandate, and the preservation of materially different campaign-finance and anti-fraud disclosure rules.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*City of Ladue* is applied by analogy without alteration. Both decisions protect a distinctive, inexpensive form of personal political expression from near-total elimination, but *McIntyre* establishes the separate rule governing anonymity. *Turner Broadcasting* remains the rule for content-neutral cable regulation and does not supply the scrutiny applied here.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 7-2, with the historical Chief joining Scalia’s dissent. Stone instead joins Stevens’s position, producing an 8-1 judgment. The other historical opinion alignments remain intact. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/334/case.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the leaflets, statutory requirement, dates, historical holding, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/334/case.pdf))

---

### Kyles v. Whitley, 514 U.S. 419

Argued November 7, 1994 | Decided April 19, 1995

From: After Kyles’s murder conviction and death sentence, state collateral proceedings disclosed eyewitness statements, informant statements, and physical-evidence information that police had not provided to the defense. The Fifth Circuit denied federal habeas relief after assessing much of the evidence item by item. The question is whether the suppressed evidence, considered collectively, undermines confidence in the verdict. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/419/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The writ shall issue unless Louisiana retries Kyles within a reasonable period fixed by the district court.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Adds observations concerning the evidentiary record and reliability of the verdict |
| Dissent | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Concludes that the suppressed material does not create a reasonable probability of a different result |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Cumulative Brady materiality and prosecution-team responsibility

Holding: The State violates *Brady* when favorable evidence suppressed by the prosecution team, considered collectively in the context of the complete record, creates a reasonable probability of a different result sufficient to undermine confidence in the verdict. The suppressed evidence in Kyles’s case satisfies that standard.

Operative rule: Materiality does not require proof by a preponderance that disclosure would have produced acquittal, and it is not a sufficiency-of-the-remaining-evidence inquiry. Courts must evaluate the net effect of all suppressed favorable evidence together. The prosecutor bears responsibility for favorable evidence known to police and other government actors participating in the investigation, whether or not those actors communicated it to the trial prosecutor. Once *Brady* materiality is established, no separate harmless-error inquiry follows.

Reasoning: The reasonable-probability standard asks whether nondisclosure leaves the verdict unworthy of confidence. Item-by-item review understates the way impeachment, inconsistencies, alternative investigative leads, and weaknesses in physical evidence reinforce one another. Assigning responsibility only for material personally known to the prosecutor would permit the government to benefit from internal failures of communication and would undermine the disclosure obligation.

Application: The withheld eyewitness statements would have weakened the State’s principal identifications. Beanie’s inconsistent and self-implicating statements would have supported a substantial attack on the informant, the police investigation, and the interpretation of physical evidence. The vehicle list conflicted with the prosecution’s reconstruction. Together, the evidence would have produced a markedly different trial.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not require open-file discovery, make every nondisclosure structural error, or relieve the defense of ordinary responsibility for evidence already known and available to it.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause and *Brady v. Maryland*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand with instructions for a new trial.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins cumulative materiality, prosecution-team responsibility for police-held evidence, and the conclusion that the combined suppression undermines confidence in Kyles’s conviction.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*O’Neal v. McAninch* remains the rule for grave doubt when a court separately reviews the harmlessness of an established constitutional trial error under *Brecht*. *Kyles* holds that *Brady* materiality itself incorporates the required prejudice determination, so a court finding material suppression does not conduct a second harmless-error review. *Schlup* remains confined to the actual-innocence gateway for procedurally barred claims.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 5-4, with the historical Chief joining Scalia’s dissent. Stone joins Souter’s position, producing a 6-3 judgment while preserving the remaining alignments and separate writings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/419/case.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the suppressed evidence, procedural posture, materiality principles, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/419/case.pdf))

---

### Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co., 514 U.S. 476

Argued November 30, 1994 | Decided April 19, 1995

From: Federal law prohibited beer labels from displaying alcohol content, and the Bureau rejected Coors’s proposed factual labels. The Government defended the prohibition as a means of preventing competition based on beer strength. The Tenth Circuit affirmed an injunction against enforcement after concluding that the ban did not directly and materially advance that interest. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/476/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The injunction against enforcement of the beer-label prohibition remains in force.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Would give still less weight to suppression of truthful product information as a temperance measure |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Alcohol-content information on beer labels

Holding: The federal prohibition on truthful, nonmisleading statements of alcohol content on beer labels violates the First Amendment.

Operative rule: A restriction on truthful commercial information must materially advance a substantial governmental interest and have a reasonable fit with that interest. Internal statutory and regulatory contradictions that leave comparable conduct or incentives untouched are evidence that the restriction does not directly advance its asserted purpose.

Reasoning: Preventing strength-based competition is a substantial objective, but the wider regulatory system undercuts the labeling ban. Federal rules permit comparable alcohol-content information in significant advertising settings, identify certain high-alcohol beers as malt liquor, and require alcohol-content disclosure for wine and spirits. A rule suppressing accurate information on beer labels while leaving those channels and classifications available cannot materially prevent the asserted strength competition. Direct regulation of alcohol strength, deceptive claims, or marketing practices would address the alleged harm without keeping consumers ignorant of accurate product information.

Limits and questions left open: Government may prohibit false or misleading strength claims and may directly regulate alcohol content, taxation, and harmful marketing practices. The decision protects truthful factual information about a lawful product.

Legal basis: First Amendment and *Central Hudson*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the conclusion that the statutory scheme’s contradictions defeat direct advancement and that more closely fitted regulation remains available.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Ibanez* and the prior-day decision in *Lanphere* are applied without alteration. Together they require evidentiary support and a proportionate fit when government burdens truthful commercial communication. *Rubin* confirms that internal regulatory inconsistency may demonstrate the absence of direct and material advancement.

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the labeling rule, asserted governmental interests, dates, historical unanimous judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/476/case.pdf))

---

### Celotex Corp. v. Edwards, 514 U.S. 300

Argued December 6, 1994 | Decided April 19, 1995

From: After Celotex entered Chapter 11, the Florida bankruptcy court enjoined judgment creditors from executing against sureties without its permission. The Edwardses nevertheless obtained an order from a Texas federal court allowing execution against the supersedeas bond, and the Fifth Circuit affirmed. The question is whether parties subject to the bankruptcy injunction could disregard it through collateral proceedings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/300/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

The execution order against the bond shall be set aside. Respondents remain free to seek modification, a stay, or reversal of the injunction through the bankruptcy appellate process.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would affirm | Concludes that the bankruptcy court lacked authority over the independent bond obligation and that collateral enforcement should be permitted |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Obedience to a bankruptcy injunction pending authorized review

Holding: Parties subject to a bankruptcy injunction issued by a court with at least a colorable basis for jurisdiction must obey the order until it is stayed, modified, or reversed through authorized review. They may not obtain collateral permission from another court to act in defiance of it.

Operative rule: The duty to comply is distinct from the ultimate validity of the injunction. A party with notice must challenge the order in the issuing court and through the prescribed appellate route unless the order is transparently beyond any arguable jurisdictional basis.

Reasoning: The bankruptcy court had at least a colorable basis for treating immediate execution on Celotex-related surety bonds as related to the reorganization. It found that simultaneous collection against numerous bonds would directly and substantially affect the debtor’s reorganization and associated surety obligations. Respondents had notice and access to review. Allowing another federal court to authorize contrary action would make compliance optional and permit conflicting commands within the federal judicial system.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not approve every injunction protecting a nondebtor surety, decide the ultimate permissible breadth of relief under § 105, or require obedience to an order entered without even an arguable jurisdictional foundation.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1334, 11 U.S.C. § 105, and federal principles governing compliance with judicial injunctions.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes the obligation to obey a reviewable injunction from the merits of the jurisdictional and statutory objections to that injunction. Respondents were required to pursue those objections through the bankruptcy review process.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing collateral defiance of a bankruptcy injunction. It does not enlarge bankruptcy jurisdiction or resolve the ultimate power to protect every nondebtor obligation.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 7-2 with the same judgment coalition. The historical Chief authored the Court’s opinion; Stone occupies that institutional line and authors the simulated opinion without changing the judgment or the remaining alignments. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/300/case.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the injunction, bond proceedings, jurisdictional setting, dates, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/300/case.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### California Department of Corrections v. Morales, 514 U.S. 499

Argued January 9, 1995 | Decided April 25, 1995. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514499/usrep514499.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

From: The Ninth Circuit granted habeas relief after concluding that California’s retrospective amendment, which permitted deferral of some subsequent parole-suitability hearings for prisoners convicted of multiple homicides, effectively increased Morales’s sentence. The amendment left the substantive parole standard unchanged, required case-specific findings, allowed a deferral of no more than three years, and preserved authority to advance a hearing. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514499/usrep514499.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Souter.

The district court’s denial of the habeas petition is reinstated. Morales’s sentence and parole process remain subject to the amended schedule and ordinary state review.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter | Would affirm the habeas judgment | Concludes that reduced access to parole review creates an impermissible risk of prolonged confinement |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Retrospective changes to parole-hearing frequency

Holding: Applying California’s limited parole-hearing deferral provision to prisoners whose crimes predated its enactment does not violate the Ex Post Facto Clause because the provision creates only a speculative and attenuated possibility of increased punishment.

Operative rule: A retrospective change in parole procedure violates the Ex Post Facto Clause when its design or operation creates a sufficient risk of increasing the measure of punishment attached to the covered crime. A merely conceivable or conjectural possibility is insufficient.

Reasoning: The amendment changes hearing scheduling rather than the sentence, parole-eligibility date, or substantive suitability standard. It applies to the narrow class of prisoners convicted of more than one homicide, permits deferral only after findings that parole is not reasonably foreseeable during the intervening period, and leaves the Board authority to advance review. Those constraints make additional confinement attributable to the scheduling change too speculative to establish an ex post facto violation.

Limits and questions left open: A law that materially delays eligibility, removes meaningful reconsideration, or predictably lengthens confinement on a demonstrated record may present a different case.

Legal basis: U.S. Const. art. I, § 10.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the sufficient-risk inquiry, the distinction between scheduling and substantive punishment, and the conclusion that the narrow deferral provision creates no constitutionally sufficient risk of increased confinement.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Weaver v. Graham* and *Collins v. Youngblood* are applied and clarified. The Ex Post Facto Clause reaches procedural changes that create a sufficient risk of increased punishment, but it does not invalidate every retrospective adjustment with some conceivable effect on confinement.

#### Historical Comparison

The 7-2 judgment and the remaining opinion alignments match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Thomas’s majority. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514499/usrep514499.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the record, dates, historical judgment, governing rationale, and opinion lineup. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514499/usrep514499.pdf))

### United States v. Williams, 514 U.S. 527

Argued February 22, 1995 | Decided April 25, 1995. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/527/case.pdf))

From: Lori Williams was not personally assessed her former husband’s tax, but she paid it under protest to remove a federal lien from property transferred to her before the lien was recorded. The Ninth Circuit held that 28 U.S.C. § 1346(a)(1) permitted her refund action. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/527/case.pdf))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Kennedy and Thomas.

Williams’s refund action may proceed subject to the ordinary administrative-exhaustion, timeliness, merits, and proof requirements governing tax-refund litigation.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Agrees that the enacted waiver reaches Williams while separately addressing construction of sovereign-immunity waivers |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Thomas | Would reverse | Would confine the refund action to the assessed taxpayer and require use of another congressionally specified remedy |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Refund jurisdiction for a compelled third-party payment

Holding: Section 1346(a)(1) authorizes a refund action by a person who was not the assessed taxpayer but paid the tax under protest to remove a federal tax lien from her own property.

Operative rule: A claimant from whom the Government compelled payment through a tax lien on the claimant’s property may invoke § 1346(a)(1), provided the claimant satisfies the applicable administrative and procedural requirements. The waiver is applied through its enacted reference to a tax erroneously or illegally collected, not limited categorically to the person named in the assessment.

Reasoning: The Government placed its lien on Williams’s home and accepted her payment as the condition for removing that encumbrance. She was therefore the person from whom the money was collected. The Government’s proposed interaction among the exhaustion, limitations, and definitional provisions cannot overcome § 1346(a)(1)’s broader text. The proposed levy, quiet-title, and substitute-fund procedures supplied no realistic remedy in Williams’s circumstances, reinforcing the statutory reading rather than creating an equitable exception. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/527/case.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not determine when a volunteer or stranger who pays another person’s tax may sue. It is confined to a compelled payment tied to a concrete lien against the claimant’s property. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/527/case.pdf))

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1346(a)(1), together with 26 U.S.C. §§ 6511 and 7422.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the text-based construction of the refund waiver, the treatment of Williams as the person from whom the tax was collected, and the preservation of ordinary exhaustion and proof requirements.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of § 1346(a)(1) for a compelled payment by a person other than the assessed taxpayer. It does not create a general equitable exception to sovereign immunity or authorize refund actions by volunteers.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 6-3, with the historical Chief dissenting. Stone joins Ginsburg’s position, producing a 7-2 judgment; Kennedy carries the dissenting statutory position with Thomas. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/527/case.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the lien, payment, statutory provisions, alternative-remedy discussion, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/527/case.pdf))

### New York State Conference of Blue Cross & Blue Shield Plans v. Travelers Insurance Co. and companions, 514 U.S. 645

Argued January 18, 1995 | Decided April 26, 1995. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514645/usrep514645.pdf))

From: New York imposed hospital surcharges on patients covered by commercial insurers, but not patients covered by Blue Cross and Blue Shield plans, and imposed related charges on certain HMOs. The Second Circuit held the provisions preempted because the resulting cost differences were intended to influence choices made by ERISA plans. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514645/usrep514645.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded in Nos. 93-1408, 93-1414, and 93-1415, 9-0 in each docket. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The injunction against the surcharge scheme shall be dissolved to the extent it rests on ERISA preemption. Any remaining state or federal claims may proceed.

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Indirect economic influence on ERISA plans

Holding: ERISA does not preempt New York’s hospital surcharges merely because the resulting price differences may influence an employee benefit plan’s selection of an insurer or coverage arrangement.

Operative rule: A state law does not relate to an ERISA plan merely because it changes market prices or indirectly influences plan choices. Preemption remains applicable when a law refers specifically to ERISA plans, binds administrators to a particular benefit structure or method of administration, creates an alternative enforcement mechanism, or produces such acute indirect effects that it effectively dictates coverage or insurer selection.

Reasoning: The surcharges regulate hospital rates in a field traditionally occupied by the States. They neither calculate liability by reference to ERISA coverage nor require a plan to offer particular benefits, use a particular insurer, or administer claims in a particular manner. Treating ordinary cost influence as sufficient would displace broad fields of health, tax, workplace, and commercial regulation that Congress gave no indication it intended to supersede. The plans remain free to assess the available products and choose the best coverage for the price. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514645/usrep514645.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not hold that only direct regulation can be preempted. A materially more coercive state law whose indirect effects effectively compel a plan design or insurer choice may present a different case. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514645/usrep514645.pdf))

Legal basis: ERISA § 514(a), 29 U.S.C. § 1144(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand in all three dockets.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the objective-centered interpretation of ERISA’s preemption clause, the distinction between market influence and binding plan regulation, and the preservation of preemption for laws that dictate benefits, administration, or enforcement.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Shaw v. Delta Air Lines* is clarified. Its connection-with language does not make every state law affecting an ERISA plan’s costs preempted. *District of Columbia v. Greater Washington Board of Trade* is distinguished because the obligation there was expressly keyed to employee-benefit coverage.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous disposition and substantive rule track the historical comparator. Stone occupies the Chief Justice line in the unanimous Court. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514645/usrep514645.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the three consolidated dockets, surcharge structure, lower-court rationale, historical judgment, and unanimous opinion. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514645/usrep514645.pdf))

### Allied-Bruce Terminix Cos. v. Dobson, 513 U.S. 265

Argued October 4, 1994 | Decided April 26, 1995, on the supplied simulated calendar. `OT_1994CHUNK5.md`

From: The Alabama Supreme Court refused to enforce a termite-treatment arbitration clause because it applied FAA § 2 only when the parties contemplated substantial interstate activity at the time of contracting. The actual transaction involved multistate businesses, interstate procurement, and materials moving into Alabama. Historically, the Supreme Court decided the case on January 18, 1995; the controlled simulation moves the decision to April 26, immediately before *Lopez*. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/513/265/case.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Mastrobuono v. Shearson Lehman Hutton, Inc.* requires enforcement of arbitration according to the agreement as construed under ordinary contract principles, but it does not determine whether a transaction falls within FAA § 2.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The Alabama courts shall apply the FAA and address formation, consent, fraud, duress, unconscionability, severability, and other generally applicable contract defenses under ordinary law.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Accepts the governing interstate-commerce construction while preserving her objection to the prior state-court application of the FAA |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would affirm | Rejects the FAA’s displacement of Alabama law |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm | Concludes that the FAA does not apply in state-court proceedings |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Commerce coverage under FAA § 2

Holding: Section 2 reaches contracts evidencing transactions within the full extent of Congress’s lawful commerce authority, and coverage depends on the transaction’s actual commercial operation rather than whether the parties contemplated an interstate connection.

Operative rule: The phrase involving commerce invokes the breadth of Congress’s commerce power. A court determines coverage from commerce in fact, while preserving generally applicable grounds for revoking the contract under § 2’s saving clause.

Reasoning: A contemplation test would make federal coverage depend on private predictions, drafting happenstance, and post hoc evidence about what the parties discussed. The statutory language instead directs attention to the transaction itself. The Terminix arrangement was performed through multistate commercial operations and interstate supplies, establishing an actual interstate commercial connection. The saving clause leaves States authority to enforce ordinary contract defenses without applying a special anti-arbitration rule. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/513/265/case.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The decision defines the statutory reach of § 2 but does not define the outer constitutional boundary of the Commerce Clause. It does not hold that an otherwise local transaction becomes federal merely because an incidental object crossed a state line at some earlier point.

Legal basis: Federal Arbitration Act § 2.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the commerce-in-fact construction, the rejection of the contemplation test, and the reservation of the constitutional boundary for a case that actually presents it.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Mastrobuono* is applied without alteration. That decision governs what the parties agreed to arbitrate; *Allied-Bruce* governs whether the transaction falls within the federal statute. Neither issue eliminates generally applicable contract defenses.

#### Historical Comparison

The 7-2 division and opinion alignments track the historical comparator, with Stone occupying the historical Chief’s position in Breyer’s majority. The simulation changes the decision date from January 18 to April 26 and makes the decision the immediate statutory precursor to *Lopez*. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/513/265/case.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the contract, Alabama rule, historical date, statutory holding, judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/513/265/case.pdf))

### United States v. Lopez, 514 U.S. 549

Argued November 8, 1994 | Decided April 26, 1995. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514549/usrep514549.pdf))

From: The Fifth Circuit set aside Lopez’s conviction under the Gun-Free School Zones Act, which prohibited knowing possession of a firearm in a school zone but contained no commercial element, no offense-specific interstate nexus, and no connection to a broader federal economic regime. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514549/usrep514549.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Allied-Bruce*, released immediately before this case, establishes that Congress used FAA § 2 to reach its full lawful commerce authority. It expressly leaves the constitutional limit of that authority unresolved.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Lopez’s federal conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 922(q) may not be reinstated. The judgment does not affect state authority to prohibit the same conduct.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | O’Connor | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes structural federalism and incremental enforcement of enumerated limits |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Would reconsider the substantial-effects doctrine more fundamentally |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would reverse | Treats firearm possession near schools as sufficiently connected to national commerce |
| Dissent | Souter | No other Justice | Would reverse | Would apply highly deferential review to Congress’s commerce judgment |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Would aggregate the economic consequences of school violence and educational impairment |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Federal regulation of noneconomic local possession

Holding: The Commerce Clause does not authorize a stand-alone federal prohibition of noneconomic local firearm possession near schools based only on an attenuated chain from violence, to education, to productivity, and finally to the national economy.

Operative rule: Congress may regulate the channels of interstate commerce; instrumentalities, persons, and things in interstate commerce, including intrastate threats to them; and economic activity whose properly defined class substantially affects an actual interstate market. Congress may also reach local conduct whose inclusion is reasonably necessary to a comprehensive and otherwise valid regulation of interstate economic activity. It may not aggregate noneconomic local conduct through a chain of generalized social and economic consequences that would erase the distinction between enumerated commerce authority and a general police power.

Reasoning: Section 922(q) regulates bare possession rather than a commercial transaction or economic class. It is not needed to prevent evasion of a broader market regulation, and it requires no proof that the particular possession has a concrete interstate connection. The Government’s reasoning would permit federal regulation of nearly every serious local problem because almost every activity eventually affects education, productivity, consumption, or insurance. Enumeration cannot retain legal force under that approach. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514549/usrep514549.pdf))

Application: Lopez was a local student possessing a firearm at a local school. The enacted offense supplied no case-specific commerce element, and the Court may not insert one that Congress omitted.

Limits and questions left open: The judgment creates no constitutional right to possess a firearm near a school. States retain general police authority over weapons, schools, and local crime. Congress remains free to regulate interstate firearm commerce, protect channels and instrumentalities, enact an offense with a constitutionally meaningful interstate element, or include local conduct within a comprehensive valid economic regime.

Legal basis: U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cls. 3 and 18.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes broad national commerce authority from a general police power and holds that § 922(q), as enacted, lacks an economic class, an offense-specific nexus, or a necessary relationship to a comprehensive interstate economic regulation.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy concurrence, joined by O’Connor: Judicial enforcement of the commerce boundary should respect settled national authority while preserving the structural distinction between national and local governance. The concurrence regards the school-zone law as an intrusion into an area of traditional state responsibility without an adequate commercial anchor. The proposition is noncontrolling beyond the Court’s holding.

Thomas concurrence: The substantial-effects cases may already extend commerce authority beyond the Constitution’s original meaning. Thomas joins the Court but would reconsider that broader line in an appropriate case. The proposition is noncontrolling.

Breyer dissent, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: Congress could rationally find that firearm possession near schools, considered as a class, substantially affects education and the national economy. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Allied-Bruce* is distinguished. A statute may invoke the full commerce authority Congress possesses without defining where the Constitution places the boundary. *Wickard v. Filburn* and *Perez v. United States* remain controlling for genuinely economic classes and are not extended to bare noneconomic possession. The decision supplies the first controlling simulated outer limit on Congress’s commerce authority.

#### Historical Comparison

The 5-4 judgment and the associate-Justice alignments match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s institutional line and authors the simulated Court opinion. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514549/usrep514549.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the statute, record, Fifth Circuit judgment, historical holding, and complete opinion lineup. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514549/usrep514549.pdf))

### United States v. Harris, 25 F.3d 1275

Argued March 1, 1995 | Decided April 27, 1995, on the supplied simulated calendar. `OT_1994CHUNK5.md`

From: Harris and Hoskins used a sawed-off shotgun and an ax handle to force the occupants from a 1976 Buick Electra and take the vehicle. The Buick had been manufactured in Michigan and titled in Arkansas and Mississippi. The Fifth Circuit affirmed convictions under the then-operative carjacking statute, 18 U.S.C. § 2119, and the consecutive firearm provision, § 924(c), relying in substantial part on the aggregate economic effects of carjacking. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/25/1275/571794/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Lopez* rejects downstream aggregate social effects as a substitute for an enumerated-power anchor, while preserving direct congressional authority over channels, instrumentalities, persons, and things in interstate commerce. *X-Citement Video*, *Staples*, and *Ratzlaf* govern distinct mens rea questions.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The convictions and consecutive sentences remain in force.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Direct protection of an interstate transportation instrumentality

Holding: The Commerce Clause permits Congress to protect a motor vehicle against a direct intrastate forcible taking when the federal offense requires proof of the particular vehicle’s interstate connection. Section 2119 is constitutional as applied to the taking of the Buick proved here.

Operative rule: An offense-specific commerce element must perform real constitutional work rather than add a remote interstate fact to an ordinary local crime. A direct attack on possession and control of a transportation instrumentality, combined with proof that the particular vehicle moved through interstate commerce, falls within Congress’s authority over instrumentalities and things in commerce.

Reasoning: The Fifth Circuit’s theory that carjacking is federal because violent crime affects travel, insurance, and the national economy cannot survive *Lopez*. The judgment is sustained on a narrower ground. The object of the offense is the motor vehicle itself, an instrumentality of transportation, and § 2119 requires proof that the vehicle was transported, shipped, or received in interstate or foreign commerce. The Buick’s manufacture and multistate title history established that enacted connection. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/25/1275/571794/))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not hold that every local offense involving an object that once crossed a state line is federal, that every crime occurring in or near a car is subject to federal jurisdiction, or that generalized insurance and productivity effects are sufficient. The holding rests on the direct forcible seizure of the transportation instrumentality and the statute’s vehicle-specific interstate requirement.

Legal basis: U.S. Const. art. I, § 8, cls. 3 and 18.

##### Mens rea and fair notice under the then-operative § 2119

Holding: Section 2119 requires knowing participation in the taking of a covered vehicle by force, violence, or intimidation. It does not require knowledge that the vehicle moved through interstate commerce or a separate intent permanently to deprive the owner.

Reasoning: The traditional mens rea presumption attaches to the conduct that makes the act wrongful. Forcibly removing occupants at gunpoint is not apparently innocent behavior whose criminality turns on an obscure characteristic. The interstate history is jurisdictional, and the statute contains no willfulness language requiring knowledge of federal law. The trial evidence established knowing participation in the forcible taking. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/25/1275/571794/))

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not construe materially different statutory language or remove mens rea from a fact Congress makes part of the wrongful conduct itself.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 2119 and the Due Process Clause.

##### Consecutive punishment under § 924(c)

Holding: Congress authorized a consecutive § 924(c) sentence in addition to the sentence for carjacking under the then-operative § 2119, so the cumulative punishment imposed in this single prosecution does not violate the Double Jeopardy Clause.

Reasoning: *Blockburger* supplies a rule for determining legislative intent when that intent is uncertain. Section 924(c)’s additional-and-consecutive language resolves the issue directly, even though the same firearm conduct also satisfied an element of the carjacking offense. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/25/1275/571794/))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide a charging pair for which Congress supplied no clear cumulative-punishment instruction.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 924(c) and the Double Jeopardy Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the rejection of the Fifth Circuit’s aggregate-effects rationale, the narrower instrumentality holding, the mens rea construction, and the conclusion that Congress expressly authorized the consecutive firearm sentence.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Lopez* is applied and clarified. A jurisdictional element is not talismanic; it must identify a constitutionally relevant connection. Section 2119 satisfies that requirement here because the offense directly attacks the covered transportation instrumentality itself. *X-Citement Video* and *Staples* are applied to the facts constituting wrongful conduct but are not extended to require knowledge of a jurisdictional interstate fact. *Ratzlaf* remains confined to its separate willfulness language.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The Fifth Circuit affirmed both convictions and sentences through a broader aggregate-effects rationale. The simulation preserves the judgment but rejects that rationale after *Lopez*, relying instead on direct protection of a transportation instrumentality and the enacted vehicle-specific commerce element. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/25/1275/571794/))

Research sources: The published Fifth Circuit opinion supplies the facts, statutory text, jury evidence, lower-court commerce rationale, mens rea treatment, cumulative-punishment ruling, and judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/25/1275/571794/))

### United States v. Robertson, 514 U.S. 669

Argued February 27, 1995 | Decided May 1, 1995. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/669/case.pdf))

From: The Ninth Circuit reversed Robertson’s RICO conviction after finding insufficient proof that his Alaska gold mine was an enterprise engaged in or affecting interstate commerce. The Government had shown that the mine purchased equipment and supplies in California, brought workers from outside Alaska, and shipped 15 percent of its output out of State. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/669/case.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Lopez* requires a real and judicially bounded commerce connection. *Harris* confirms that a statutory commerce element must perform constitutional work rather than rely on generalized downstream effects.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The RICO conviction may be reinstated subject to any remaining preserved appellate claims.

#### Opinion

Per curiam.

#### Decision of the Court

##### A RICO enterprise’s own interstate operations

Holding: Robertson’s gold mine was engaged in interstate commerce within 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a).

Operative rule: An enterprise is engaged in interstate commerce when its own continuing operations include interstate purchasing, interstate recruitment or movement of labor, and interstate shipment of output. When that direct commerce is proved, no separate substantial-effects analysis is necessary.

Reasoning: The commerce facts describe the mine’s regular commercial operation rather than the remote history of an incidental object or a chain of downstream social consequences. The mine acquired inputs across state lines, used out-of-state workers, and shipped a meaningful portion of its production interstate. That proof satisfies the statutory element directly. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/669/case.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether a purely intrastate enterprise with only remote economic consequences satisfies § 1962(a).

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 1962(a).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joins the per curiam opinion.

Announced ground: Stone joins the direct interstate-operations analysis and the decision not to rely on aggregation or attenuated substantial effects.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Lopez* and *Harris* are applied without alteration. *National Organization for Women v. Scheidler* remains unchanged: RICO contains no economic-motive element, but the statute’s separate interstate-enterprise requirement must still be proved.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous per curiam reversal tracks the historical comparator. The simulation additionally recognizes *Lopez* and *Harris* as entering law but reaches the same result through the mine’s direct interstate activity. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/669/case.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the enterprise facts, statutory element, historical judgment, and per curiam disposition. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/669/case.pdf))
