### Hubbard v. United States, 514 U.S. 695

Argued February 21, 1995 | Decided May 15, 1995. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514695/usrep514695.pdf))

From: Hubbard’s false statements in unsworn bankruptcy filings produced convictions under 18 U.S.C. § 1001 after the trial court instructed that a bankruptcy court was a federal department. The Sixth Circuit affirmed and rejected the lower-court doctrine that had excluded adjudicative, but not administrative, judicial functions from the statute. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514695/usrep514695.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed in part and remanded, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor and Souter.

The § 1001 convictions based on the bankruptcy filings shall be vacated. Unaffected counts and proceedings remain governed by the lower court’s judgment.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer as to Parts I, II, III, and VI; Ginsburg and Breyer also as to Parts IV and V | Supports reversal in part | Six-Justice statutory holding and overruling of *Bramblett*; three-Justice complete stare decisis rationale |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy | Supports reversal in part | Agrees that *Bramblett* must be overruled but relies on the threat to adversarial advocacy and the impossibility of a textually sound judicial-function exception |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supports reversal in part | Would resolve adjudicative judicial filings on a narrower statutory-stare-decisis ground |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Souter | Would affirm the § 1001 convictions | Would retain *Bramblett* under statutory stare decisis and leave correction to Congress |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Federal courts under 18 U.S.C. § 1001

Holding: A federal court is neither a department nor an agency within § 1001, and the statute therefore does not criminalize false statements on the theory that a judicial proceeding is a matter within the jurisdiction of a federal department or agency.

Operative rule: The terms department and agency in § 1001 are governed by their ordinary executive-branch meaning and the definitions applicable throughout Title 18. A federal court does not change character depending on whether it performs adjudicative, administrative, or housekeeping work.

Reasoning: Section 6 defines agency through a list of executive-type institutions and presumptively defines department as an executive department unless statutory context shows otherwise. Nothing in § 1001 supplies the required contrary context. A branch-wide construction derived from generalized statutory purpose cannot overcome that text, and a judicial-function exception would preserve the same erroneous premise while adding distinctions the statute does not contain. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514695/usrep514695.pdf))

Application: Hubbard’s unsworn bankruptcy papers were submitted in a judicial proceeding and cannot support the challenged § 1001 convictions. Other statutes governing perjury, bankruptcy fraud, false claims, contempt, and obstruction remain available according to their terms.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not protect false statements made to executive agencies merely because litigation is pending. Congress may enact a different rule expressly covering the Judicial and Legislative Branches and may define exceptions appropriate to adversarial proceedings.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. §§ 6 and 1001.

Authority of the proposition: Parts I, II, III, and VI of Stevens’s opinion are joined by Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer and supply a six-Justice majority. The Court’s statutory holding and overruling of *United States v. Bramblett* are controlling. No single broader account of statutory stare decisis commands a majority because Parts IV and V have only three votes, while Scalia and Kennedy rely on a separate rationale. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514695/usrep514695.pdf))

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse the § 1001 component and remand.

Public role: Concurs in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone concludes that the false statements in adjudicative bankruptcy filings fall outside § 1001. He gives statutory stare decisis substantial weight but finds special justification for overruling *Bramblett* in this setting because its branch-wide language exceeds its legislative-record facts, conflicts with the enacted definitions, and has generated an unadministrable judicial-function exception. Stone does not join the Court’s treatment of every possible judicial administrative function.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia concurrence, joined by Kennedy: *Bramblett* should be overruled because its construction threatens vigorous adversarial representation and can be confined only through irrational or textually unsupported exceptions. This rationale supports the judgment but is not the Court’s general statutory-stare-decisis rule.

O’Connor dissent, joined by Souter: The special force of statutory stare decisis requires adherence to *Bramblett* until Congress changes the statute. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*United States v. Bramblett* is overruled. Its construction treating the three branches as departments under § 1001 no longer controls. The decision establishes the statutory boundary but does not create a generally controlling test for overruling statutory precedent beyond the proposition-level majority identified above.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed in part, 6-3, with the historical Chief dissenting alongside O’Connor and Souter. Stone agrees with reversal but writes on a narrower adjudicative ground, producing a 7-2 judgment. Stevens’s six-Justice controlling statutory coalition otherwise remains intact. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514695/usrep514695.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the bankruptcy filings, question presented, statutory definitions, historical judgment, partial joins, overruling of *Bramblett*, and separate writings. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514695/usrep514695.pdf))

### Reynoldsville Casket Co. v. Hyde, 514 U.S. 749

Argued February 27, 1995 | Decided May 15, 1995. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514749/usrep514749.pdf))

From: Hyde’s Ohio tort action was timely only because of a limitations-tolling provision later held unconstitutional in *Bendix*. The Ohio Supreme Court nevertheless reinstated the suit, reasoning that *Bendix* should not be applied to claims accruing before that decision because parties may have relied on the former statute. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514749/usrep514749.pdf))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. No opposing Justice.

The Ohio courts shall apply the governing federal rule to this nonfinal case and adjudicate any remaining issues without relying on the invalid tolling provision.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal | Agrees with the result through a distinct account of retroactivity |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Kennedy | O’Connor | Supports reversal | Emphasizes remedial authority while rejecting the particular reliance exception |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Reliance and retroactive application of a federal constitutional rule

Holding: A state court may not deny the ordinary retroactive application of a controlling federal constitutional rule to a nonfinal case merely because a party relied on the invalidated state statute.

Operative rule: Once this Court applies a federal rule to the parties before it, that rule governs other cases still open on direct review. A different outcome remains possible when an independently valid legal doctrine supplies it, such as an alternative cure, a preexisting remedial rule, qualified immunity, res judicata, or a doctrine limiting retroactivity itself. Simple reliance on the former law does not create a free-standing exception.

Reasoning: *Harper* requires *Bendix* to govern Hyde’s still-pending case. Calling the refusal to apply *Bendix* a remedy does not change its operation; the Ohio judgment preserves the unconstitutional tolling rule for the very cases to which federal retroactivity applies. No independent state rule or established remedial doctrine supports that result. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514749/usrep514749.pdf))

Limits and questions left open: Ordinary rules concerning final judgments, statutes of repose, waiver, harmless error, qualified immunity, and other independently valid remedial doctrines remain intact.

Legal basis: Supremacy Clause and the federal retroactivity rule of *Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between retroactivity and remedy, the rejection of a free-standing reliance exception, and the preservation of independently valid procedural and remedial doctrines.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Harper* is applied and clarified. Reliance alone cannot produce selective prospectivity under a remedial label. *Landgraf* and *Rivers* are distinguished because they govern the temporal reach of newly enacted statutes, not the application of a federal judicial rule to cases still open on direct review.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment and opinion structure track the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Breyer’s Court opinion. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514749/usrep514749.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the Ohio tolling rule, procedural posture, *Harper* analysis, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Library of Congress Tile

](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep514/usrep514749/usrep514749.pdf))

### A. Law entering the case

#### Issue 1: Whether thermal imaging is a search

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No simulated holding specifically governs thermal imaging. Existing Fourth Amendment law protects the home and recognizes both property-based and reasonable-expectation paths.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No registered reusable doctrine directly resolves sense-enhancing thermal surveillance.
* **Other Governing Law:** Fourth Amendment; *Katz v. United States*; *Silverman v. United States*; *United States v. Karo*; *Dow Chemical Co. v. United States*.
* **Other Authority Used:** Technology used from a lawful public vantage does not necessarily escape the Fourth Amendment when it is employed to obtain information about the interior of a home that ordinary public observation could not acquire.
* **Open Point:** Existing law does not specifically resolve whether the warrantless use of thermal-imaging technology to obtain information concerning activity inside a home constitutes a search.

### B. Stone's approved position

* **Stone's Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** **VACATE the judgment of the Eighth Circuit and REMAND.**
* **Stone's Remedy and Remand Instruction:** On remand, the lower courts shall give effect to the holding that the warrantless thermal scan constituted a Fourth Amendment search. Stone does not direct the lower courts to excise the thermal-scan information and determine whether the remaining warrant affidavit independently established probable cause, because Pinson did not preserve an appellate challenge to the affidavit's sufficiency and the Eighth Circuit expressly made no determination of that issue. Any further relief, including any question concerning the warrant, suppression, or good faith, remains subject to ordinary preservation, forfeiture, and remedial law and may be considered only to the extent lawfully open on remand.

#### Issue 1: Whether thermal imaging is a search

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the preserved Fourth Amendment question whether the warrantless thermal scan was a search. He does not reach the separate sufficiency of the warrant affidavit after removal of the thermal information. Pinson did not preserve that issue on appeal, and the Eighth Circuit expressly declined to determine it. The Court therefore has no basis in this case to direct a new probable-cause determination as though that question had been preserved.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** The home's Fourth Amendment protection does not end at the physical surface of its walls. Officers deliberately used sense-enhancing technology from outside the residence to obtain information about patterns of heat associated with activity occurring inside that ordinary public observation could not reveal. The acquisition of that otherwise unavailable information about the interior of the home is a search. The absence of physical entry does not remove the surveillance from the Fourth Amendment merely because the instrument detects heat rather than producing a conventional visual image.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Law-enforcement use of sense-enhancing technology from outside a home to obtain information about activity within the home that ordinary public observation could not otherwise obtain constitutes a Fourth Amendment search. The warrantless thermal scan of Pinson's residence therefore was a search.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The home lies at the center of the security protected by the Fourth Amendment. The officers did not merely observe something exposed to ordinary public perception. They employed a technological instrument for the purpose of learning something about activity occurring behind the walls of a residence that their unaided senses could not discern. Constitutional protection cannot depend on whether information about the interior is extracted through reflected light, sound, heat, or some other physical signal. A rule treating every measurable emission escaping a home as abandoned to unrestricted governmental examination would permit increasingly capable instruments to obtain information about domestic activity without constitutional process while leaving the formal protection of the home intact only against physical entry. The Fourth Amendment protects the security of the interior, not merely the walls surrounding it.

  The Eighth Circuit affirmed on the premise that use of the thermal device was not a search. Stone would reject that premise. That resolves the preserved constitutional question. It does not authorize Stone to decide a different question concerning the legal sufficiency of the warrant after the thermal information is removed. The published appellate record states that Pinson did not challenge the affidavit's sufficiency on appeal and that the Eighth Circuit therefore made no determination of that issue. The proper course is accordingly to vacate the judgment resting on the erroneous no-search rule and return the case for whatever further proceedings remain lawfully available under ordinary preservation and remedial principles.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not hold that ordinary visual observation from a lawful vantage, smoke or other conditions perceptible to unaided members of the public, utility records obtained through lawful process, or every technological measurement made near private property constitutes a search. The holding concerns deliberate technological acquisition of otherwise unavailable information about activity inside a home. Stone also expresses no view on whether the warrant affidavit would establish probable cause without the thermal-scan information, whether any such question remains procedurally available after remand, whether suppression ultimately follows, or whether *United States v. Leon* applies. Those matters are not decided because the warrant-sufficiency issue was not preserved on appeal and was not determined by the Eighth Circuit.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Pinson, 24 F.3d 1056

Argued March 20, 1995 | Decided May 8, 1995

From: The Eighth Circuit held that warrantless aerial use of a forward-looking infrared device to identify unusual surface-heat patterns at Pinson’s home was not a Fourth Amendment search. The thermal information, electrical-usage evidence, and other investigative facts appeared in the later warrant affidavit. The court expressly stated that Pinson had not challenged the affidavit’s sufficiency on appeal and that it made no determination of that issue. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/24/1056/499231/))

Question presented: Whether law-enforcement use of thermal-imaging technology from outside a home to obtain information concerning activity inside the home constitutes a Fourth Amendment search.

Controlling simulated law before decision: No completed simulated holding specifically governs sense-enhancing thermal surveillance. *Arizona v. Evans* concerns exclusion for erroneous warrant records and does not determine whether technological acquisition of information from a home is a search.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, O’Connor, and Kennedy.

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The lower courts shall give effect to the holding that the warrantless thermal scan constituted a Fourth Amendment search. The Court does not direct excision of the thermal information or a new determination whether the remaining affidavit independently established probable cause because Pinson did not preserve that appellate issue and the Eighth Circuit did not decide it.

Any further question concerning the warrant, suppression, good faith, forfeiture, or another remedial doctrine may be considered only to the extent it remains lawfully open on remand.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | O’Connor, Kennedy | Would affirm | Would treat measurement of heat reaching the home’s exterior as observation of a condition exposed beyond the protected interior |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Technological acquisition of information from inside a home

Holding: Law-enforcement use of sense-enhancing technology from outside a home to obtain information about activity within the home that ordinary public observation could not otherwise acquire constitutes a Fourth Amendment search. The thermal scan of Pinson’s residence was a search.

Operative rule: Physical entry is unnecessary when officers deliberately use technological means to obtain otherwise unavailable information concerning activity behind the walls of a home. The constitutional inquiry does not depend on whether the instrument detects light, sound, heat, or another physical signal.

Reasoning: The home stands at the center of the Fourth Amendment’s protection. The officers did not merely perceive a condition exposed to unaided members of the public. They used an instrument to infer patterns of activity inside the residence that ordinary observation could not disclose. Treating every measurable signal leaving a house as abandoned would permit increasingly capable technology to obtain domestic information without constitutional process while formally preserving protection only against physical entry.

Limits and questions left open: Ordinary visual observation from a lawful vantage remains unaffected. The decision does not address smoke, odor, or other conditions perceptible without specialized technology; utility records obtained through lawful process; every technological measurement near private property; or surveillance that reveals no information concerning activity inside a home. The Court expresses no view on the remaining affidavit, probable cause, suppression, or *United States v. Leon*.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone holds that the security of the home protects its interior from deliberate technological acquisition of otherwise unavailable information. He confines the judgment to the preserved search question and leaves warrant sufficiency and remedy to ordinary preservation law.

#### Material Separate Writings

Stevens dissent: The device measured comparative temperatures at the exterior surface, caused no physical intrusion, and did not reveal intimate visual or auditory details. The dissent would leave any broader rule concerning future technology to a case presenting more extensive disclosure of domestic activity. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing sense-enhancing technological surveillance of a home. It does not alter *Arizona v. Evans*, which concerns the distinct exclusionary consequences of erroneous governmental warrant records.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation vacates the published Eighth Circuit judgment and rejects its treatment of escaping heat as categorically analogous to discarded garbage or odor exposed to a canine sniff. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/24/1056/499231/))

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion supplies the surveillance method, warrant-affidavit facts, preservation statement, lower-court rationale, and judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/24/1056/499231/))

### City of Edmonds v. Oxford House, Inc., 514 U.S. 725

Argued March 1, 1995 | Decided May 15, 1995

From: Edmonds limited single-family residences to any number of related persons or no more than five unrelated persons. Oxford House operated a residence for 10 to 12 adults recovering from addiction. The Ninth Circuit held that the Fair Housing Act’s exemption for reasonable maximum-occupancy restrictions did not categorically remove the City’s family definition from the Act. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/725/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The Fair Housing Act challenge may proceed. The Court makes no final determination concerning discrimination, reasonable accommodation, or ultimate relief.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia, Kennedy | Would reverse | Would treat the family definition as part of a permissible occupancy regime |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Family composition and the maximum-occupancy exemption

Holding: The Fair Housing Act’s exemption for reasonable maximum-occupancy restrictions does not cover a zoning rule that permits an unlimited number of related occupants while imposing a numerical limit on unrelated occupants.

Operative rule: A maximum-occupancy restriction covered by 42 U.S.C. § 3607(b)(1) limits the number of persons who may occupy a dwelling to address crowding, safety, or comparable conditions. A rule whose operation turns on the occupants’ legal or familial relationships rather than a uniform numerical ceiling remains subject to the Act’s ordinary provisions.

Reasoning: Edmonds’s rule allows any number of persons related by genetics, adoption, or marriage, but no more than five unrelated persons. Its operative distinction therefore regulates family composition rather than the maximum number of human beings who may inhabit the structure. The zoning label assigned by the City cannot enlarge the statutory exemption.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not invalidate single-family zoning, establish that Edmonds violated the Fair Housing Act, decide Oxford House’s accommodation claim, or prevent evenhanded occupancy limits genuinely directed to health and safety.

Legal basis: Fair Housing Act, 42 U.S.C. §§ 3604(f) and 3607(b)(1).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between a uniform crowding limit and a family-composition rule and announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This is the first controlling simulated construction of the Fair Housing Act’s maximum-occupancy exemption. The decision leaves the Act’s substantive discrimination and reasonable-accommodation requirements for further proceedings.

#### Historical Comparison

The simulated 6-3 judgment, controlling rule, authorship, and associate-Justice alignments match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Justice Ginsburg’s majority. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/725/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the ordinance, lower-court posture, dates, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/725/))

### Day v. Holahan, 34 F.3d 1356

Argued March 27, 1995 | Decided May 15, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

Minnesota increased a participating candidate’s expenditure ceiling by the amount of a qualifying independent expenditure opposing that candidate or supporting the candidate’s opponent. It also made an additional public subsidy equal to one-half of the independent expenditure available to an eligible participating candidate. The Eighth Circuit invalidated the mechanism because it concluded that the prospect of increasing an opponent’s resources chilled independent political spending. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/34/1356/552077/))

The granted question is confined to the responsive spending-limit and public-financing provisions. Other components of the consolidated Eighth Circuit judgment remain outside the decision.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *McIntyre* protects independent electoral advocacy against direct governmental burdens. It does not decide whether an independent speaker is burdened when protected spending produces additional lawful speech or public support for another electoral participant.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded as to the responsive-financing provisions, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The Eighth Circuit shall reinstate those provisions against the facial First Amendment theory resolved here. Every other component of its consolidated judgment remains undisturbed.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm invalidation of the trigger | Treats the automatic benefit to the opposed candidate as a governmental burden on the independent spender |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Responsive financing and the independent speaker

Holding: A public-financing system does not burden an independent expenditure merely because that expenditure triggers additional public funds or a higher voluntary spending limit for the candidate opposed, where the independent speaker remains legally free to spend and incurs no restriction, charge, delay, reporting duty, or other legal disability.

Operative rule: The First Amendment protects an independent speaker against governmental suppression of the speaker’s own advocacy. It does not provide a right to prevent another electoral participant from receiving lawful resources with which to respond, or to preserve the original expenditure’s strategic value against responsive speech.

Reasoning: Minnesota neither limits nor taxes the independent expenditure. It changes the resources available to a different candidate. The Eighth Circuit converted the political consequence of provoking an answer into a legal penalty on the original message. The Constitution does not guarantee that protected advocacy will remain unanswered or that government must ration campaign speech to preserve one speaker’s preferred electoral advantage.

Limits and questions left open: A different question would arise if a trigger reduced the independent speaker’s rights, imposed a fee or new disclosure obligation because of the message, discriminated according to identity or viewpoint, or provided support so disproportionate that the program operated as a device for suppressing disfavored advocacy.

Legal basis: First Amendment.

##### Increased limits within a voluntary public-financing program

Holding: Government may increase a participating candidate’s voluntarily accepted spending ceiling when specified electoral conditions occur, including qualifying independent expenditures.

Reasoning: A candidate may accept expenditure conditions in exchange for public financing. Relaxing that accepted ceiling enlarges rather than restricts the candidate’s capacity to speak. The increase creates no independent constitutional injury to the candidate or to a nonparticipant whose spending remains unrestricted.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide a system in which participation is practically coercive or benefits are allocated through a viewpoint-based trigger.

Legal basis: First Amendment and the enacted public-financing arrangement.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse the independent-expenditure-trigger component and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between governmental suppression of a speaker and governmental support for responsive speech by another participant. He also joins the conclusion that increasing a voluntarily accepted expenditure ceiling does not burden the independent spender.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy dissent: A law that automatically converts an independent expenditure into increased spending authority and public money for the candidate opposed predictably discourages the original advocacy. The dissent would permit general public financing but not make the amount of a candidate’s public advantage depend on a private speaker’s adverse message. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*McIntyre* remains controlling for direct restrictions on independent citizen advocacy. *Day* establishes the distinct rule that a political consequence produced through additional responsive resources is not itself a legal burden on the original speaker.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation reverses the Eighth Circuit only as to the responsive spending-limit and public-financing mechanism and leaves its unrelated campaign-finance rulings undisturbed. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/34/1356/552077/))

Research sources: The published Eighth Circuit opinion supplies the statutory trigger, subsidy formula, lower-court chilling rationale, and judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/34/1356/552077/))

### U.S. Term Limits, Inc. v. Thornton / Bryant v. Hill, 514 U.S. 779

Argued November 29, 1994 | Decided May 22, 1995

From: Arkansas Amendment 73 prevented an otherwise eligible congressional candidate’s name from appearing on the general-election ballot after service of three House terms or two Senate terms. The Arkansas Supreme Court held that the restriction added a substantive qualification to those fixed by Article I. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/779/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed in both dockets, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas.

The congressional component of Amendment 73 remains unenforceable. Arkansas retains authority to administer elections through neutral procedural rules.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in both dockets | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes the national character of congressional representation |
| Dissent | Thomas | O’Connor, Scalia | Would reverse | Would treat authority not withdrawn from the States as reserved and would uphold the ballot rule |

#### Decision of the Court

##### State-imposed qualifications for Congress

Holding: A State may not condition ordinary congressional ballot access on prior congressional service when the restriction operates as an additional substantive qualification for an otherwise constitutionally eligible candidate.

Operative rule: States may regulate the time, place, and manner of congressional elections and may enforce neutral ballot-administration requirements. They may not use ballot access to impose a personal disability based on a candidate characteristic beyond the age, citizenship, and residency qualifications fixed by the Constitution.

Reasoning: The restriction is triggered by the candidate’s prior federal service rather than by a neutral filing rule, signature requirement, deadline, or election-management concern. Its purpose and operation are to prevent voters from electing a person whom Article I makes eligible. A State cannot accomplish indirectly through the official ballot what it lacks authority to impose as a direct disqualification.

The Tenth Amendment does not reserve a power to add qualifications because the relevant offices and their constitutional qualifications arise from the federal structure established by the Constitution rather than from a preexisting state power.

Limits and questions left open: Genuine time, place, and manner rules, petition requirements, filing deadlines, anti-fraud protections, and neutral ballot-management measures remain valid when they do not add substantive qualifications for office.

Legal basis: U.S. Const. art. I, §§ 2, 3, and 4.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm in both dockets.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the exclusivity of the Qualifications Clauses, the distinction between substantive qualifications and election administration, and the conclusion that Amendment 73 cannot be sustained as a ballot rule.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy concurrence: Members of Congress form part of a national government and exercise authority deriving from the people of the United States, even though elected from particular States. State control over eligibility would impair that national constitutional relationship. The proposition is noncontrolling beyond the Court’s holding.

Thomas dissent, joined by O’Connor and Scalia: The Constitution establishes minimum qualifications but does not expressly prohibit the people of a State from adding ballot-access conditions. The dissent would treat the retained authority as reserved. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Powell v. McCormack* is applied and extended from congressional exclusion to state-imposed candidate restrictions. *Storer v. Brown* remains applicable to neutral election-administration rules that do not operate as additional qualifications.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court affirmed 5-4, with the historical Chief joining Thomas’s dissent. Stone joins Stevens’s position, producing a 6-3 judgment while preserving the other opinion alignments. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/779/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies Amendment 73’s operation, the Arkansas judgment, dates, historical result, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/779/))

### First Options of Chicago, Inc. v. Kaplan, 514 U.S. 938

Argued March 22, 1995 | Decided May 22, 1995

From: MK Investments signed the only workout document containing an arbitration clause. Manuel and Carol Kaplan did not sign that document and objected to the arbitrators’ authority over them while presenting that objection to the panel. The Third Circuit independently reviewed whether they had agreed to arbitrate arbitrability and vacated the personal award. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/938/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Mastrobuono* and *Allied-Bruce* confirm that arbitration remains grounded in actual agreement. Neither establishes that statutory coverage or participation under objection delegates arbitrability.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The award against the Kaplans personally remains vacated. The corporate award against MK Investments is unaffected.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Agreement to arbitrate arbitrability

Holding: Courts independently determine whether parties agreed to submit arbitrability to an arbitrator unless clear and unmistakable evidence establishes that delegation.

Operative rule: Ordinary state-law principles governing contract formation and consent determine whether the parties delegated arbitrability. Silence, ambiguity, or defensive presentation of a jurisdictional objection to the arbitrators does not supply the required clear agreement.

Reasoning: Arbitration is a matter of consent. A party who objects to the panel’s authority must be able to explain that objection without thereby granting the very authority disputed. The Kaplans did not sign the document containing the arbitration clause, consistently denied personal submission, and did not clearly authorize the panel to determine its own jurisdiction over them.

##### Judicial review

Holding: An appellate court applies ordinary standards when reviewing a district court’s judicial determination of arbitrability. Legal conclusions receive independent review, while underlying factual findings receive the review ordinarily governing such findings.

Limits and questions left open: A clear delegation clause may assign arbitrability to the arbitrator. When the parties validly submit a merits dispute, judicial review of the resulting merits award remains deferential under the Federal Arbitration Act.

Legal basis: Federal Arbitration Act and ordinary contract law.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the clear-and-unmistakable delegation rule, the conclusion that an objection presented to the arbitrators is not consent, and the use of ordinary appellate standards.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Mastrobuono* is applied without alteration. The agreement controls the scope of arbitral authority. *Allied-Bruce* remains limited to federal statutory coverage and does not establish assent to arbitrate any particular question.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment, rule, authorship, and opinion structure match the historical comparator. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/938/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the workout documents, objection to arbitrability, lower-court judgment, dates, and historical disposition. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/938/))

### Wilson v. Arkansas, 514 U.S. 927

Argued March 28, 1995 | Decided May 22, 1995

From: Arkansas courts rejected Wilson’s argument that the common-law requirement to announce identity and authority before forcible home entry forms part of the Fourth Amendment. Her conviction followed denial of a suppression motion challenging the officers’ unannounced entry. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/927/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Pinson* confirms the special constitutional protection of information concerning the home’s interior. It does not determine the manner in which officers may execute a valid warrant and enter the premises.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The Arkansas courts shall evaluate the entry as part of Fourth Amendment reasonableness, including any record-supported justification for proceeding without announcement and the remedy lawfully available.

Justice Thomas delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Knock and announce as a component of reasonableness

Holding: The common-law requirement that officers announce their identity and authority before forcibly entering a home forms part of the Fourth Amendment’s reasonableness inquiry.

Operative rule: Announcement is ordinarily required before forcible residential entry. It may be excused when case-specific circumstances give officers adequate reason to believe that announcement would create danger, be futile, permit escape, or lead to destruction of evidence.

Reasoning: The Fourth Amendment was adopted against a common-law tradition that treated unannounced forcible entry as relevant to the lawfulness of a search. Announcement protects life, property, privacy, and the orderly execution of warrants. Arkansas therefore erred by treating the principle as constitutionally irrelevant.

Limits and questions left open: The requirement is not absolute. The Court does not fix the exact quantum of suspicion necessary for an exception, adopt a categorical offense-based exception, determine whether the officers possessed an adequate justification here, or decide whether suppression follows from every violation.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins incorporation of the common-law announcement principle into Fourth Amendment reasonableness and leaves exceptions and remedy for record-based adjudication.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing announcement before forcible home entry. *Pinson* remains confined to technological acquisition of information concerning the home’s interior.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment, opinion by Thomas, and governing rule match the historical comparator. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/927/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the suppression posture, Arkansas judgment, dates, historical holding, and unanimous opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/514/927/))

### Kelley v. Board of Trustees of the University of Illinois, 35 F.3d 265

Argued March 28, 1995 | Decided May 22, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

The University eliminated several athletic programs while addressing a substantial budget deficit, including men’s swimming, but retained women’s swimming. Women constituted approximately 44 percent of the undergraduate population and 23.4 percent of intercollegiate athletes. The former men’s swimmers alleged that eliminating their team violated Title IX and that preserving the women’s team was unconstitutional sex-conscious state action. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/35/265/605037/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: No completed simulated holding governs program-wide athletic participation under Title IX. *J.E.B.* confirms that purposeful sex classifications are subject to equal-protection review and may not rest on stereotype, but it supplies no athletics-specific rule.

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Title IX claim | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The judgment rejecting a statutory right to continuation of men’s swimming remains in force. |
| Equal-protection claim | Affirmed, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Thomas | The judgment sustaining the University’s restructuring remains in force. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia and Thomas as to the Title IX proposition | Supports both affirmances for seven Justices and the Title IX affirmance for all nine | Complete opinion for the seven-Justice coalition; Title IX holding for Scalia and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Supports the Title IX affirmance; opposes the equal-protection affirmance | Would require a more specific remedial justification for the sex-conscious decision |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Title IX participation opportunities

Holding: Title IX does not entitle athletes to preservation of a particular intercollegiate team. Participation opportunities and accommodation of interests and abilities are evaluated across the athletic program rather than through sport-for-sport symmetry.

Operative rule: Substantial proportionality may demonstrate effective accommodation, but it is neither a rigid quota nor the exclusive route to compliance. An institution may instead establish a continuing practice of expanding opportunities for the underrepresented sex or effective accommodation of that sex’s interests and abilities.

Reasoning: The elimination of men’s swimming concretely injured its athletes, but the statute does not transform every team reduction into sex discrimination. Title IX regulates equal athletic opportunity across the program. It neither requires a male and female counterpart in every sport nor commands preservation of every existing men’s team.

Limits and questions left open: Participation opportunities cannot offset discrimination in equipment, facilities, coaching, scheduling, scholarships, or another separately regulated component. The decision does not require reduction of men’s opportunities or make proportionality the only lawful means of compliance.

Legal basis: Title IX, 20 U.S.C. § 1681, and 34 C.F.R. § 106.41.

Authority of the proposition: All nine Justices support the Title IX disposition and the stated program-wide rule.

##### Equal protection and sex-conscious restructuring

Holding: A public university may consider sex when restructuring a financially constrained athletic program to avoid perpetuating a demonstrated inequality in participation opportunities, when the choice is substantially related to that remedial objective and does not rest on stereotype or pretext.

Operative rule: Correcting a demonstrated inequality in athletic opportunity is an important governmental objective. The university must establish a meaningful disparity, a genuine fiscal or programmatic restructuring, and a substantial relationship between the sex-conscious choice and preservation or correction of the underrepresented sex’s opportunities. Numerical imbalance alone is insufficient.

Reasoning: The record showed a marked and continuing disparity in women’s participation and an actual departmental deficit requiring program reductions. The University eliminated several programs rather than categorically excluding men from athletics. Preserving women’s swimming avoided further contraction of the already underrepresented sex’s participation opportunity and did not rely on assumptions concerning the athletic abilities or preferences of men or women.

Limits and questions left open: The principle applies symmetrically to whichever sex is demonstrably underrepresented. It does not decide classifications involving sexual orientation, gender identity, transgender status, or eligibility for an individual sex-designated team.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Equal Protection Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Justice O’Connor’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing seven votes.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm both components of the Seventh Circuit’s judgment.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the program-wide Title IX analysis, rejects any right to continuation of a particular team, and concludes that the demonstrated participation disparity and genuine fiscal restructuring supply an adequate, substantially related remedial justification.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia’s partial dissent, joined by Thomas: Title IX does not guarantee a specific team, but the Equal Protection Clause requires more than numerical imbalance and a desire to comply with federal administrative guidance before a public university may preserve one team expressly because of sex while eliminating the other. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*J.E.B.* is applied without alteration to the prohibition against sex stereotypes. *Kelley* supplies the first controlling simulated law concerning program-wide Title IX athletic participation and sex-conscious restructuring during a genuine fiscal contraction.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation affirms the published Seventh Circuit judgment while separating the unanimous statutory disposition from the 7-2 constitutional disposition and stating narrower limits on the remedial use of sex. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/35/265/605037/))

Research sources: The published Seventh Circuit opinion supplies the participation figures, budget circumstances, Title IX framework, equal-protection analysis, and lower-court judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/35/265/605037/))

### Garlotte v. Fordice, 515 U.S. 39

Argued April 24, 1995 | Decided May 30, 1995

From: Garlotte received a three-year marijuana sentence followed by concurrent life sentences for murder. Although the first sentence had formally been served, its placement at the beginning of the consecutive sequence postponed the start and completion of the remaining sentences. The Fifth Circuit dismissed his federal habeas challenge to the marijuana conviction for lack of custody. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/39/))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Thomas.

The habeas petition may proceed. The lower courts shall address any remaining procedural and merits questions.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | No other Justice | Would affirm | Would apply the completed-sentence limit separately to the marijuana conviction |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Custody under consecutive sentences

Holding: A prisoner serving consecutive sentences remains in custody under the conviction supporting an earlier sentence already served in sequence when invalidating that conviction would advance the date of release from the aggregate term.

Operative rule: Consecutive sentences are treated as a continuous stream of custody for purposes of 28 U.S.C. § 2254. Formal completion of one segment does not defeat jurisdiction while that conviction continues to postpone release from the undischarged aggregate confinement.

Reasoning: *Peyton v. Rowe* rejected sentence-by-sentence formalism for a prisoner challenging a consecutive sentence not yet begun. The same aggregate principle governs a first sentence whose formal term has expired but whose validity continues to determine when the remaining imprisonment ends. Vacating the marijuana conviction would advance Garlotte’s parole and release timetable, making the present custody concrete and redressable.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not revive a challenge after all custody has fully ended when the petitioner relies solely on collateral consequences insufficient under otherwise governing law.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 2254.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins treatment of consecutive sentences as continuous aggregate custody and the conclusion that success on the marijuana conviction would shorten Garlotte’s present imprisonment.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Heck v. Humphrey* remains limited to channeling conviction-invalidating damages claims into habeas while that remedy is available. *Garlotte* supplies the separate rule identifying custody when consecutive sentences form one continuing period of incarceration.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed 7-2, with the historical Chief joining Thomas’s dissent. Stone joins Ginsburg’s position, producing an 8-1 judgment and leaving Thomas as the sole dissenter. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/39/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the sentence sequence, custody issue, lower-court disposition, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/39/))

### United States v. Wellons, 32 F.3d 117

Argued March 29, 1995 | Decided May 30, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

Wellons drove a Hertz rental car with the renter’s permission but was not listed as an authorized driver. After Hertz requested impoundment, a narcotics dog alerted and officers searched the car and its luggage, finding cocaine and heroin. The Fourth Circuit held that omission from the rental agreement eliminated any legitimate privacy interest in the vehicle and, derivatively, in every bag inside it. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/32/117/633420/))

The Court addresses the categorical privacy rulings but does not make new findings concerning the duration of permission, repossession, lawful impoundment, the dog sniff, ownership of particular bags, probable cause, or inventory procedures.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Pinson* and *Wilson* concern the special protection of a home and do not determine privacy in a rental vehicle or closed luggage.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The lower courts shall reconsider Wellons’s personal Fourth Amendment interests without treating omission from the rental agreement as categorically dispositive and shall determine any preserved warrant exception before ordering suppression.

Justice Kennedy delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Privacy in a rental vehicle

Holding: A driver’s absence from a rental agreement does not by itself defeat a legitimate expectation of privacy when the driver possesses and controls the vehicle with the authorized renter’s permission and without theft, fraud, or comparable wrongful possession.

Operative rule: The inquiry considers the defendant’s actual relationship to the vehicle, including permission, possession of the keys, practical control, ability to exclude strangers, the owner’s retained rights, and whether possession was obtained or continued wrongfully. A private contractual driver restriction is relevant but not conclusive.

Reasoning: The Fourth Circuit converted one contractual term into a universal constitutional forfeiture. Fourth Amendment protection turns on the individual’s own legitimate relationship to the place searched. Permission and practical control can support that relationship even when use breaches a private rental condition. Conversely, effective repossession, theft, fraud, or another wrongful basis for possession may defeat it.

Limits and questions left open: Permission from the renter does not automatically establish privacy under every circumstance. The Court does not protect a thief, carjacker, fraudulent renter, or person continuing possession after an effective repossession.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

##### Independent privacy in closed luggage

Holding: A person’s lack of a legitimate expectation of privacy in a vehicle does not by itself eliminate a legitimate expectation of privacy in that person’s closed luggage found within the vehicle.

Operative rule: Privacy in a container depends on ownership, possession, control, and steps taken to preserve its contents as private. The Government may nevertheless search the container when the automobile exception, consent, a valid inventory, or another lawful authority reaches it.

Reasoning: Privacy interests in a place and in a closed container do not merge. A person may lack lawful control of the surrounding vehicle while retaining a personal privacy interest in a bag. That interest permits a challenge to the legal authority for opening the container but does not determine whether a warrant exception applies.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide ownership of each bag, disclaimer, abandonment, the scope of probable cause, or the validity of any inventory procedure.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

##### Warrant exceptions and remedy

Holding: When a lower court rejects a Fourth Amendment claim solely under an erroneous categorical privacy rule, preserved warrant exceptions and the resulting suppression question ordinarily remain for remand.

Reasoning: Hertz’s request, the lawful basis and timing of impoundment, the dog alert, the scope of probable cause, consent, container ownership, and standardized inventory procedures may independently determine admissibility. This Court does not convert an incomplete appellate record into trial findings.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the individualized inquiry into permission and control, the separate treatment of closed luggage, and the remand preserving every properly presented warrant exception.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule concerning an unlisted rental-car driver and confirms that a privacy determination concerning a vehicle does not categorically determine privacy in every container within it. The ordinary *Rakas* inquiry remains individualized.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The simulation vacates the Fourth Circuit’s judgment and rejects both its agreement-based categorical rule and its derivative rule extinguishing privacy in personal luggage. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/32/117/633420/))

Research sources: The published Fourth Circuit opinion supplies the rental arrangement, Hertz impound request, dog alert, searches, categorical privacy rulings, and historical judgment. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/32/117/633420/))

### Missouri v. Jenkins, 515 U.S. 70

Argued January 11, 1995 | Decided June 12, 1995, immediately before *Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*

From: In long-running Kansas City school-desegregation litigation, the challenged orders required Missouri to fund broad salary increases and continued quality-education programs. The lower courts justified salary measures in part through the goal of increasing the district’s desegregative attractiveness and continued educational measures in part through achievement levels below national norms, while declining component-specific withdrawal of federal control. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/70/))

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall reconsider each challenged salary, quality-education, and remedial component under the violation-remedy nexus stated by the Court. It shall relinquish control over any separable component no longer necessary to eliminate a proven vestige of the constitutional violation to the extent practicable.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Emphasizes the district court’s continuing authority over components still necessary to remedy proven vestiges |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Would impose broader historical limits on federal remedial supervision |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Concludes that the lower courts acted within equitable authority over the intradistrict violation |
| Additional dissent | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Would affirm | Emphasizes the practical connection between educational conditions and dismantling the formerly segregated system |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Violation-remedy nexus and interdistrict attraction

Holding: A school-desegregation remedy may continue only insofar as it remains reasonably directed to eliminating an identified vestige of the constitutional violation to the extent practicable. An intradistrict violation does not authorize interdistrict attraction or reversal of demographic movement as an independent constitutional objective.

Operative rule: For each challenged component, the district court must identify the constitutional violation or vestige addressed, determine whether the measure remains reasonably connected to eliminating it, and assess whether continued federal control is still necessary. Federal remedial authority is corrective rather than a general mandate to optimize a school system.

Reasoning: Salary measures may be lawful when genuinely necessary to cure a proven intradistrict vestige, but they cannot be sustained solely as a means to make the district attractive to suburban students or to reverse white flight not legally attributable to an interdistrict violation. That objective introduces demographic and policy considerations too remote from the judicial task of dismantling the identified dual system.

Application: The lower courts relied materially on desegregative attractiveness without adequately identifying which intradistrict vestige each salary component still remedied. Component-specific reconsideration is therefore required.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not categorically forbid salary measures, educational improvements, or other relief genuinely necessary to eliminate a proven vestige. It does not require premature termination or impose a fixed administrative checklist.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment and federal equitable remedial authority.

##### Achievement benchmarks and incremental withdrawal of control

Holding: National achievement levels are not freestanding constitutional benchmarks for determining whether desegregation remedies must continue. Federal control must be withdrawn incrementally from separable areas in which compliance is complete and the relevant vestige has been eliminated to the extent practicable.

Operative rule: Academic performance may be relevant evidence when prior de jure segregation caused an identified educational deficit, but the constitutional question is whether that attributable vestige has been remedied to the practicable extent, not whether students have reached national averages or the district’s maximum potential.

Reasoning: The Equal Protection Clause requires eradication of the consequences of state-imposed segregation. It does not authorize indefinite federal management until a district attains a nationally defined level of educational performance. *Freeman* requires courts to recognize partial unitary status and relinquish control over completed components while retaining authority over areas in which legally attributable vestiges remain.

Application: The district court made no adequate component-specific findings establishing why continued quality-education control remained necessary under the governing violation-remedy standard. Remand permits those findings without presuming either immediate termination or indefinite continuation.

Limits and questions left open: Test scores and other educational evidence remain relevant when tied to a proven vestige. The Court does not determine on the present record which particular programs must end or continue.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone holds that federal remedial power must remain tied to the constitutional violation, rejects interdistrict attraction and national achievement as independent ends, and requires incremental withdrawal of control from completed components without terminating measures still necessary to cure proven vestiges.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor concurrence: The remand does not presume that salary or educational programs are unlawful. A measure may continue if the district court makes adequate findings connecting it to an unremedied vestige and explains why continued control remains necessary.

Thomas concurrence: Federal courts should apply still stricter limits to structural injunctions and should not treat unequal outcomes or present racial identifiability as sufficient proof of continuing constitutional injury. The proposition is noncontrolling.

Souter dissent, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: The record and history of the litigation adequately connect the educational and salary measures to dismantling the intradistrict system, and the majority understates the district court’s equitable discretion. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Milliken v. Bradley* is clarified. Its violation-remedy requirement applies throughout the life of a structural decree rather than only when relief is first imposed. *Freeman v. Pitts* is applied to require component-specific consideration of partial unitary status and incremental withdrawal of federal supervision. The decision does not narrow the authority to impose effective initial relief against an established constitutional violation.

#### Historical Comparison

The 5-4 judgment division and associate-Justice alignments match the historical comparator. The historical Court reversed the Eighth Circuit. The simulation instead vacates and remands under Stone’s narrower component-specific formulation, preserving the possibility that individual salary or educational measures may continue upon adequate findings. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/70/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the salary and quality-education orders, lower-court rationales, historical judgment, dates, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/70/))
