## Section II: Entering law and Stone's approved position

### A. Law entering the case

* **Current Through:** Immediately before the June 5, 1995 simulated merits event, including all completed OT1993 simulated holdings and all earlier effective OT1994 decisions material to this case.
* **Earlier Same-Term Decisions:** *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund*, released December 6, 1994, dismissed the petition for want of jurisdiction. The Court did not reach the separation-of-powers challenge to the nonvoting congressional officers, severability, or ratification. *FEC* therefore supplies no controlling same-term merits rule concerning congressional participation in execution and no controlling severability rule for this case.
* **Unresolved Same-Term Dependencies:** None identified.

#### Issue 1: Congressional control over execution

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No completed simulated holding specifically resolves the revised Airports Authority Board arrangement. *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund* supplies no merits rule because the Court dismissed that matter on the jurisdictional threshold without reaching separation of powers.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No registered reusable doctrine specifically addresses the revised Airports Authority Board.
* **Other Governing Law:** U.S. Const. arts. I and II; *Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Citizens for the Abatement of Aircraft Noise, Inc.*; *Buckley v. Valeo*; *Bowsher v. Synar*.
* **Other Authority Used:** The prior Airports Authority decision, *Buckley*, and *Bowsher* establish that Congress and its agents may not exercise legally operative authority over execution outside the constitutional legislative process. Those authorities do not prohibit communication, consultation, observation, persuasion, reporting, or oversight merely because congressional actors may thereby influence another decisionmaker.
* **Open Point:** Existing authority requires application of the separation-of-powers boundary to the revised Board, which differs formally from the previously invalidated Board but continues to possess statutory authority capable of affecting whether identified Authority decisions may proceed.

#### Issue 2: Delay mechanism, bicameralism, and presentment

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No completed simulated holding specifically governs the revised report-and-delay mechanism.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No registered reusable doctrine directly resolves this issue.
* **Other Governing Law:** U.S. Const. art. I, § 7; *INS v. Chadha*; the prior Airports Authority decision.
* **Other Authority Used:** Congress may require reports, provide a fixed period before executive action becomes effective, debate the reported action, and enact responsive legislation. It may not make the legal effectiveness of execution depend on congressional approval, disapproval, suspension, or other legally operative intervention outside bicameralism and presentment.
* **Open Point:** The constitutional question is whether the revised mechanism merely creates time for ordinary congressional consideration or instead gives Congress or its agents legally operative control over execution during that period.

#### Issue 3: Severability and continuity of airport operations

* **Controlling Simulated Law:** No completed same-term simulated holding specifically resolves severability of the revised Board provisions. *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund* supplies no severability holding because the Court did not reach the merits.
* **Current Reusable Doctrine:** No registered reusable doctrine directly resolves this issue.
* **Other Governing Law:** Federal severability doctrine; the governing transfer statute, lease, and interstate compact; ordinary remedial principles.
* **Other Authority Used:** The unconstitutional control mechanism should be removed without invalidating otherwise lawful airport administration when the remaining Authority can continue operating consistently with the enacted transfer and compact structure.

### B. Stone's approved position

* **Stone's Controlled Judgment or Disposition:** **AFFIRM the judgment of the D.C. Circuit.**
* **Stone's Remedy and Remand Instruction:** The revised Board-of-Review provisions conferring constitutionally impermissible legal control over Authority decisions are unenforceable and shall be severed to the extent permitted by the governing statutory scheme. The Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority may continue ordinary airport operations under the remaining valid lease, compact, and statutory provisions. Any transaction-specific reliance, finality, or remedial issue not necessary to the facial judgment may be addressed in further proceedings without restoring the unconstitutional control mechanism.

#### Issue 1: Congressional control over execution

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches the separation-of-powers question because the challenged Board's composition and statutory authority present a live structural dispute. The prior dismissal in *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund* does not resolve this question and supplies no controlling merits rule.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** The constitutional distinction is between influence and legally operative power. Congress and its Members may receive information, conduct oversight, express views, advise, persuade, and legislate in response to executive or operational decisions. The Constitution does not require the political branches to operate without communication or influence. But Congress may not retain legal control over execution by placing that power in itself or in agents whose authority derives from their relationship to Congress. A vote is not the only form of legal control. Statutory authority to suspend, delay, approve, disapprove, alter, or condition the implementation of an operational decision can exercise the same constitutional power.

  The revised Board crosses that line because its role is not confined to observation or advice. The governing arrangement gives the Board legally operative authority to delay or otherwise control concrete Authority decisions while congressional review occurs. That legal consequence distinguishes the Board from a congressional observer who can speak but cannot bind, suspend, or alter the action of the executive or operational body.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** The separation of powers does not prohibit congressional officers or agents from receiving information, advising, observing, or attempting to persuade executive or operational decisionmakers merely because those activities may influence governmental action. Congress violates the constitutional allocation of power, however, when it gives itself or its agents legally operative authority to approve, disapprove, suspend, delay, alter, or condition the execution of federal policy outside constitutionally prescribed legislative action. Because the revised Board possesses legally operative control over Authority decisions rather than merely an advisory role, the challenged arrangement is unconstitutional.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** *Buckley*, *Bowsher*, and the prior Airports Authority decision concern the exercise of governmental power, not insulation of one branch from the political influence of another. The constitutional inquiry therefore does not stop at the identity of the Board's members or at whether they cast a formal vote. It asks what legal consequence their authority produces.

  Here, the Board's statutory role reaches the implementation of particular airport decisions. Its intervention can legally delay or prevent an Authority decision from proceeding according to the ordinary course of the transferred operational authority. That is materially different from a report to Congress, testimony before a committee, a recommendation from Members, or political pressure that leaves the lawful decisionmaker free to act. Congress may establish standards for the airports, require compliance with the lease, appropriate funds, conduct oversight, demand reports, and enact new legislation. It may not remain legally inside the execution of individual Authority decisions after transferring operational responsibility.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not hold that congressional membership, congressional affiliation, consultation, advice, observation, reporting, or political influence is itself unconstitutional. Nor does he invalidate the interstate compact or Congress's authority to regulate the airports through enacted law. The defect is the possession of legally operative control over execution by Congress or its agents.

#### Issue 2: Delay mechanism, bicameralism, and presentment

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches this issue independently because the challenged mechanism raises a distinct Article I question even apart from the Board's institutional identity.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** Congress may lawfully require an executive or operational body to report a proposed action before it takes effect. Congress may also create a fixed waiting period that expires automatically under the enacted statute, during which Members may investigate, debate, negotiate, or enact new legislation. Those mechanisms leave the legal rule fixed by the statute already enacted.

  The constitutional line is crossed when congressional action or the action of a congressional agent has legal force to determine whether the underlying executive or operational decision may become effective. Approval, disapproval, suspension, extension of the statutory delay, or another legally operative intervention changes the legal consequences of the existing law and therefore must occur through the constitutionally prescribed legislative process.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** Congress may require advance reporting and a fixed review period that leaves an operational decision legally effective according to previously enacted law when the period expires. It may not make that decision legally contingent on congressional approval, disapproval, suspension, or other control exercised outside bicameralism and presentment.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** *Chadha* prevents Congress from retaining legislative control after enactment through a procedure that gives legally operative effect to action falling short of passage by both Houses and presentment to the President. The revised Airports Authority arrangement cannot escape that rule merely by describing the intervention as review or delay.

  A waiting period itself presents no constitutional problem when Congress has enacted the duration in advance and the Authority's decision becomes effective automatically when the statutory condition is satisfied. The defect here is the additional legal control supplied through the Board and congressional review mechanism. To the extent that mechanism allows Congress or its agents to hold, suspend, approve, disapprove, or otherwise determine whether an Authority decision may proceed, it performs legislative work without legislation.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not invalidate a purely informational reporting requirement, congressional hearings concerning a reported action, ordinary political pressure, or a fixed statutory waiting period that ends automatically and gives no congressional actor legal authority to alter the result. Congress remains free during such a period to enact a new law through bicameralism and presentment.

#### Issue 3: Severability and continuity of airport operations

* **Threshold and Merits Reach:** Stone reaches severability because identifying the constitutional defect does not itself determine whether the entire airport-transfer arrangement must fall.

* **Stone's Legal Position:** The unconstitutional feature is the mechanism for congressional legal control, not the existence of the Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority, the interstate compact, the federal lease, or the transfer of ordinary airport administration. The Authority remains capable of carrying out those functions without the Board's unconstitutional control. The remedy should therefore remove the offending mechanism rather than dismantle an otherwise functioning governmental arrangement.

* **Stone's Proposed Holding:** The provisions granting the revised Board constitutionally impermissible control over Authority decisions are severable from the remaining legal framework when the Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority can continue operating under the valid transfer statute, lease, and compact without that control.

* **Stone's Reasoning and Record Application:** The constitutional injury is cured when Congress and its agents cease possessing the legally operative power that the Constitution requires Congress to exercise through legislation. Invalidating the entire transfer, undoing the interstate compact, or disabling ordinary airport operations would go beyond that defect. The remaining arrangement can continue to administer the airports under enacted standards without giving Congress an extra-statutory operational veto.

  *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund* supplies no severability rule because that case ended at jurisdiction. Severability here therefore rests independently on the governing statutory structure and ordinary remedial principles.

* **Boundary or Reserved Question:** Stone does not validate a particular past action whose only legal authorization depended on the unconstitutional Board, and he does not decide transaction-specific reliance, finality, or retrospective-remedy questions unnecessary to the facial judgment.

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority v. Hechinger, 36 F.3d 97

Argued April 17, 1995 | Decided June 5, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

After the original Airports Authority Board of Review was invalidated, Congress removed its formal veto and mandatory congressional-membership requirement. The revised statute still required appointments from lists supplied by congressional leaders, allowed Board members to participate without voting in Authority meetings, permitted recommendations, and empowered the Board to determine whether specified Authority actions could take effect promptly or would enter a 60-legislative-day congressional review period. The D.C. Circuit held that the revised arrangement continued to give a congressional agent constitutionally impermissible power. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/36/97/563170/))

The questions are whether advisory participation or political influence alone violates separation of powers, whether the Board’s statutory delay authority supplies legally operative control, whether the congressional-review mechanism complies with bicameralism and presentment, and what remains after invalidation.

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund* supplies no merits rule because that case ended at the jurisdictional threshold. *Buckley*, *Bowsher*, *Chadha*, and the prior Airports Authority decision govern.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

##### Judgment, Mandate, and Remand

The revised Board-of-Review provisions granting legally operative control over Authority decisions, together with any dependent provision that would disable airport operations because the unconstitutional Board cannot act, are unenforceable and severed.

The Metropolitan Washington Airports Authority may continue ordinary airport administration under the remaining transfer statute, lease, compact, and valid operational provisions. No prior transaction is invalidated automatically. Transaction-specific reliance, finality, or remedial questions not necessary to this facial judgment remain for ordinary adjudication.

Justice Stevens delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Congressional influence and legally operative control

Holding: Congressional officers or agents do not violate separation of powers merely by receiving information, attending meetings, advising, observing, reporting, or attempting to persuade an executive or operational decisionmaker. Congress crosses the constitutional line when it gives itself or its agents legally operative authority to approve, disapprove, suspend, delay, alter, or condition execution outside the constitutional legislative process.

Operative rule: The inquiry turns on the legal consequence of the asserted authority, not solely on the identity of the participant, the presence or absence of a formal vote, or the practical likelihood that political influence will be effective.

Reasoning: Political communication among the branches is an ordinary part of constitutional government. Oversight, appropriations, hearings, reports, recommendations, and persuasion leave the lawful decisionmaker with authority to act under existing law. The revised Board possesses something different. It can determine whether specified Authority decisions become effective promptly or are subjected to a prolonged congressional-review period. That statutory consequence gives the Board legal leverage over execution, not merely a platform from which to express views.

Application: The Board’s ability to select between immediate effectiveness and a 60-legislative-day delay materially controls the implementation of contracts, budgets, bonds, designs, and other operational decisions. Its members’ nonvoting participation and recommendations would not alone establish a violation, but the delay authority does.

Limits and questions left open: Congressional affiliation, consultation, advice, observation, political pressure, and ordinary oversight are not independently unconstitutional. Congress may regulate the airports through enacted law.

Legal basis: Articles I and II and separation of powers.

##### Report-and-wait mechanisms and bicameralism

Holding: Congress may require advance reporting and may enact a fixed waiting period that expires automatically. It may not make the legal effectiveness of an operational decision depend on approval, disapproval, suspension, extension, or another intervention by Congress or its agent outside bicameralism and presentment.

Reasoning: A fixed statutory period leaves the legal rule established by the law already enacted. During that period Congress may investigate and enact a new law. The revised Board instead has discretion to determine whether a particular Authority action must undergo additional congressional review. That discretionary legal intervention performs legislative work without passage by both Houses and presentment to the President.

Limits and questions left open: Purely informational reports, hearings, recommendations, and an automatic waiting period remain permissible.

Legal basis: Article I, § 7.

##### Severability and continuity of airport operations

Holding: The unconstitutional control mechanism is severable from the remaining legal framework governing the Airports Authority.

Reasoning: The constitutional defect lies in Congress’s retention of operational control, not in the transfer of airport administration, the interstate compact, the lease, or the Authority’s existence. The Authority can administer the airports under enacted standards without the Board. Enforcing a dependent disabling provision would recreate the unconstitutional control by making ordinary airport operations contingent on maintenance of the invalid mechanism.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not validate a transaction whose only legal authorization depended on the Board or decide a retrospective claim not necessary to the facial judgment.

Legal basis: Federal severability doctrine and ordinary remedial principles.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between political influence and legal control, the invalidation of the discretionary delay mechanism, and severance preserving ordinary airport operations.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

The prior Airports Authority decision is clarified. Congressional influence is not itself execution of law, but a congressional agent may not possess legal authority over whether execution proceeds.

*Chadha* is applied to a discretionary congressional-control mechanism rather than extended to a purely automatic report-and-wait provision. *Federal Election Commission v. NRA Political Victory Fund* remains solely a jurisdictional decision and supplies no merits or severability holding.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The D.C. Circuit affirmed invalidation of the revised Board based on the combined effects of congressional agency, influence, and delay authority. The simulation affirms on the narrower ground that advisory influence is permissible but legally operative control over implementation is not, and it expressly preserves continued airport operations through severability. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/36/97/563170/))

Research sources: The published D.C. Circuit opinion supplies the revised statutory structure, appointment process, delay mechanism, judgment, and treatment of past acts. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/36/97/563170/))

### Ryder v. United States, 515 U.S. 177

Argued April 18, 1995 | Decided June 12, 1995

From: Two civilian judges on the Coast Guard military appellate panel that reviewed Ryder’s court-martial had been appointed by the Department of Transportation’s General Counsel rather than by the President, a court of law, or the head of a department. Ryder raised and preserved his Appointments Clause objection throughout direct review, but the court below applied the de facto officer doctrine and affirmed. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/177/))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Ryder is entitled to a new direct appellate review before a lawfully appointed military panel. His underlying conviction is not automatically dismissed.

Justice O’Connor delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Timely Appointments Clause challenge

Holding: A litigant who timely challenges the unconstitutional appointment of officers adjudicating his own case is entitled to a fresh adjudication by lawfully appointed officers. The de facto officer doctrine does not validate the defective panel’s judgment against that litigant.

Operative rule: The de facto officer doctrine may protect institutional continuity and settled acts in appropriate circumstances, but it does not defeat relief for a timely, preserved constitutional challenge to the source of an adjudicator’s authority.

Reasoning: The defect is not a technical mistake in assigning a lawfully appointed judge. The civilian judges were inferior officers whose appointments did not follow any constitutionally authorized route. Ryder objected before the adjudication became final and pursued the objection through the available review system. Denying relief would remove the practical incentive to raise timely structural challenges.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not reopen final judgments in which no timely objection was made, invalidate routine ministerial acts, or prescribe relief for a challenge raised only after the ordinary review process ended.

Legal basis: Appointments Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between a constitutional appointment defect and a technical assignment error, as well as the requirement of a new lawful appellate adjudication for the timely challenger.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hechinger* is applied only for the general distinction between political or institutional influence and the legal exercise of governmental authority. Ryder supplies the first controlling simulated rule concerning the remedial consequence of a timely Appointments Clause challenge to the adjudicators in the challenger’s own case.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment and controlling rule match the historical comparator. The historical Chief Justice authored the opinion; Justice O’Connor authors the simulated unanimous opinion. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/177/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the appointments, preservation history, lower-court reliance on the de facto officer doctrine, dates, and historical result. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/177/))

### Johnson v. Jones, 515 U.S. 304

Argued April 18, 1995 | Decided June 12, 1995

From: Three officers sought an immediate appeal from the denial of qualified-immunity summary judgment. Their contention was not that the conduct alleged by Jones would be constitutionally permissible, but that the pretrial evidence was insufficient to show that they had participated in or been present during the alleged beatings. The Seventh Circuit dismissed the appeal. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/304/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The factual dispute may proceed to trial. The officers may obtain review of preserved legal immunity questions after final judgment or through another independently authorized route.

Justice Breyer delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Evidence-sufficiency disputes in qualified-immunity appeals

Holding: A denial of qualified immunity is not immediately appealable insofar as it determines only that the pretrial record contains sufficient evidence to create a genuine factual dispute.

Operative rule: An interlocutory qualified-immunity appeal may present the legal question whether conduct, taken under an assumed or properly supported version of the facts, violated clearly established law. It does not authorize appellate reconsideration of whose account the evidence supports.

Reasoning: Immediate review protects the legal entitlement not to stand trial when the assumed conduct was not clearly unlawful. Reviewing evidentiary sufficiency would require record-intensive appellate jurisdiction over ordinary summary-judgment disputes, fragment trials, and duplicate review available after final judgment.

Limits and questions left open: Immediate review remains available for a separable legal immunity question. Section 1292(b), mandamus, and final appellate review remain governed by their own requirements.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 1291 and the collateral-order doctrine.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between the immediately reviewable legal immunity question and the nonappealable dispute over evidentiary sufficiency.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Digital Equipment* and the same-term decision in *Swint* are clarified. A genuine immunity may support immediate review, but that review does not absorb separable nonfinal issues or ordinary disputes over what the evidence could prove.

*Elder v. Holloway* remains controlling on the complete body of law relevant to the legal clearly-established-law inquiry. It does not make factual sufficiency immediately appealable.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment, rule, and Breyer authorship match the historical comparator. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/304/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the factual dispute, interlocutory posture, lower-court judgment, dates, and historical disposition. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/304/))

### Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña, 515 U.S. 200

Argued January 17, 1995 | Decided June 12, 1995, after *Missouri v. Jenkins*

From: A federal subcontractor-compensation clause gave a prime contractor a financial incentive to hire a certified small disadvantaged business. The record did not establish which certification route the successful subcontractor used, how the relevant racial presumption operated, whether it was rebutted or rebuttable in practice, or whether race determined the subcontract award. The Tenth Circuit affirmed summary judgment under a standard resembling intermediate scrutiny. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/200/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Missouri v. Jenkins*, released earlier the same day, requires a governmental remedy to remain tied to an identified violation or vestige rather than becoming an independent policy project. It does not determine the equality tier here.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The lower courts shall determine how race operated in the certification and subcontracting process, including rebuttability, decisional weight, the identified remedial predicate, and race-neutral alternatives. They shall then apply the controlling proposition appropriate to the measure’s actual operation. No final judgment concerning the program’s constitutionality is entered.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment and Opinion of the Court in part | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to strict scrutiny for a race-determinative allocation and the remand; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to intermediate scrutiny for a genuinely nonexclusive access measure | Supports vacatur for the judgment coalition | No complete rationale commands five votes |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports vacatur and remand | Would apply strict scrutiny to every federal racial classification |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Joins the nonexclusive-access proposition but rejects the strict-scrutiny extension to federal remedial action and opposes vacatur |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Nonexclusive federal access measures

Holding: A genuinely remedial federal measure that expands access without making race decisive for a discrete contract, payment, or comparable benefit may be reviewed for an important remedial objective and a substantial relationship between the measure and that objective.

Operative rule: The measure must address identified exclusion rather than generalized societal discrimination, racial balance for its own sake, or permanent racial administration. Formal labels and nominal percentages do not control; the measure’s actual legal operation does.

Reasoning: Congress retains authority to dismantle identified barriers to participation. A nonexclusive measure that enlarges opportunity without deciding a zero-sum allocation does not operate in the same manner as a rule awarding a particular benefit because of race.

Limits and questions left open: Every adverse racial exclusion and every race-determinative allocation remains outside this proposition. The Court does not establish a fixed multi-part gateway.

Legal basis: Fifth Amendment equal-protection component.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined on this proposition by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing an actual five-Justice majority.

##### Race-determinative governmental benefits

Holding: Strict scrutiny governs when race determines or effectively determines who receives a particular government contract, payment, or comparable discrete benefit.

Operative rule: Government must establish a compelling interest and narrow tailoring. The inquiry includes the identified remedial predicate, necessity, the operation and duration of the classification, workable race-neutral alternatives, and whether the racial rule reaches materially beyond the demonstrated injury.

Reasoning: A race-determinative allocation distributes a zero-sum governmental benefit to identified persons according to race. The most searching review is required to distinguish a necessary remedial classification from racial administration unsupported by an adequate constitutional predicate.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide whether the compensation clause operated in this manner or whether it could survive strict scrutiny on a developed record.

Legal basis: Fifth Amendment equal-protection component.

Authority of the proposition: Stone’s opinion is joined on this proposition by O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, producing an actual five-Justice majority.

##### Authority of the judgment and remand

The five-Justice judgment coalition agrees that the present record is inadequate for final adjudication and that the Tenth Circuit’s undifferentiated lenient review cannot stand. No single rationale under *Marks* explains the judgment as a whole. The two issue-specific propositions above are independently supported by actual majorities and control within their stated fields.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand.

Public role: Author of the opinion announcing the judgment and the two proposition-level majority holdings.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes nonexclusive access measures from classifications that determine a discrete allocation. He requires remand because the record does not establish which legal operation occurred.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor concurrence, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Every governmental racial classification, including every federal remedial classification, should receive strict scrutiny. That broader proposition has four votes and is noncontrolling.

Stevens partial dissent, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Congress’s remedial judgments should receive intermediate scrutiny unless the classification is invidious. The writing would sustain the program on the existing record. That broader proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Metro Broadcasting* is limited but not overruled. Its intermediate-scrutiny principle remains applicable to a genuinely nonexclusive federal access measure that does not determine a discrete benefit.

*Croson* is applied to a federal classification when race decides or effectively decides a particular governmental allocation. It is not extended to every federal measure that takes race into account.

*Fullilove* remains relevant to congressionally designed access remedies within the first category. *Missouri v. Jenkins* reinforces the requirement of an identified remedial predicate and a measure fitted to that predicate.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court also vacated and remanded, 5-4, but adopted strict scrutiny for all federal racial classifications and overruled *Metro Broadcasting* to the contrary. The simulation preserves the judgment division while rejecting universal strict scrutiny. Two different five-Justice coalitions instead establish intermediate scrutiny for genuinely nonexclusive access measures and strict scrutiny for race-determinative allocations. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/200/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the certification uncertainty, lower-court standard, dates, historical judgment, and historical opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/200/))

### Gutierrez de Martinez v. Lamagno, 515 U.S. 417

Argued March 22, 1995 | Decided June 14, 1995

From: The Attorney General’s delegate certified that a federal employee was acting within the scope of employment during an automobile accident in Colombia. Certification substituted the United States and, because the claim arose abroad, would terminate the action under the FTCA’s foreign-country exception. The district court and Fourth Circuit treated the certification as unreviewable. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/417/))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Souter, and Thomas.

The district court shall review the scope-of-employment certification under the applicable law, permit appropriate evidentiary procedures, and determine the proper defendant before addressing immunity and the merits.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Parts addressing statutory reviewability |
| Further opinion | Ginsburg | Stevens, Kennedy, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Additional discussion of possible federal jurisdiction after certification is rejected |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | O’Connor | Stone | Supports reversal and remand | Joins reviewability but reserves the unpresented jurisdictional question |
| Dissent | Souter | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Would treat certification as conclusive for substitution |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Judicial review of Westfall Act certification

Holding: An Attorney General certification that a federal employee acted within the scope of employment is judicially reviewable before substitution of the United States becomes final.

Operative rule: Certification initiates substitution and is conclusive for removal where the statute expressly says so. It does not conclusively determine the scope-of-employment question for final substitution. The district court may receive evidence and resolve disputed facts under ordinary procedures.

Reasoning: Certification can change the defendant, forum, immunity, and existence of any remedy. Congress did not clearly assign final, unreviewable authority over those consequences to an interested executive official. The statute expressly makes certification conclusive for removal but contains no corresponding command for substitution, and the presumption favoring review applies. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/417/))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not determine whether Lamagno acted within the scope of employment, waive federal immunity, create a cause of action against the United States, or decide the federal jurisdiction that would remain after a court rejects certification in a case lacking another jurisdictional basis.

Legal basis: 28 U.S.C. § 2679(d).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Court’s reviewability opinion and Justice O’Connor’s concurrence.

Announced ground: Stone agrees that certification is reviewable and reserves the distinct jurisdictional question that the case does not require the Court to decide.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*FDIC v. Meyer* is applied without alteration. Immunity, substitution, cause of action, jurisdiction, and merits remain separate questions.

*O’Melveny & Myers* remains applicable to the underlying state-law scope inquiry absent federal displacement.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed and remanded, 5-4. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s dissenting vote and joins O’Connor’s limited concurrence, producing a 6-3 judgment while leaving Ginsburg’s broader jurisdictional discussion without a majority. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/417/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the certification, foreign-country consequence, lower-court judgment, statutory distinction, dates, and historical opinion topology. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/417/))

### Oklahoma Tax Commission v. Chickasaw Nation, 515 U.S. 450

Argued April 24, 1995 | Decided June 14, 1995

From: Oklahoma sought to impose its motor-fuels tax on fuel sold by the Tribe at retail stores on trust land and to tax wages paid by the Tribe to members residing both inside and outside Indian country. The Tenth Circuit ruled for the Tribe and its members on both questions. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/450/))

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Motor-fuels tax imposed on the tribal retailer | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | Oklahoma may not enforce the current tax against the Tribe. |
| Income tax on tribal members residing in Indian country | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | None | The covered wages remain exempt from state income tax. |
| Income tax on tribal members residing outside Indian country | Reversed, 5-4 | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Breyer | Oklahoma may apply its generally applicable income tax, subject to ordinary law. |

The judgment is affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | All participating Justices as to the motor-fuels tax and members residing in Indian country; Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to members residing outside Indian country | Supports the mixed judgment | Complete opinion for the five-Justice off-reservation coalition |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter | Supports the tribal tax rulings but opposes taxation of members residing outside Indian country | Would extend immunity to all wages paid by the Tribe |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Legal incidence of the motor-fuels tax

Holding: Oklahoma’s motor-fuels tax, as currently written, may not be enforced against the Tribe because its legal incidence falls on the tribal retailer in Indian country and Congress has not clearly authorized the tax.

Operative rule: When the legal incidence of a state tax falls directly on a Tribe or tribal member inside Indian country, the tax is invalid absent clear congressional authorization. The court first determines legal incidence from the enacted liability, payment, reporting, and pass-through provisions.

Reasoning: Oklahoma places payment, reporting, and legal liability on the retailer and does not require the tax to be passed through to consumers. The Court declines to decide the Hayden-Cartwright Act argument first raised in the merits briefing. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/450/))

Limits and questions left open: Oklahoma may prospectively redesign the tax within federal limits. The Court does not decide a tax whose incidence is lawfully placed on a nontribal distributor or consumer.

Legal basis: Federal Indian-law tax immunity.

##### Income earned from the Tribe

Holding: Oklahoma may not tax wages paid by the Tribe to a tribal member residing in Indian country. It may tax the wages of a member residing outside Indian country absent federal law providing an exemption.

Operative rule: Reservation residence and tribal-source income place the member within the territorial protection recognized by federal Indian law. That categorical protection does not follow a member to an off-reservation residence merely because the employer is the Tribe.

Reasoning: Members residing in Indian country receive tribal wages within the sphere protected by *McClanahan*. Members residing elsewhere are ordinarily subject to the State’s generally applicable income tax and receive ordinary state governmental services. No federal enactment extends the exemption to those residences.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not govern nonmember employees, income from nontribal sources, or an express federal alteration of state taxing authority.

Legal basis: Federal Indian law and the Indian-country statutes.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the motor-fuels and Indian-country-residence components; reverse the off-reservation-residence component; remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone joins the legal-incidence rule, the territorial protection for members residing in Indian country, and the conclusion that ordinary state income taxation applies to members residing outside Indian country.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hagen v. Utah* is distinguished. Its reservation-diminishment rule helps identify Indian country but does not authorize direct state taxation of a Tribe or member within Indian country.

#### Historical Comparison

The component judgments, vote divisions, Ginsburg authorship, and associate-Justice alignments match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in the five-Justice off-reservation income-tax coalition. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/450/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the tax structure, residence distinction, preservation of the Hayden-Cartwright issue, dates, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/450/))

### Commissioner v. Schleier, 515 U.S. 323

Argued March 27, 1995 | Decided June 14, 1995

From: The Fifth Circuit affirmed a determination that backpay and liquidated damages received in settlement of an ADEA claim were excluded from gross income under 26 U.S.C. § 104(a)(2). The controlled reference to the Federal Circuit is corrected to the Fifth Circuit. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/323/))

#### Judgment

Reversed, 6-3. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Souter, and Thomas.

The tax assessment is sustained to the extent consistent with ordinary allocation and computation rules. The case returns for any necessary calculation.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Reaches the same result through a narrower textual account |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Thomas in full; Souter as to Part II | Would affirm | Would treat the settlement as received on account of personal injury within the statutory exclusion |

#### Decision of the Court

##### ADEA settlements under § 104(a)(2)

Holding: ADEA backpay and liquidated damages are not excluded from gross income under § 104(a)(2).

Operative rule: Exclusion requires both a cause of action based on tort or tort-type rights and damages received on account of personal injuries or sickness. Each component of a recovery must satisfy the statutory causal requirement.

Reasoning: Backpay replaces wages that would have been taxable had they been earned in the ordinary course. Its amount is calculated from lost compensation rather than personal injury. ADEA liquidated damages punish a willful violation and do not compensate personal injury or sickness. Neither component therefore satisfies the causal exclusion. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/323/))

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide damages expressly compensating physical injury, medical injury, or an emotional injury under a statute and record satisfying the enacted exclusion.

Legal basis: 26 U.S.C. §§ 61(a) and 104(a)(2).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the component-specific tax characterization, treatment of backpay as replacement earnings, and treatment of liquidated damages as punitive.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*McKennon v. Nashville Banner Publishing Co.* is applied without alteration. Its characterization of ADEA backpay as a wage-replacement remedy informs the tax analysis but does not itself determine exclusion under § 104(a)(2).

#### Historical Comparison

The 6-3 judgment and remaining opinion alignments match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Stevens’s majority. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/323/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the settlement components, Fifth Circuit posture, statutory test, dates, historical judgment, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/323/))

### Sandin v. Conner, 515 U.S. 472

Argued February 28, 1995 | Decided June 19, 1995

From: Conner alleged that Hawaii prison officials denied him procedural due process by refusing requested witnesses and imposing disciplinary segregation. The Ninth Circuit reversed summary judgment for the officials after concluding that a prison regulation conditioned guilt on substantial evidence and could create a liberty interest. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/472/))

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

The Ninth Circuit’s reversal of summary judgment remains in force. The district court shall determine whether Hawaii law genuinely constrained officials’ authority to impose punitive segregation, whether the deprivation was meaningful rather than routine, and what process was due. No liability finding is made.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Would replace the mandatory-language approach with an atypical-and-significant-hardship rule and hold that Conner lacked a protected liberty interest |

#### Decision of the Court

##### State-created liberty in prison discipline

Holding: A State creates a protected prison liberty interest when substantive law meaningfully limits officials’ authority to impose a significant deprivation. Mandatory wording alone is insufficient, but a court may not disregard a genuine legal constraint on punitive segregation.

Operative rule: The court examines both the substance of state law and the deprivation at issue. State law must genuinely constrain official authority rather than merely prescribe procedures or use mandatory drafting. The restraint must concern a meaningful loss of liberty rather than a routine transfer, brief inconvenience, or ordinary discretionary condition of confinement.

Reasoning: The mandatory-language methodology associated with *Hewitt* made drafting conventions dispositive and encouraged artificial negative inferences. The cure is not to make substantive state law irrelevant. A State that conditions punitive segregation on a supported misconduct finding has placed a legal limit on official power. Whether that limit and the resulting deprivation are substantial enough to create protected liberty requires an examination of the actual law and record.

Application: Hawaii’s rules may have forbidden punitive segregation without a supported misconduct finding, and material facts remain disputed concerning the legal constraint, the conditions of segregation, and the process Conner received. Summary judgment is premature.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not constitutionalize every prison regulation, routine transfer, brief inconvenience, or discretionary management choice. Institutional security and lawful correctional judgment retain their ordinary weight.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Due Process Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone narrows *Hewitt* by rejecting mandatory language as sufficient while preserving genuine substantive state-law limits on significant punitive deprivations.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor dissent, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: A state-created prison liberty interest should generally be limited to restraint imposing atypical and significant hardship compared with ordinary prison life. The dissent would find no such hardship here. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hewitt v. Helms* is narrowed. Mandatory language alone no longer creates a protected interest, but neither does the Court adopt a rule that disregards substantive legal restrictions on prison officials.

*Wolff* remains controlling once a protected liberty interest is established. *Meachum* remains applicable to ordinary transfers and conditions not constrained by substantive state law.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed the Ninth Circuit, 5-4, and adopted the atypical-and-significant-hardship formulation. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s vote, joins the historical dissenters, and authors a different five-Justice rule preserving substantive state-law constraints while rejecting mandatory wording as independently sufficient. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/472/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the disciplinary proceeding, regulation, lower-court judgment, dates, historical rule, and historical opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/472/))

### United States v. Gaudin, 515 U.S. 506

Argued April 17, 1995 | Decided June 19, 1995

From: Gaudin was convicted under 18 U.S.C. § 1001 after the trial judge instructed the jury that materiality was for the court and then ruled that the charged statements were material. The Ninth Circuit reversed on Fifth and Sixth Amendment grounds. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/506/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Hubbard v. United States* excludes Article III courts acting adjudicatively from § 1001’s references to departments and agencies. Gaudin’s statements concerned an executive agency, so *Hubbard* does not remove the offense or any of its enacted elements.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Gaudin’s conviction remains vacated. The Government may retry him under instructions submitting every element, including materiality, to the jury.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | Breyer | Supports affirmance | Emphasizes that materiality is treated as an element of the charged provision and reserves materially different statutory questions |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Jury determination of materiality

Holding: When materiality is an element of a charged false-statement offense, the Fifth and Sixth Amendments require the jury to determine materiality beyond a reasonable doubt.

Operative rule: The judge states the governing legal definition of materiality. The jury determines whether the charged statement, under the proved facts, had the natural tendency or capacity to influence the governmental decision.

Reasoning: A criminal conviction must rest on a jury determination of every element. Materiality determines guilt and requires application of a legal standard to facts, a task juries routinely perform. Historical practice and functional convenience cannot transfer an element to the judge. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/506/))

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not assign abstract statutory interpretation or other pure questions of law to juries. It does not decide whether materiality is an element under materially different statutory language.

Legal basis: Fifth and Sixth Amendments.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the requirement that the jury decide materiality as an element and announces no additional ground.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Sinclair v. United States* is overruled to the extent it reserves an offense’s materiality element to the judge.

*Hubbard* is distinguished and remains unchanged. It governs whether the institution receiving the statement falls within § 1001; *Gaudin* governs who decides materiality when the statute applies.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment and Scalia opinion match the historical comparator. The historical Chief’s concurrence, joined by O’Connor and Breyer, is carried in the simulation by an O’Connor concurrence joined by Breyer. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/506/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the HUD statements, instruction, constitutional question, dates, historical judgment, and opinion structure. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/506/))

### Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston, 515 U.S. 557

Argued April 25, 1995 | Decided June 19, 1995

From: A private veterans council organized Boston’s St. Patrick’s Day-Evacuation Day Parade under a city permit. Massachusetts courts applied the State’s public-accommodations law to require inclusion of GLIB as an identified contingent expressing pride in the members’ Irish heritage and sexual orientation. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/557/))

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The injunction compelling GLIB’s inclusion shall be dissolved. The Massachusetts public-accommodations law remains enforceable in applications that do not compel private expression.

Justice Souter delivered the Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Compelled inclusion in a private parade

Holding: Applying a public-accommodations law to compel private parade organizers to include an identified contingent conveying a message the organizers do not choose violates the First Amendment.

Operative rule: A parade is expressive even when it contains diverse messages, has no single precise theme, and admits participants through a broad selection policy. Selection of participating contingents forms part of the organizers’ expressive composition.

Reasoning: A private speaker need not formulate one narrow proposition before receiving First Amendment protection. Compelling inclusion of a group formed to communicate an identified message changes the expressive content selected by the organizers. Use of public streets under a neutral permit does not transfer editorial control to the State.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not invalidate public-accommodations laws generally, authorize exclusion from ordinary nonexpressive goods or services, or establish that a customer’s identity always constitutes a message.

Legal basis: First Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse and remand.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between equal access to ordinary commercial services and compelled alteration of a private expressive event.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Swanner v. Anchorage Equal Rights Commission* is distinguished. *Swanner* concerned a neutral law governing the terms of a commercial housing transaction and did not require a landlord to include another person’s message in the landlord’s own expression. *Hurley* concerns compelled editorial composition of an expressive event.

*Tornillo* is applied to the organizers’ selection of expressive content. *Roberts v. United States Jaycees* remains applicable where equal-access regulation does not materially alter protected expression.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment, rule, Souter authorship, and opinion structure match the historical comparator. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/557/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the parade organization, GLIB’s proposed contingent, state-court injunction, dates, historical judgment, and unanimous opinion. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/557/))

### Florida Bar v. Went For It, Inc., 515 U.S. 618

Argued January 11, 1995 | Decided June 21, 1995

From: Florida prohibited personal-injury lawyers and referral services from sending targeted direct-mail solicitations to accident victims and their relatives during the 30 days following an accident or disaster. The district court and Eleventh Circuit enjoined the prohibition. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/618/))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Ibanez*, *Lanphere & Urbaniak*, and *Rubin v. Coors Brewing Co.* require evidence of a real commercial-speech harm and a proportionate fit between that harm and the restriction.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, and Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer.

The injunction against the 30-day targeted-mail prohibition remains in force. Florida may regulate false, misleading, coercive, harassing, or improperly disclosed solicitation and may adopt proportionate privacy protections consistent with the First Amendment.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Thomas, Breyer | Would reverse | Would treat the Bar’s evidentiary record and limited 30-day period as sufficient under commercial-speech review |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Truthful targeted direct-mail solicitation

Holding: Florida may not prohibit truthful, nonmisleading targeted direct-mail solicitation for 30 days after an accident merely because recipients or the public may regard prompt solicitation as offensive or undignified, where the State has not established that the categorical ban is a proportionate response to deception, coercion, harassment, or another concrete harm.

Operative rule: For truthful commercial speech concerning lawful services, government must identify a substantial interest, establish that the restriction directly and materially advances that interest, and demonstrate a reasonable fit. Least-restrictive-means analysis is not required, but offense, poor taste, and generalized concern for professional image do not substitute for proof of actual harm and fit.

Reasoning: Direct mail is materially less coercive than in-person solicitation because a recipient can discard it unread. Florida’s surveys and complaints show that many persons dislike prompt solicitations and believe they reflect poorly on lawyers. They do not establish that truthful mail deprives recipients of choice or that a blanket period of silence materially protects privacy in a way more direct conduct rules cannot. Requirements identifying attorney advertising, restrictions on confidential-record misuse, opt-out protections, and prohibitions on deception, coercion, harassment, or repeated unwanted contact illustrate the gap between the identified harms and the categorical ban. They are evidence of poor fit, not a mandatory regulatory checklist.

Limits and questions left open: Supported restrictions on in-person solicitation, deception, coercion, repeated unwanted contact, confidential-record misuse, and proportionate disclosures remain lawful. A materially different evidentiary record may present a different application.

Legal basis: First Amendment and *Central Hudson*.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the evidence-and-fit analysis, the distinction between direct mail and coercive in-person solicitation, and the conclusion that professional image or public offense does not justify the categorical 30-day silence period on this record.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor dissent, joined by Scalia, Thomas, and Breyer: The Bar’s study, public complaints, and the short duration of the restriction sufficiently establish an invasion of accident victims’ privacy and a reasonable fit. The dissent would permit the State to protect recipients during the immediate aftermath of an accident without waiting for proof of individualized coercion. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Ibanez* is applied beyond professional credentials to targeted legal-service advertising. The regulator must support the harm and the proportionality of its response.

*Lanphere & Urbaniak* and *Rubin* are applied without alteration. Categorical suppression of truthful commercial communication requires more than conjecture, offense, or a regulatory design materially inconsistent with the asserted interest.

*Shapero v. Kentucky Bar Association* is reaffirmed. *Ohralik v. Ohio State Bar Association* is distinguished because in-person solicitation creates coercive pressures not inherent in direct mail.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed the Eleventh Circuit, 5-4, and sustained the 30-day ban. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s vote and joins Kennedy, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg, producing a 5-4 affirmance. The historical majority’s rationale becomes O’Connor’s four-Justice dissent. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/618/))

Research sources: The Supreme Court case record supplies the challenged rules, evidentiary setting, lower-court judgment, dates, historical disposition, and opinion lineup. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/515/618/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Aguilar, 515 U.S. 593

Argued March 20, 1995 | Decided June 21, 1995

From: The Ninth Circuit, sitting en banc, reversed Aguilar’s convictions for endeavoring to obstruct a pending grand-jury proceeding under 18 U.S.C. § 1503 and for disclosing a wiretap under § 2232(c) after its formal authorization had expired. The questions are whether false statements to investigating agents possess the required connection to the grand jury and whether § 2232(c) reaches the post-expiration disclosure. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Hubbard v. United States* preserves obstruction statutes independently covering interference with judicial proceedings, while *United States v. Gaudin* requires every enacted criminal element to be determined by the jury. Neither decision defines the obstruction nexus or construes § 2232(c).

#### Judgment

| Judgment component | Disposition | Supporting Justices | Opposing Justices | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Section 1503 obstruction conviction | Affirmed, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | The Ninth Circuit’s reversal of the conviction remains in force. |
| Section 2232(c) wiretap-disclosure conviction | Reversed and remanded, 8-1 | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stevens | The conviction may be reinstated, subject to remaining preserved claims and ordinary sentencing proceedings. |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Stevens as to the § 1503 holding; Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to the § 2232(c) construction except the official-confidentiality discussion | Supports the mixed judgment | Different issue coalitions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports affirmance on § 1503 and would also affirm on § 2232(c) | Rejects application of § 2232(c) after expiration of the authorization |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reinstatement of the § 2232(c) conviction and would reinstate the § 1503 conviction | Rejects the Court’s obstruction-nexus requirement |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Nexus between an obstructive act and a pending proceeding

Holding: False statements to investigators do not violate § 1503 absent proof that the defendant knew the statements were likely to reach and obstruct a pending judicial or grand-jury proceeding.

Operative rule: The alleged endeavor must have a relationship in time, causation, or logic to the proceeding. The defendant must know that the conduct is likely to affect that proceeding, and the conduct must possess the natural and probable effect of interference. An intent to mislead an investigation that remains independent of the court or grand jury is insufficient.

Reasoning: Without a nexus, the omnibus clause would become a general false-statement prohibition untethered to administration of justice. *Pettibone* requires knowledge connecting the conduct to the proceeding. Here the FBI agents were not shown to be acting as an arm of the grand jury, had not been directed or subpoenaed to testify, and were not known by Aguilar to be transmitting his answers to it.

Limits and questions left open: Direct lies to a grand jury, destruction of subpoenaed material, witness tampering, or statements knowingly routed to a pending proceeding remain within § 1503 when the other elements are proved.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 1503.

##### Disclosure after expiration of a wiretap authorization

Holding: Section 2232(c) reaches a disclosure made after the identified authorization expires when the defendant knew of the application or authorization, gave notice of the possible interception, and acted with the specific purpose of obstructing the interception.

Operative rule: The statute does not require the application or authorization to remain formally pending at the moment of disclosure. “Such interception” identifies the interception associated with the application or authorization known to the defendant. “Possible interception” accounts for uncertainty about whether interception will occur.

Reasoning: Adding an in-force requirement would narrow text Congress did not enact. The statutory knowledge and specific-purpose elements also confine liability to obstruction of a protected interception rather than ordinary discussion. As applied to an official entrusted with confidential information, the First Amendment does not require an artificial narrowing of the enacted prohibition.

Limits and questions left open: The Government must prove both knowledge and the specific obstructive purpose. Disclosure unrelated to an application, authorization, or possible interception is not covered.

Legal basis: 18 U.S.C. § 2232(c).

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm the reversal of the § 1503 conviction; reverse the § 2232(c) component and remand.

Public role: Author of the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone requires a proceeding-specific obstruction nexus under § 1503 and applies § 2232(c) according to its three enacted elements without adding a requirement that the authorization remain in force.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia’s mixed opinion, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: Intentional false statements made to persons who may convey them to the grand jury constitute an endeavor to obstruct even without the Court’s natural-and-probable-effect requirement. The writing agrees with the statutory § 2232(c) disposition but does not join the official-confidentiality discussion. The obstruction proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Hubbard* is distinguished. Removing adjudicating courts from § 1001 does not immunize conduct covered by a separate obstruction statute, but § 1503 now requires the proceeding-specific nexus stated here.

*Gaudin* is applied without alteration. The nexus and intent required by § 1503 are elements that must be submitted to the jury and supported by sufficient evidence.

The decision supplies the first controlling simulated construction of § 2232(c).

#### Historical Comparison

The component judgments and issue coalitions match the historical comparator. Stone occupies the historical Chief Justice line and authors the simulated opinion; the remaining partial joins and separate writings are preserved. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the record, statutory questions, judgment, dates, and historical opinion structure. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))
