### Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, 515 U.S. 646

Argued March 28, 1995 | Decided June 26, 1995

From: After a bench trial, the district court sustained Vernonia’s random urinalysis program for interscholastic athletes. The Ninth Circuit reversed under the Federal and Oregon Constitutions. The federal question is whether the suspicionless program is reasonable under the Fourth Amendment. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, and Souter.

The Ninth Circuit’s federal constitutional ruling is set aside. The court may address any independently preserved state constitutional ground without treating the Fourth Amendment as requiring invalidation.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Emphasizes the program’s limitation to students who voluntarily undertake athletic participation |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Souter | Would affirm | Would require a record showing that suspicion-based testing is materially ineffective before approving recurring suspicionless bodily searches |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Random urinalysis of interscholastic athletes

Holding: Vernonia’s random drug-testing policy for interscholastic student athletes is reasonable under the Fourth Amendment.

Operative rule: A school search conducted for a special need beyond ordinary law enforcement may be reasonable without a warrant, probable cause, or individualized suspicion. Reasonableness depends on the students’ legitimate privacy interests, the character of the intrusion, the nature and immediacy of the governmental concern, and the efficacy of the program in addressing that concern. The Fourth Amendment does not require the least intrusive available means.

Reasoning: Public schools exercise custodial and tutelary responsibility over students. Athletes accept preseason examinations, communal changing, coaching supervision, and sport-specific safety rules, reducing their privacy expectations in this setting. The collection procedures approximate conditions in public restrooms, the chemical analysis is confined to specified drugs, and disclosure is limited. The district court found an immediate drug problem in which athletes were prominent and drug use materially increased the risk of sports injuries. Random testing directly addresses both the broader disciplinary problem and the athlete-specific safety concern.

Limits and questions left open: The decision concerns a non-law-enforcement program limited to interscholastic athletes and restricted drug information. It does not approve disclosure to police, testing for unrelated medical conditions, or suspicionless testing of every student regardless of context.

Legal basis: Fourth Amendment.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins Justice O’Connor’s dissent.

Announced ground: Stone concludes that participation in athletics does not waive bodily and medical privacy and that the record does not show individualized reasonable-suspicion testing or materially less intrusive school measures to be impracticable.

#### Material Separate Writings

O’Connor dissent, joined by Stone, Stevens, and Souter: Recurring suspicionless bodily searches should not be approved when a workable suspicion-based regime could address the demonstrated misconduct and safety risks. The dissent regards Vernonia’s program as broader than the record justifies. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

This decision supplies the first controlling simulated rule governing suspicionless drug testing of public-school athletes. *T.L.O.*, *Skinner*, and *Von Raab* are applied without converting school enrollment or athletic participation into a general waiver of Fourth Amendment protection.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court vacated and remanded, 6-3. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s majority vote and joins O’Connor’s dissent, producing a 5-4 judgment while leaving the other alignments intact. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the policy, trial findings, judgment below, historical holding, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

---

### Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia, 515 U.S. 819

Argued March 1, 1995 | Decided June 29, 1995

From: The University used mandatory student fees to pay outside contractors for services supplied to qualifying student organizations, but declined to pay the printer of *Wide Awake* because the publication primarily promoted religious belief. The Fourth Circuit regarded the exclusion as viewpoint discrimination but upheld it as justified by the Establishment Clause. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Kiryas Joel* and *Church of Scientology v. Clearwater* require neutral civic treatment of religious speakers while distinguishing ordinary civil access from governmental supervision or financing of religious activity.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas.

UVA need not reimburse the disputed printing expense. The University remains bound to administer recognition, meeting facilities, distribution channels, security, and other genuinely neutral civic services without disqualifying an otherwise eligible organization because it is religious.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Would reverse | Treats the Student Activities Fund as a neutral student-speech program from which the University may not exclude a religious viewpoint |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Equal civic access and direct financing of devotional activity

Holding: The Free Speech Clause protects religious speakers against exclusion from genuinely opened civic and expressive channels, but it does not require a public university to use mandatory student-fee funds to pay directly for the production of devotional instruction, evangelism, or religious formation.

Operative rule: A court must distinguish access to a forum or neutral civic service from transfer of public resources for the designated religious use. Government may not disguise ordinary access as religious funding to justify exclusion. It may, however, decline direct payment for religious exercise while leaving the organization eligible for recognition, facilities, distribution access, and genuinely severable secular benefits.

Reasoning: Wide Awake Productions remains free to organize, speak, distribute its publication, use ordinary university facilities, and participate in student life. The disputed benefit is payment from a publicly administered mandatory-fee fund to an outside printer for production of the devotional publication itself. Private editorial control and a disclaimer establish that the words are the students’ speech, but they do not convert the public money into private money. Refusal of that payment leaves the speakers’ ordinary civic access intact.

Limits and questions left open: Religious organizations are not categorically ineligible for public programs. They may receive neutral civic services and may compete for genuinely secular benefits on equal terms. The holding concerns the designated religious use of the funds rather than religious identity alone.

Legal basis: Free Speech and Establishment Clauses.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the distinction between equal participation in civic and expressive channels and a claimed constitutional entitlement to public financing of devotional religious activity.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy dissent, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas: The Student Activities Fund operates as a limited forum or neutral subsidy program for independent student speech. Because the University pays third-party vendors under religion-neutral criteria and disclaims responsibility for the publications, excluding only the religious viewpoint violates the Free Speech Clause and is not required by the Establishment Clause. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Church of Scientology v. Clearwater* is applied and clarified. Private donations to a church are not governmental financing, but mandatory fees allocated and paid by a public university remain public resources. Church-state separation does not authorize civil disability or exclusion from ordinary civic access, yet the First Amendment does not compel direct financing of religious exercise.

*Kiryas Joel* remains unchanged. Neutral treatment of religious citizens does not require special allocation of governmental funds or authority to religion.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed, 5-4. Stone replaces the historical Chief’s vote and joins Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer, producing a 5-4 affirmance. Souter’s position becomes the Opinion of the Court, while Kennedy’s historical majority rationale becomes a four-Justice dissent. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the fund’s structure, the publication, the Fourth Circuit judgment, historical result, and opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

### Babbitt v. Sweet Home Chapter of Communities for a Great Oregon, 515 U.S. 687

Argued April 17, 1995 | Decided June 29, 1995

From: The Secretary defined “harm” in the Endangered Species Act’s prohibition on taking protected wildlife to include significant habitat modification or degradation that actually kills or injures wildlife. The D.C. Circuit invalidated that construction in the respondents’ facial challenge. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *MCI* requires agencies to remain within the delegated statutory boundary. *PUD No. 1* recognizes that environmental regulation may address consequences connected to the statute’s operative requirements.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Opposing: Scalia and Thomas.

The challenged regulation remains valid. Any enforcement proceeding must prove actual death or injury and the required causal connection on the particular record.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | Stone | Supports reversal | Emphasizes injury to identifiable animals and ordinary proximate-causation limits |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Independently construes the ESA and treats agency expertise as persuasive rather than controlling |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Confines “take” to conduct directed against individual animals and rejects the habitat-modification regulation |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Habitat modification that actually kills or injures wildlife

Holding: The ESA permits the Secretary to define “harm” as significant habitat modification or degradation that actually kills or injures protected wildlife, including injury caused by material impairment of essential behavioral patterns.

Operative rule: Habitat alteration alone is insufficient. The Government must establish actual death or injury to protected wildlife and the ordinary causal connection between the modification and that injury. Speculative, conjectural, or merely potential ecological effects do not constitute a completed take.

Reasoning: “Harm” performs work beyond the neighboring verbs describing direct pursuit, capture, wounding, or killing. The Act’s incidental-take permit provision presupposes that otherwise lawful activities may cause prohibited injury indirectly, while the distinction between habitat-acquisition authority and § 9 enforcement confirms that § 9 operates only after actual injury or death. The Secretary’s narrowing definition remains within the meaning the statute can bear.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not reach habitat modification causing no actual injury, eliminate ordinary causation, or authorize regulation outside the ESA’s protected-species and permit structure.

Legal basis: Endangered Species Act, 16 U.S.C. §§ 1532(19), 1538(a)(1)(B), and 1539.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Reverse.

Public role: Concurs in the judgment and joins Justice O’Connor’s concurrence.

Announced ground: Stone independently reads the statutory term “harm” and the permit structure to include significant habitat modification that actually kills or injures protected animals. He gives the Secretary’s ecological expertise persuasive weight but does not treat deference as the source of statutory authority.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia dissent, joined by Thomas: The words surrounding “harm” concern conduct directed against animals, and the regulation unlawfully converts land use into a taking without requiring the actor to direct conduct toward wildlife. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*MCI* is distinguished. The Secretary has not eliminated or replaced an enacted requirement, but has defined an operative statutory term within the ESA’s text and structure.

The Court’s six-Justice opinion applies ordinary deference within that delegated domain. Stone’s independent-construction position remains noncontrolling. The actual-injury and causation limits are controlling features of the statutory rule.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical Court reversed, 6-3. Stone departs from the historical Chief’s dissenting vote, producing a 7-2 judgment, but concurs on independent statutory grounds rather than joining the Court’s deference rationale. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the regulation, statutory structure, facial posture, judgment, and historical opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

### United States v. Hays / Louisiana v. Hays, 515 U.S. 737

Argued April 19, 1995 | Decided June 29, 1995

From: A three-judge district court invalidated Louisiana’s congressional plan at the request of voters residing in District 5 whose challenge focused principally on the majority-minority District 4. Louisiana and the United States appealed directly. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded with instructions to dismiss, 9-0. Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

The district court shall dismiss the present appellees’ claim for lack of standing without reaching the equal-protection merits. A properly injured plaintiff remains free to bring a preserved challenge.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports vacatur and dismissal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | Souter | Supports the judgment | Addresses the character of the asserted districting injury |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Reaches standing through a narrower account of cognizable injury |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports the judgment | Announces no broader ground |

#### Threshold Decision

Threshold holding: Voters who reside outside the district alleged to embody an unconstitutional racial classification lack standing absent evidence that the legislature personally classified or otherwise concretely injured them.

Reasoning and application: A generalized objection to allegedly unlawful statewide line drawing is insufficient. The appellees reside in District 5 and identified no evidence that race controlled their placement there or that the asserted classification embodied in District 4 personally injured them. Assignment of every voter to some congressional district does not give every voter standing to challenge every district.

Effect on judgment below: The judgment invalidating Louisiana Act 1 is vacated, and the present action must be dismissed.

Questions not reached: The Court does not decide whether District 4 is unconstitutional, whether residents of that district would have standing, or what equal-protection standard would govern a properly presented claim.

Legal basis: Article III.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Vacate and remand with instructions to dismiss.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the requirement of personal, district-specific injury and forms no position on the equal-protection merits.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Holder v. Hall* is clarified by contrast. Its objective-comparator requirement is a merits prerequisite under Voting Rights Act § 2 and does not itself supply Article III injury.

This decision creates the first controlling simulated standing rule for a racial-gerrymandering claim.

#### Historical Comparison

The unanimous judgment and principal opinion lineup match the historical comparator, with Stone occupying the historical Chief’s place in Justice O’Connor’s opinion. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the districts of residence, direct-appeal posture, standing analysis, judgment, and historical separate writings. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

### Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson, 515 U.S. 900

Argued April 19, 1995 | Decided June 29, 1995

From: A three-judge district court held Georgia’s Eleventh Congressional District unconstitutional after finding that race had overridden compactness, political subdivisions, communities of interest, and ordinary geography. The plan followed Justice Department refusals to preclear earlier plans containing two rather than three majority-black districts. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Hays*, released earlier the same day, concerns standing only. *Adarand* distinguishes structural, access-expanding racial remedies from racial rules determining discrete governmental allocations. *Holder* and *De Grandy* require proof of an actual voting-rights injury and reject maximization.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 5-4. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer.

Georgia shall adopt a lawful congressional plan. Race may be considered to comply with a demonstrated constitutional or statutory obligation, but the remedial process may not pursue racial maximization, proportional allocation, or a configuration unsupported by the identified injury.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality opinion | Kennedy | O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports affirmance and remand | Would apply strict scrutiny whenever race predominates |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports affirmance and remand | Emphasizes that bizarre shape is evidence rather than a threshold element |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | No other Justice | Supplies the fifth judgment vote on a narrower remedial ground | Applies strict scrutiny here because race predominated without a demonstrated remedial necessity and because the plan pursued maximization |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Would reverse | Would sustain broader remedial consideration of race in the districting process |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Proof that race predominated

Holding: A racial-gerrymandering plaintiff need not establish a threshold level of geometric bizarreness. Race predominates when it displaces the traditional, race-neutral districting principles that otherwise would explain the district.

Operative rule: Racial awareness is insufficient. Courts examine direct and circumstantial evidence, including legislative purpose, racial targets, district shape, treatment of political subdivisions, compactness, contiguity, communities of interest, and ordinary political geography. Shape is relevant evidence but not an indispensable element.

Reasoning and application: The Eleventh District joined distant black population centers through narrow corridors, divided numerous counties and municipalities, and could not be explained by ordinary political or geographic considerations. The record showed that racial targets and federal pressure for a third majority-minority district overrode the State’s traditional criteria.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Equal Protection Clause.

Authority of the proposition: Kennedy’s plurality and Stone’s concurrence support the same predominance proposition at the same level of generality, producing five votes.

##### Scrutiny of an unsupported or maximizing race-predominant district

Holding: Strict scrutiny governs a race-predominant district when the State has not demonstrated that the racial configuration was necessary to remedy an identified constitutional or statutory violation, or when the district pursues racial maximization beyond correction of the identified injury. Georgia’s Eleventh District fails that review.

Controlling rule under *Marks*: Kennedy’s plurality would apply strict scrutiny to every race-predominant district. Stone applies strict scrutiny to the logically narrower class of race-predominant districts lacking a demonstrated remedial necessity or pursuing maximization. That subset controls because it produces the same result whenever its conditions are present.

Reasoning and application: Georgia established no § 2 violation, constitutional violation, or other federal obligation requiring this configuration. The Justice Department’s demand for an additional majority-minority district and its maximization policy cannot themselves supply the compelling interest or tailoring necessary to justify the racial sorting. The State’s plan therefore fails strict scrutiny.

Limits and questions left open: The Court does not decide what scrutiny governs a materially different race-predominant district genuinely necessary to remedy demonstrated vote dilution or satisfy another valid voting-rights obligation. Stone’s possible intermediate-scrutiny treatment of that category is reserved and is not controlling law.

Legal basis: Fourteenth Amendment Equal Protection Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm and remand.

Public role: Concurs in the judgment.

Announced ground: Stone distinguishes racial awareness from racial predominance and applies strict scrutiny because Georgia lacked an identified remedial predicate and pursued an additional majority-minority district through racial maximization. He reserves the standard governing a genuinely necessary and bounded structural voting-rights remedy.

#### Material Separate Writings

Kennedy plurality, joined by O’Connor, Scalia, and Thomas: Strict scrutiny should govern whenever race becomes the predominant districting principle, regardless of the governmental objective. That broader proposition has four votes and is noncontrolling.

Ginsburg dissent, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Breyer: Redistricting necessarily occurs with knowledge of race and politics, and the record should be understood as an effort to comply with federal voting-rights obligations rather than an unconstitutional assignment of citizens by race. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Shaw v. Reno* is clarified. Geometric bizarreness is not a threshold element, and racial predominance may be established through the complete districting record.

*Adarand* is applied to the unsupported, race-determinative configuration before the Court. Its distinct treatment of a genuinely structural and access-expanding remedy remains unresolved in the redistricting setting.

*Holder* and *De Grandy* are applied to reject racial maximization absent proof of the statutory injury and remedial necessity.

#### Historical Comparison

The 5-4 judgment division matches the historical comparator. Historically, Kennedy’s categorical strict-scrutiny position commanded five votes. In the simulation it commands four, while Stone’s narrower concurrence supplies the fifth judgment vote and leaves the treatment of a proven structural voting-rights remedy open. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the plan’s construction, Justice Department involvement, district-court findings, judgment, and historical opinion lineup. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

### Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette, 515 U.S. 753

Argued April 26, 1995 | Decided June 29, 1995

From: Ohio maintained Capitol Square as a traditional public forum governed by speech-neutral access criteria. The State denied a permit for a privately sponsored unattended cross solely because it believed the display would violate the Establishment Clause. The lower courts required issuance of the permit. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Rosenberger*, released earlier the same day, distinguishes private access to an opened expressive channel from governmental payment for devotional activity. *Pinette* concerns private forum access and attribution, not public financing.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

The permit and injunction remain in force. Ohio may apply neutral identification or disclaimer requirements governing comparable private displays.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court in part and plurality opinion in part | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer as to Parts I, II, and III; Kennedy and Thomas as to Part IV | Supports affirmance | Seven-Justice case-specific Court opinion; three-Justice categorical plurality |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | States a broader original-meaning account |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | O’Connor | Stone, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Applies contextual attribution and the reasonable-observer inquiry |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Souter | Stone, O’Connor, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Relies on available identification and disclaimer measures |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would reverse | Would permit exclusion because the unattended symbol near the seat of government creates an unacceptable attribution risk |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Would reverse | Regards the offered disclaimer and setting as insufficient |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Private religious expression in an open public forum

Holding: Ohio does not establish religion by neutrally permitting this privately owned and sponsored religious display in Capitol Square, and it may not exclude the display because its viewpoint is religious.

Operative rule: When government has genuinely opened a forum on equal terms, private religious expression remains private where the forum’s history, neutral access rules, ownership and sponsorship of the display, absence of public financing or editorial control, and available identification measures make private attribution clear. A neutral identification requirement may be used, but religious expression may not alone be required to bear a special disclaimer.

Reasoning: The cross entered the square through the same permit process available to other private speakers. Ohio did not select, finance, design, or control the message. A reasonable observer acquainted with the square’s longstanding use by diverse private groups and able to read the offered identification would not attribute the expression to the State. The Establishment Clause therefore supplies no compelling justification for the viewpoint exclusion.

Limits and questions left open: The decision does not approve a government-sponsored religious display, selective religious favoritism, captive-audience worship, public financing of the religious message, or circumstances in which the forum’s operation reasonably attributes the expression to the State.

Legal basis: Free Speech and Establishment Clauses.

Authority of the proposition: Parts I, II, and III of Scalia’s opinion are joined by seven Justices and establish the case-specific holding. No majority adopts Part IV’s categorical rule that private religious speech in a public forum can never be attributed to government.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins Parts I, II, and III of Justice Scalia’s opinion and joins the separate opinions of Justices O’Connor and Souter.

Announced ground: Stone relies on neutral forum access and contextual attribution. Private ownership, sponsorship, broad forum history, and identification measures make the cross private expression rather than governmental religious speech.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia plurality, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: Purely private religious speech in a traditional or designated public forum categorically cannot violate the Establishment Clause. The proposition has three votes and is noncontrolling.

O’Connor and Souter concurrences, each joined by Stone, Breyer, and the other author: The contextual endorsement inquiry remains applicable, but the informed observer would recognize the display as private, particularly with adequate identification. These writings support the judgment but do not create a separate majority rule beyond the seven-Justice Court opinion.

Stevens and Ginsburg dissents: An unattended religious symbol beside the statehouse may be attributed to the State despite the forum policy and offered disclaimer. The propositions are noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Rosenberger* is distinguished. That decision concerns direct payment from a public fund for devotional activity; *Pinette* concerns private expression admitted to an existing forum without public financing.

*Kiryas Joel* is applied without alteration. Neutral forum access neither specially allocates governmental authority to religion nor establishes the private speaker’s faith.

#### Historical Comparison

The historical judgment was also 7-2. Stone occupies the historical Chief’s place in Parts I, II, and III but does not join Scalia’s Part IV. The categorical plurality therefore falls from four votes to three, while both contextual concurrences gain Stone as a fourth joiner. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

Research sources: The official U.S. Reports supplies the forum, permit denial, judgment, historical fragmentation, and competing attribution approaches. ([Supreme Court](https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/515bv.pdf))

### Chabad-Lubavitch of Georgia v. Miller, 5 F.3d 1383

Argued April 24, 1995 | Decided June 29, 1995

#### Entering Posture and Questions

Georgia opened the State Capitol Rotunda to private secular and religious expression under a content-neutral, first-come, first-served policy and permitted unattended displays. Chabad sought to place a privately owned fifteen-foot menorah accompanied by identifying material in the Rotunda for Chanukah. Georgia excluded it because the display was religious and asserted that admission would violate the Establishment Clause. The en banc Eleventh Circuit entered judgment for Chabad. ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/chabad-lubavitch-of-georgia-885296228))

Controlling simulated law before decision: *Rosenberger* protects equal access to genuine civic and expressive channels while separating that access from public financing. *Pinette*, released immediately before this case, governs private religious displays admitted through neutral forum rules.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2. Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Breyer. Opposing: Stevens and Ginsburg.

Georgia shall admit Chabad’s display on the same neutral terms governing comparable private expression. The State may apply neutral rules concerning size, duration, scheduling, safety, identification, and unattended displays, or may prospectively close or redefine the forum through viewpoint-neutral rules.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Contextual forum and attribution analysis |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Supports affirmance | Would apply a broader categorical rule for private religious speech in a genuinely open forum |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would reverse | Regards the display’s scale, duration, unattended character, and interior Capitol location as creating an impermissible attribution risk |

#### Decision of the Court

##### Exclusion of a religious viewpoint from the Rotunda forum

Holding: A State that opens a government-building forum to private displays on neutral terms violates the Free Speech Clause when it excludes an otherwise eligible display solely because the private speaker’s viewpoint is religious.

Operative rule: Government may enforce neutral subject-matter, physical, scheduling, safety, identification, and time, place, and manner rules. It may also close or redefine a designated forum prospectively on viewpoint-neutral terms. It may not maintain the forum for private secular expression while excluding a religious perspective on an otherwise eligible subject.

Reasoning: The menorah is privately owned, financed, and sponsored. Georgia’s policy admits diverse private displays to the Rotunda and does not select or pay for their messages. Equal access removes a religious disability; it does not confer preferential placement or governmental assistance.

Limits and questions left open: Georgia need not maintain the Rotunda forum, disregard neutral operational limits, or grant Chabad preferential duration or location.

Legal basis: Free Speech Clause.

##### Establishment Clause attribution inside the Capitol

Holding: Neutral access for this clearly identified private religious display does not violate the Establishment Clause merely because the forum is located inside the State Capitol.

Operative rule: Attribution depends on the forum’s established character, its neutral access rules, private ownership and sponsorship, governmental control or financing, and contextual measures that make private authorship clear. Location in a government building is relevant but not dispositive.

Reasoning: The Rotunda had been used for numerous private political, cultural, educational, and religious programs and unattended exhibits. Chabad’s accompanying material identifies the private sponsor, and no public funds produce the menorah or its religious message. An informed observer would understand that Georgia permits the display rather than adopts its doctrine.

Limits and questions left open: A display selected, financed, designed, controlled, or presented by the State as its own may be attributed to government. The Court also reserves a pretextual forum, obscured sponsorship, or circumstances in which the State affirmatively adopts the message.

Legal basis: Establishment Clause.

#### Justice Stone’s Announced Position

Vote: Affirm.

Public role: Joins the Opinion of the Court.

Announced ground: Stone joins the conclusion that neutral access to the Rotunda is constitutionally distinct from governmental financing or sponsorship and that the identifying context preserves the menorah’s private character.

#### Material Separate Writings

Scalia concurrence in the judgment, joined by Thomas: Once the State genuinely opens the forum on equal terms, admission of private religious expression cannot constitute governmental establishment. The broader categorical proposition is unnecessary to the Court’s contextual holding.

Stevens dissent, joined by Ginsburg: A large unattended religious symbol displayed for eight days inside a core government building may reasonably appear to carry state approval despite the forum’s history and identifying material. The proposition is noncontrolling.

#### Effect on Existing Simulated Law

*Rosenberger* is applied. Religious speakers receive equal access to genuine civic channels, while government remains free from any duty to finance the religious message.

*Pinette* is applied and clarified for a designated forum inside a government building. Interior location increases the relevance of attribution safeguards but does not override neutral access, private sponsorship, and clear identification.

#### Historical Comparison

No historical Supreme Court merits decision exists. The en banc Eleventh Circuit reversed the district court and held that admitting Chabad’s privately sponsored menorah under Georgia’s equal-access policy would not communicate state endorsement. The simulation affirms that judgment, 7-2, under the same-day rules established in *Rosenberger* and *Pinette*. ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/chabad-lubavitch-of-georgia-885296228))

Research sources: The published en banc Eleventh Circuit opinion supplies the Rotunda’s forum history, the dimensions and sponsorship of the menorah, Georgia’s reason for exclusion, and the judgment below. ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/chabad-lubavitch-of-georgia-885296228))
